Smooth ℚ \mathbb{Q} -homology planes satisfying the Negativity Conjecture Thanks: This research project was funded by the National Science Centre, Poland, grant No. 2015/18/E/ST1/00562.
Tomasz Pełka

Abstract
. A complex algebraic surface S S is a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -homology plane if H i ​ ( S , ℚ ) = 0 H_{i}(S,\mathbb{Q})=0 for i > 0 i>0 . The Negativity Conjecture of Palka asserts that κ ⁡ ( K X + 1 2 ​ D ) = − ∞ \kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty , where ( X , D ) (X,D) is a log smooth completion of S S . We give a complete description of smooth ℚ \mathbb{Q} -homology planes satisfying the Negativity Conjecture. We restrict our attention to those of log general type, as otherwise their geometry is well understood. We show that, as conjectured by tom Dieck and Petrie, they can be arranged in finitely many discrete series, each obtained in a uniform way from an arrangement of lines and conics on ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} . We infer that these surfaces satisfy the Rigidity Conjecture of Flenner and Zaidenberg; and a conjecture of Koras, which asserts that # ​ Aut ⁡ ( S ) ⩽ 6 \#\operatorname{Aut}(S)\leqslant 6 .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 14R05; Secondary: 14J26, 14J50

1.
Introduction

We work with complex algebraic varieties. A smooth surface
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane
(
\mathbb{Q}
HP
, for short) if
H_{i}(S;\mathbb{Q})=0
for
i>0
. All
\mathbb{Q}
HPs are affine
[
Fuj82
, 2.5]
and rational
[
GP99
]
. Being in many ways similar to the affine plane, they gained attention as a testing ground for many problems concerning
\mathbb{C}^{2}

[
Miy07
, 3.3, 5.2]
; as well as a source of exotic structures on
\mathbb{C}^{n}

[
Zai98
]
. The classification of
\mathbb{Q}
-, or even
\mathbb{Z}
-homology planes is still a challenging problem.

We note that in the literature,
\mathbb{Q}
HPs are usually allowed to be singular
[
GM88
,
Pal11b
]
. Since we approach the classification problem only in the smooth case, for convenience we include smoothness in the definition.

Structure theorems for affine surfaces have led to a complete description of
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes
S
whose logarithmic Kodaira-Iitaka dimension
\kappa(S)
is not maximal, i.e.
\kappa(S)<2
; see Proposition
2.2
for a summary. Hence we restrict our attention to those with
\kappa(S)=2
, i.e.
of log general type
.

Here, no structure theorem is known. Nonetheless, many authors have constructed various series of examples, see Section
5
. These examples suggest certain rigidity, which can be expressed by means of the following, open conjectures. For definitions of
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
and a core graph see Sections
10
and
2A
, respectively.

Conjecture 1.1
.

Let
S
be a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type, and let
(X,D)
be a minimal log smooth completion of
S
. Then

(a)

(Tom Dieck–Petrie Conjecture
[
tD92
, 1.1]
) There is a birational morphism
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
\tau_{*}D
is a sum of lines and conics.

(b)

(Flenner–Zaidenberg Rigidity Conjecture
[
Zai95
, 1.3]
, cf.
[
FZ94
]
)
H^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
for all
i\geqslant 0
.

(c)

(Finiteness Conjecture, cf.
[
Zai95
, 1.6]
) The number of possible core graphs for
D
is finite.

(d)

(Koras Conjecture) The automorphism group of
S
has at most six elements.

In fact, even a weaker rigidity conjecture, asserting that
\chi(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
, is open, see
[
Zai95
, 1.4]
. By
[
Pal19
, 4.3(i)]
, the number
\chi(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
is equal to
h^{0}(2K_{X}+D)
, see Lemma
10.1
(d)
(for yet another interpretation of
\chi(\mathcal{T}(-\log D))
see
[
tD92
, 4.7]
). Hence from the birational geometry viewpoint, a natural generalization of
[
Zai95
, 1.4]
is the following conjecture, which holds for all known examples.

Conjecture 1.2

(Palka Negativity Conjecture
[
Pal19
, 4.7]
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane. Then
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty
.

We note that
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)
depends only on the isomorphism class of the surface
X\setminus D
, see
[
Pal21
, 2.10]
.

The main result of this article is the classification Theorem
1.6
, where for every
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane satisfying the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, we explicitly construct the morphism
\tau
from tom Dieck–Petrie Conjecture
1.1
(a)
. As a consequence, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

The Negativity Conjecture
1.2
implies all parts of Conjecture
1.1
.

Moreover, in Corollaries
1.7
and
1.8
we provide quantitative answers to Conjectures
1.1
(c)
and
(d)
.

We remark that the original Finiteness Conjecture, formulated by Zaidenberg in
[
Zai95
, 1.6]
, asked if the number of Eisenbud–Neumann diagrams of
D
is finite. A positive answer to this question follows from the work of Tono
[
Ton05
, 4.4]
, cf.
[
Ore17
]
, who bounded the number of twigs of
D
by
17
(see Section
2A
for definitions). Its stronger version, Conjecture
1.1
(c)
, was proved by Palka
[
Pal19
, 1.3]
in case when
X\setminus D
is a complement of a rational cuspidal curve, i.e.
X\setminus D\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\overline{E}
for some curve
\overline{E}
which is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(in the Euclidean topology). In this case, Negativity Conjecture
1.2
implies the Rigidity Conjecture
1.1
(b)

[
PP19
, 1.6(d)]
.

The main tool used to prove all the above results is the theory of almost minimal models
[
Pal19
,
Pal21
]
, applied to the pair
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
. This method is also a key ingredient of the proof of the Coolidge–Nagata Conjecture
[
KP17
]
and other structure theorems for rational cuspidal curves
[
KP22
]
.

Theorem
1.6
is stated in terms of of an algorithm proposed by tom Dieck and Petrie
[
tDP89
,
tD92
]
. Before we formulate it in Definition
1.5
, we explain the key observation that motivates it. Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP. Assume there is a
(-1)
-curve
A\not\subseteq D
meeting
D
normally in two points, exactly one of which lies on a
(-2)
-tip
T
of
D
, see Figure
1
. Let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a contraction of
A
, and let
\overline{A}=\sigma_{*}T
,
\overline{D}=\sigma_{*}D-\overline{A}
. Then
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP (with a minor exception if
d(\overline{D})=0
, see Lemma
3.3
), and
\overline{A}
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
\overline{D}
normally, twice. If
\overline{A}
meets a
(-2)
-tip of
\overline{D}
then we repeat this process, otherwise we contract
\overline{A}
and stop. Performing this construction for other
A
’s as above yields a morphism
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, called
expansion
in Definition
1.4
.

Conversely, given a
(-1)
-curve
A\subseteq X
meeting
D
twice, let
\widetilde{\sigma}\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow X
be a blowup at one of the points of
A\cap D
, and let
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}_{*}(D+A)
,
\widetilde{A}=\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\sigma}
,
\widetilde{T}=\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}_{*}A
. Again, up to a minor exception,
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
is a log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP, and
\widetilde{A}\not\subseteq\widetilde{D}
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
\widetilde{D}
in two points, exactly one of which lies on a
(-2)
-tip
\widetilde{T}
of
\widetilde{D}
. Therefore, we can iterate this process to get a
tower
of
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes, each obtained by an expansion from a fixed
basic
pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.

\dots
\widetilde{A}
-1
\widetilde{T}
-2
\widetilde{\sigma}
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
A
-1
T
-2
\sigma
(X,D)
\overline{A}
-1
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})

Figure 1
.
Expansion produces a tower of
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes, see Definition
1.4
.

The main point of using almost MMP is that it naturally produces curves
A
as above. Indeed, let
\psi\colon(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)\longrightarrow(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
be an MMP run. It follows from
[
Pal21
, 5.2]
that every curve
A\not\subseteq D
contracted by
\psi
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
D
normally, twice, so it is of the above type. This way, we get an expansion
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, where
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a minimal log resolution of
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
, see Lemma
3.12
.

Assume
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty
, so
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
is a log Mori fiber space over some base
B
. If
\dim B=1
then
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered, cf.
[
Pal19
, 4.5(4)]
. This case was settled in
[
MS91
]
, see Section
7
. If
\dim B=0
then
[
Pal21
, 5.5]
implies that
X_{\min}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, see Lemma
3.13
. Such surfaces are classified in
[
Fur86
]
. To infer Conjecture
1.1
(a)
, we will transform
D_{\min}\subseteq X_{\min}
into a planar configuration.

In order to state Theorem
1.6
, we will now describe the above procedure in more precise terms. We adapt terminology from
[
tDP93
]
. Some examples constructed this way appeared earlier e.g. in
[
MS91
]
, where what we call
expansion
is called an
oscillating sequence of blowing-ups
.

Definition 1.4

(Expansion)
.

Let
D^{\prime}
be an snc divisor on a smooth projective surface
X^{\prime}
. Fix an integer
n\geqslant 0
. For
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, fix a node
x_{i}\in\operatorname{Sing}D^{\prime}
and coprime positive integers
u_{i},w_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
U_{i},W_{i}
be the components of
D^{\prime}
meeting at
x_{i}
. Put
\mathcal{C}=((U_{1},W_{1};x_{1}),\dots,(U_{n},W_{n};x_{n}))
and
\boldsymbol{v}=(\frac{u_{1}}{w_{1}},\dots,\frac{u_{n}}{w_{n}})\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}^{n}
.

An
expansion over
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
with centers
\mathcal{C}
and weights
\boldsymbol{v}
is a morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
with the following properties. First,
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{i}
, where each
T_{i}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(x_{i})
is a chain with a unique
(-1)
-curve
A_{i}
. Second, the multiplicity of
A_{i}
in
\varphi^{*}U_{i}
,
\varphi^{*}W_{i}
equals
u_{i}
and
w_{i}
, respectively. Third,
D=(\varphi^{*}D^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
.

A
tower over
\mathcal{C}
is the set of pairs
(X,D)
obtained by an expansion with centers
\mathcal{C}
and varying weights.

Definition 1.5

(tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, see Example
4.4
)
.

Let
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a reduced configuration of lines and conics, and let
P
be a subset of
\operatorname{Sing}{p}
. Denote by
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
the minimal log resolution and by
E^{\prime}
be the sum of
(-1)
-curves in
\pi^{-1}(P)
. Put
D^{\prime}=(\pi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}-E^{\prime}
. On
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, fix
\mathcal{C},\boldsymbol{v}
as in Definition
1.4
, and let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the expansion with centers
\mathcal{C}
and weights
\boldsymbol{v}
.

In this case, we say that the pair
(X,D)
is
obtained via tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm
from
({p},P,\mathcal{C},\boldsymbol{v})
.

With these preparations at hand, we can now state our main classification result.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
S
be a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type and let
(X,D)
be a minimal log smooth completion of
S
. Assume that
S
satisfies the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, that is,

\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty.

Then
(X,D)
is obtained via tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm from data listed in one of the rows of Tables
–
.

Conversely, any pair
(X,D)
obtained this way is a minimal log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type satisfying the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
.

We denote by
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
the tower obtained from the
*
-th row of Tables
–
. For each pair
(X,D)
in
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
, the complement
X\setminus D
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP, unless the corresponding weight
\boldsymbol{v}
is excluded in the second-to-last column. By a slight abuse of notation, we will refer by
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
to the set of those
\mathbb{Q}
HPs, too.

Configurations
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
from Definition
1.5
are constructed in Section
8
. There is
39
of them, and they satisfy
\deg{p}\leqslant 11
. Theorem
1.6
asserts that each
\mathbb{Q}
HP belongs to a tower over one of these
39
configurations.
The corresponding graphs of
D^{\prime}
are shown in Figures
5
–
22
. To obtain
D
, one needs to remove from
D^{\prime}
the points of
\mathcal{C}
, and replace each of them by
T_{i}-A_{i}
as in Definition
1.4
, i.e. by at most two twigs of
D
. Given Theorem
1.6
, this proves parts
(a)
and
(b)
of the following corollary. We write
\operatorname{core}(D)
for
D
minus the sum of its twigs.

Corollary 1.7

(Finiteness)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a minimal log smooth completion of a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type, satisfying the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
. Then

(a)

D
has at most
10
twigs,
\#\operatorname{core}(D)\leqslant 7
, and
C\cdot(D-C)\leqslant 5
for every component
C
of
D
.

(b)

The core graph (hence the Eisenbud-Neumann diagram) of
D
has at most
17
vertices.

(c)

Up to an isomorphism, there are exactly
{N}\leqslant 4
smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes
\widetilde{S}
of log general type; satisfying Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, such that, denoting by
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
a minimal log smooth completion of
\widetilde{S}
, the weighted graphs of
\widetilde{D}
and
D
are the same. The number
{N}
is listed in Tables
–
.

To prove Corollary
1.7
(c)
, we note that each weighted graph of
D
from Theorem
1.6
is uniquely determined by the initial data from Tables
–
. Moreover, since each blowup within the construction of
(X,D)
is centered at the common point of specific components of the boundary, these combinatorial data determine the isomorphism class of
(X,D)
, up to certain symmetries of that pair. Thus to infer
(c)
, we need to understand those symmetries. This is done in Section
9
, where we prove the following corollary, too.

Corollary 1.8

(Automorphisms)
.

Let
S
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type. Assume that
S
satisfies the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is isomorphic to a subgroup of the symmetric group
S_{3}
. In case
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\cong S_{3}
, the surface
S
is isomorphic to the complement of the planar tricuspidal quartic given by

(xy)^{2}+(yz)^{2}+(zx)^{2}=2xyz(x+y+z).

In general, if the group
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is nontrivial then
S
is isomorphic to one of the surfaces in Proposition
9.11
.

It turns out that all but one
\mathbb{Q}
HPs from Proposition
9.11
have already appeared in the literature, see
[
tD90
]
and Section
5
. The exception is the surface
, which is a complement of a certain new four-cuspidal curve on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
, and admits an involution constructed in Example
9.6
. In general, no absolute bound on
\#\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is known; even in case of
\mathbb{Z}
-homology planes, for which
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is cyclic by
[
GK+14
, Theorem 2.2]
.

The following simple consequence of Theorem
1.6
is proved in Section
8
.

Corollary 1.9
.

Every
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type satisfying Conjecture
1.2
is defined over a number field of degree at most
4
over
\mathbb{Q}
. This field is specified in column
“
{k}
”
of Tables
–
.

We conclude with an observation pointed out by Marco Golla. Part
(b)
shows that Corollary
1.7
(c)
gives a lot of interesting examples of non-isomorphic, but diffeomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes of log general type.

Corollary 1.10

(see Proposition
9.13
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an
{N}
-tuple from Corollary
1.7
(c)
. That is,
\mathcal{S}
is a set of isomorphism classes of all
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes of log general type, satisfying Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, whose minimal log smooth completions
(X,D)
share a given weighted graph of
D
. Then the following holds.

(a)

All surfaces in
\mathcal{S}
are defined over a number field
{k}
, and the Galois group of
{k}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{S}
. In particular, the fundamental groups of all surfaces in
\mathcal{S}
have the same profinite completions.

(b)

If
\mathcal{S}
is contained in towers

–
,
–
,

,

,

,

,

,

,

or

then
\mathcal{S}
contains a pair of non-isomorphic surfaces which are complex-conjugate, hence diffeomorphic.

Remark 1.11
.

The assumption
\kappa(S)=2
is crucial for all the above corollaries. Indeed, almost all
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes of non-general type admit a
\mathbb{C}^{1}
- or a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibration, see Proposition
2.2
. With such a structure at hand, one can easily deform its degenerate fibers to construct arbitrarily high-dimensional families of
\mathbb{Q}
HPs with the same weighted boundary graph; see
[
FZ94
, 4.16, 6.9]
,
[
Pal11b
, 5.1]
or
[
Ton01
]
.

The article is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall some basic definitions and notation. Next, in Section
3
we list the properties of expansions
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
used in Theorem
1.6
. In particular, in Lemma
3.3
we show how to check if the resulting pair
(X,D)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane; and in Lemma
3.12
we relate it with an almost MMP run described in
[
Pal21
]
.
Before we delve into the proof of Theorem
1.6
, in Section
4
we take a break to illustrate our strategy on particular examples; and in Section
5
we explain how to recover some well-known
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes from our list.

To prove Theorem
1.6
, we study a minimal model
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
of
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
. The assumption
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty
implies that
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
is a log Mori fiber space: either over a point, or over a curve. The first case is treated in Section
6
: this is the main part of the proof. In the second case, the assumption
\kappa=2
implies that
S
is
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered, see Lemma
3.13
(a)
. Such
\mathbb{Q}
HPs are described in
[
MS91
]
, see Table
. Nonetheless, in Section
7
we give a self-contained proof of Theorem
1.6
in the
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered case. This is motivated by the fact that, as observed in
[
Sug99
]
, the proof in
[
MS91
]
can be much simplified using a later result of
[
GM92
]
that
\mathbb{Q}
HPs of log general type contain no curves isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{1}
.

Planar divisors used in Theorem
1.6
are constructed in Section
8
.
In Section
9
we prove Corollaries
1.7
(c)
and
1.8
by describing the automorphisms of our surfaces and their boundary graphs. In Section
10
we prove the remaining part of Theorem
1.3
, that is, we show that surfaces in Theorem
1.6
satisfy the Rigidity Conjecture
1.1
(b)
. To do this, we follow the argument in
[
FZ00
, Proposition 2.2]
showing rigidity for complements of certain rational cuspidal curves. Eventually, in Section
11
we check that
our
\mathbb{Q}
HPs are indeed of log general type.

2.
Preliminaries

2A.
Divisors on surfaces

In order to settle the notation for the remaining part of the article, we now briefly recall some standard definitions from the theory of log surfaces. For details see e.g.
[
Fuj82
]
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface. We denote by
\operatorname{NS}(X)
the Néron-Severi group of divisors on
X
modulo numerical equivalence; and write
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)=\operatorname{NS}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
. By a
curve
we mean irreducible and reduced variety of dimension
1
. A curve
C\subseteq X
such that
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
C^{2}=n
is called an
n
-curve
. By adjunction formula, an
n
-curve
C
satisfies
K_{X}\cdot C=-2-n
, where
K_{X}
is the canonical divisor on
X
.

Let
D
be a reduced divisor on
X
. By a
component
of
D
we mean an
irreducible
component. We denote their number by
\#D
. A
subdivisor
of
D
is an effective divisor
T
such that
D-T
is effective. Its
branching number
is
\beta_{D}(T)=T\cdot(D-T)
. A component
C
of
D
is a
tip
of
D
if
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 1
, and is
branching
in
D
if
\beta_{D}(C)\geqslant 3
.

A point of
p\in D
is a
simple normal crossing
(
snc
) point if
p
lies on exactly two different components of
D
, which meet transversally at
p
. We say that
D
is an
snc divisor
if all its singular points are snc. Note that two components of an snc divisor can meet in more than one point.

Let
D
be a connected snc divisor. If
\beta_{D}(T)\leqslant 2
for every component
T
of
D
, then
D
is called a
chain
in case at least one of these inequalities is strict and
circular
otherwise. If
D
has no circular subdivisor then
D
is a
tree
.
We say that
D
is
rational
if all its components are.

Let
T
be a chain. Its components
T_{1},\dots,T_{r}
can be ordered in such a way that
T_{i}\cdot T_{i+1}=1
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}
.
If
T
is rational, we say that it is of
type

[-T_{1}^{2},\dots,-T_{r}^{2}]
, and often abuse notation by writing
T=[-T_{1}^{2},\dots,-T_{r}^{2}]
. We write
(m)_{k}
for the sequence consisting of an integer
m
repeated
k
times.

Let again
D
be a reduced divisor, and let
D_{1},\dots,D_{s}
be its components. We say that
D
is
negative definite
if its intersection matrix
[D_{i}\cdot D_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant s}
is. The
discriminant
of
D
is
d(D)\coloneqq\det[-D_{i}\cdot D_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant s}
. By convention,
d(0)\coloneqq 1
. For elementary properties of discriminants we refer to
[
Fuj82
, Section 3]
.

A
twig
of
D
is a nonzero subchain
T\subseteq D
containing a tip of
D
, such that
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 2
for every component
C
of
T
. If a twig
T\subseteq D
is not a connected component of
D
, we order it so that its first component is a tip of
D
, so the last component of
T
meets
D-T
. A
(-2)
-twig
is a twig whose all components are
(-2)
-curves. We say that a twig (or a
(-2)
-twig) of
D
is
maximal
if it is not properly contained in any other twig (or a
(-2)
-twig) of
D
. Note that a maximal
(-2)
-twig is not necessarily a maximal twig.

Let
B
be the sum of branching components of
D
, and let
T
be the sum of twigs of
D
. The
core graph
(respectively, the
Eisenbud-Neumann diagram
) of
D
is an unweighted graph, whose vertices are components of
D-T
(resp.
B
) and connected components of
T
(resp.
D-B
); and two vertices are connected by an edge whenever the corresponding subdivisors of
D
are distinct and meet each other. In other words, the core graph is obtained from the graph of
D
by forgetting the weights and contracting each twig to a single vertex; and EN-diagram is obtained by further contracting all chains of non-branching components.

Note that the core graph does not encode self-intersection numbers of the components of
D
, or lengths of the twigs. In fact, for surfaces in Theorem
1.6
those numbers can be arbitrarily big. This is why Conjecture
1.1
(c)
is formulated in terms of core graphs.

Let
T
be a rational twig of
D
whose components have self-intersection at most
-2
. The
bark
of
T
in
D
, denoted by
\operatorname{Bk}_{D}T
is the unique effective
\mathbb{Q}
-subdivisor of
T
such that
T^{\prime}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}_{D}T=T^{\prime}\cdot(K_{X}+D)=\beta_{D}(T^{\prime})-2
for every component
T^{\prime}
of
T
, see
[
Fuj82
, 6.12]
. It exists because
T
is negative definite. We write
\operatorname{Bk}T=\operatorname{Bk}_{D}T
if
D
is clear from the context. If
T_{1},\dots T_{r}
are disjoint twigs of
D
, we put
\operatorname{Bk}T=\sum_{i}\operatorname{Bk}T_{i}
. If
T=T^{(1)}+\dots+T^{(r)}
is an ordered
(-2)
-twig such that
T^{(r)}
meets
D-T
, then a direct computation, see
[
Miy01
, II.3.3.3]
, gives

(1)

\operatorname{Bk}T=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{r-i+1}{r+1}T^{(i)}.

A
(-1)
-curve
L\subseteq D
is called
superfluous
if it contracts to a smooth or snc point of the image of
D
. Equivalently,
L
is superfluous if
\beta_{D}(L)=1
or
\beta_{D}(L)=2
and
L
meets two different components of
D-L
. An snc divisor is
snc-minimal
if it has no superfluous
(-1)
-curves.

Let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be a birational morphism between smooth projective surfaces. The
rank
of
\sigma
, denoted by
\rho(\sigma)
, is the difference of Picard ranks
\rho(X)-\rho(X^{\prime})
. A
center
of
\sigma
is a base point of
\sigma^{-1}
, i.e. a point
p\in X^{\prime}
such that
\sigma
is not an isomorphism in any neighborhood of the preimage of
p
. In this case,
\sigma^{-1}(p)
is a rational tree with at least one
(-1)
-curve. We denote by
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma^{-1}\subseteq X^{\prime}
the (finite) set of centers of
\sigma
.

A morphism of pairs
\sigma\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a morphism
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
such that
\sigma_{*}D=D^{\prime}
.

Let
S
be a smooth surface. A surjective morphism
f\colon S\longrightarrow B
onto a smooth curve is called a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
- (respectively,
\mathbb{C}^{(n*)}
-)
fibration
if there is a nonempty Zariski-open subset
U\subseteq B
such that for every
b\in U
the fiber
f^{*}(b)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(respectively,
\mathbb{C}^{1}\setminus\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
for some pairwise distinct
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
, depending on
b
). A fiber without this property is called
degenerate
. Any fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration can be contracted to a
0
-curve by iterated contractions of
(-1)
-curves. Therefore, such fibers are easy to understand, see
[
Fuj82
, Section 4]
.

A curve
C
on
S
is called
vertical
if its image
f(C)
is a point; otherwise
C
is called
horizontal
. For every divisor
T
on
S
, we have a unique decomposition
T=T_{\mathrm{vert}}+T_{\mathrm{hor}}
, where all components of
T_{\mathrm{vert}}
and
T_{\mathrm{hor}}
are vertical and horizontal, respectively. We call
T_{\mathrm{vert}}
and
T_{\mathrm{hor}}
the
vertical part
and the
horizontal part
of
T
.

2B.
Log smooth completions

A
log smooth surface
is a pair
(X,D)
consisting of a smooth projective surface
X
and an snc-divisor
D
on
X
. Any smooth surface
S
admits a
log smooth completion
, that is, a log smooth pair
(X,D)
such that
X\setminus D\cong S
. A log smooth completion is
minimal
if
D
is snc-minimal.

The following lemma is a well known consequence e.g. of
[
FKZ07
, Corollary 3.36]
. Part
(a)
allows us to speak of
the
log smooth completion of a surface of log general type.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
S
be a smooth surface and let
(X,D)
be some minimal log smooth completion of
S
. Assume that one of the following conditions holds.

(a)

The surface
S
is of log general type.

(b)

Every non-branching rational component of
D
has negative self-intersection number.

Then
(X,D)
is the unique minimal log smooth completion of
S
. In particular, every automorphism of
S
extends to an automorphism of
(X,D)
.

First, we show that condition
(a)
implies
(b)
. Suppose that
D
has a non-branching, rational component
C
with
C^{2}\geqslant 0
. Choose a point
p\in C\cap(D-C)
, or any
p\in C
in case
C\cap(D-C)=\emptyset
. Blow up over
p
until the proper transform of
C
, say
\widehat{C}
, becomes a
0
-curve. Denote the resulting surface by
\widehat{X}
, and let
\widehat{D}
be the reduced total transform of
D
. Then
\beta_{\widehat{D}}(\widehat{C})\leqslant 2
, so the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|\widehat{C}|
restricts to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-,
\mathbb{C}^{1}
- or
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibration of
\widehat{X}\setminus\widehat{D}=S
. The Iitaka easy addition theorem gives
\kappa(S)<2
, which proves the claim.

Therefore, we can assume condition
(b)
. Suppose
S
admits another minimal log smooth completion, say
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
. Let
(\widehat{X},\widehat{D})
be a log smooth completion of
S
which dominates both, i.e. there are birational morphisms
\varphi\colon(\widehat{X},\widehat{D})\longrightarrow(X,D)
and
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon(\widehat{X},\widehat{D})\longrightarrow(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
. We choose
(\widehat{X},\widehat{D})
in such a way that the Picard rank
\rho(\widehat{X})
is minimal possible. Then the first exceptional curve of
\widetilde{\varphi}
, say
L
, is a superfluous
(-1)
-curve in
\widehat{D}
and is not contracted by
\varphi
. Since
\varphi(L)
cannot be a superfluous
(-1)
-curve in
D
, the morphism
\varphi
is not an isomorphism near
L
. Hence
\varphi(L)^{2}\geqslant L^{2}+1=0
. Since
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi\subseteq\widehat{D}
and
D=\varphi_{*}\widehat{D}
is snc, we have
\beta_{D}(\varphi(L))\leqslant\beta_{\widehat{D}}(L)\leqslant 2
. Therefore,
\varphi(L)
is a non-branching, rational component of
D
with
\varphi(L)^{2}\geqslant 0
; a contradiction.

∎

2C.
Structure theorems for
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes

Structure theorems for smooth
\mathbb{Q}
HPs of non general type (
\kappa<2
) can be summarized as follows.

Proposition 2.2
.

Any smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane
S
is affine
[
Fuj82
, 2.5]
and rational
[
GP99
]
. Moreover,

(a)

[
Miy01
, III.1.3.2]
If
\kappa(S)=-\infty
then
S
has a
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-fibration.

(b)

[
Miy01
, III.1.7.1]
Assume that
\kappa(S)\in\{0,1\}
. Then either
S
has a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibration, or
S
has a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration and is one of the Fujita surfaces
Y\{3,3,3\}
,
Y\{2,4,4\}
or
Y\{2,3,6\}

[
Fuj82
, 8.64]
(cf.
[
Miy01
, III.4.4.2, III.4.4.3]
).

Similar results for singular
\mathbb{Q}
HPs hold, too, see
[
Pal13
]
and
[
Pal11b
]
for a survey.

The main tool to study surfaces of log general type (
\kappa=2
) is the logarithmic Bogomolov-Miyaoka-Yau inequality
[
KNS89
,
Lan03
]
, see
[
Pal11a
, Corollary 2.5]
. One of its consequences is the following result, which will be of key importance for us. The main idea of its proof appeared in
[
MT92
]
(cf.
[
Miy01
, II.3.11]
), where it was shown that a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
HP contains no curve isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{1}
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
GM92
]
)
.

Let
S
be a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type. Then
S
contains no topologically contractible curves.

2D.
Del Pezzo surfaces of rank one with
\mathrm{A}_{k}
-singularities

In Lemma
3.12
(d)
, we will see that the underlying surface
X_{\min}
of the minimal model of
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of Picard rank one. Such surfaces were classified in
[
Fur86
]
, cf.
[
AN89
,
Ye02
]
. We will use the following part of this classification.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
Fur86
, Theorem 2]
1
1

1

There are some misprints in Table II loc. cit. In case
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
there should be
(P_{1}^{3},P_{2}^{4})
and
3P_{1}\equiv 0,3P_{2}\equiv 0
in the third and fourth column, respectively: this can be seen from the required singularity types. The cubic
\widetilde{D}_{1}
in cases
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
and
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
should be nodal: indeed, the equations in the fourth column have a solution if
\widetilde{D}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
, not
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Moreover, instead of
\mathrm{D}_{4}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
and
\mathrm{E}_{7}+\mathrm{A}_{2}
there should be
\mathrm{D}_{4}+3\mathrm{A}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{6}+\mathrm{A}_{2}
, respectively, cf.
[
Ye02
]
.
)
.

Let
\overline{Y}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one. Assume that each singular point of
\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{k}
for some
k\geqslant 1
.
Let
\alpha\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the minimal resolution. Then there is a birational morphism
\vartheta\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
(\operatorname{Exc}\alpha+\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta)_{\mathrm{red}}
has one of the graphs in Figure
2
, where:

•

\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is the union of solid lines (both fine and bold): they are
(-2)
-curves,

•

\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta
is the union of fine lines (both solid and dashed): the dashed ones represent
(-1)
-curves.

(a)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}

(b)

3\mathrm{A}_{2}

(c)

2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}

(d)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}

(e)

2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}

(f)

\mathrm{A}_{4}

(g)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5}

(h)

\mathrm{A}_{7}

(i)

\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}

(j)

\mathrm{A}_{8}

(k)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}

(l)

2\mathrm{A}_{4}

(m)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}

(n)

4\mathrm{A}_{2}

.

\mathrm{A}_{k}
singularities, see Lemma

2E.
Double points on curves

In Lemma
3.12
(g)
, we will see that the singularities of
D_{\min}
lie in
X_{\min}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and have multiplicity two. It will be convenient not to resolve them. Instead, we will use their
\delta
-invariants, cf.
[
Wal04
, p. 151]
.

Let
p
be a smooth point of a surface and let
\chi
be a curve germ of multiplicity
2
at
p
. Let
r_{p}
be the number of branches of
\chi
at
p
: we call
(p\in\chi)
a
cusp
if
r_{p}=1
and
node
if
r_{p}=2
. In some local coordinates
(x,y)
at
p
,
\chi
is given by
\{x^{2}=y^{2\delta_{p}-r_{p}+2}\}
for some
\delta_{p}\geqslant 1
, where
\delta_{p}
is the
\delta
-invariant
of
p\in\chi
. If
\delta_{p}=1
then
p\in\chi
is called
ordinary
(it can be a cusp or a node). Note that
\delta_{p}-1
is the minimal number of blowups at
p
after which the the proper transform of
\chi
is nc. For the minimal log resolution, one needs two additional blowups if
p\in\chi
is a cusp and one if
p\in\chi
is a non-ordinary node, cf.
[
Har77
, Figure 20]
.

If
C
is a curve whose all singular points have multiplicity two, we put
\delta_{C}=\sum_{p\in\operatorname{Sing}C}\delta_{p}
. For planar curves, we have the following observations, Lemma
2.5
(b)
being a consequence of the adjunction formula.

Lemma 2.5

(Planar curves with double points)
.

Let
{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a planar curve of degree
d
. Let
p\in{c}
be a point of multiplicity two and let
{l}_{p}
be a line tangent to
{c}
at
p
. Then

(a)

2<({l}_{p}\cdot{c})_{p}\leqslant d
and either
({l}_{p}\cdot{c})_{p}=2k
for some
k\leqslant\delta_{p}
or
({l}_{p}\cdot{c})_{p}\geqslant 2\delta_{p}+1
.

(b)

If
{c}
is rational and all singularities of
{c}
have multiplicity two then
\delta_{{c}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
{c}_{1},{c}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be two conics and let
{l}
be their common tangent line.

(a)

If
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{p}=({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{q}=2
for some
p,q\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
p\neq q
, then
p\in{l}
or
q\in{l}
.

(b)

If
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{p}=4
for some
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
then
p\in{l}
.

Suppose the contrary, so
{l}\cap{c}_{1}\neq{l}\cap{c}_{2}
. Let
{l}^{\prime}
be the line joining
p
and
q
in case
(a)
and the line tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
p
in case
(b)
. Then
2{l}^{\prime}
is a member of the pencil of conics generated by
{c}_{1}
and
{c}_{2}
. This pencil induces a map
{l}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
2
, ramified at
{l}\cap{c}_{1}
,
{l}\cap{c}_{2}
and
{l}\cap{l}^{\prime}
. This is impossible by the Hurwitz formula; a contradiction.

∎

2F.
Hirzebruch surfaces

In order to settle the notation, we list some basic properties of Hirzebruch surfaces, cf.
[
Har77
, V.2]
.

For
m\geqslant 0

the
m
-th Hirzebruch surface
is
\mathbb{F}_{m}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
. Assume
m>0
. Let
F
be a fiber of the unique
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\pi_{\mathbb{F}_{m}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then
\mathbb{F}_{m}
contains a unique curve of negative self-intersection number, which we call the
negative section
and denote by
\Xi_{m}
. It satisfies
\Xi_{m}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\Xi_{m}\cdot F=1
and
\Xi_{m}^{2}=-m
. In case
m=0
, we have
\mathbb{F}_{m}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and we let
F
,
\Xi_{0}
be a fiber of each projection.

For any
m\geqslant 0
, the group
\operatorname{NS}(\mathbb{F}_{m})
is a free abelian group generated by the classes of
\Xi_{m}
and a fiber
F
. A divisor
G
on
\mathbb{F}_{m}
is said to be
of type

(a,b)
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
if
G\cdot\Xi_{m}=a
and
G\cdot F=b
. In this case,
G\equiv(a+bm)F+b\Xi_{m}
, and
G
is effective if and only if
G=\Xi_{m}
or
a,b\geqslant 0
. The canonical divisor
K_{\mathbb{F}_{m}}
is of type
(m-2,-2)
. It follows from the adjunction formula that any divisor of type
(0,1)
is an
m
-curve. We call it a
positive section
of
\mathbb{F}_{m}
.

We have the following consequence of the adjunction formula, similar to Lemma
2.5
(b)
.

Lemma 2.7

(Curves with double points on
\mathbb{F}_{m}
)
.

If
C\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{m}
is a rational curve of type
(a,b)
and all singularities of
C
have multiplicity
2
then
\delta_{C}=(b-1)(a+\tfrac{m}{2}b-1)
.

We now introduce some additional notation for
m=2
. It will be useful in the case when
X_{\min}
is the quadric cone
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
, i.e. the image of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
after contracting
\Xi_{2}
.

Fix
p\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\setminus\Xi_{2}
, and let
F_{p}
be the fiber containing
p
. Define a birational map
\sigma_{p}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
as a blowup at
p
followed by the contraction of the proper transform of
F_{p}+\Xi_{2}
. Write
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{p}^{-1}=\{\widehat{p}\}
, so
\widehat{p}=\sigma(\Xi_{2})
. The image of the exceptional curve is a line, we denote it by
\widehat{}{l}_{p}
. For every line
{l}
not containing
\widehat{p}
, its preimage
(\sigma_{p}^{-1})_{*}{l}
is a positive section passing through
p
. Similarly, for every positive section
H
not containing
p
, its image
(\sigma_{p})_{*}H
is a conic tangent to
\widehat{{l}}_{p}
at
\widehat{p}
.

Using the map
\sigma_{p}
, it is easy to see that for any two points
p,q\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\setminus\Xi_{2}
there is a pencil of positive sections passing through
p
,
q
. Moreover, if
(p,\chi)
is a cusp (i.e. an irreducible analytic curve germ) of multiplicity sequence
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots)
such that
(\chi\cdot F_{p})_{p}=\mu_{1}
, then there is a unique positive section
L_{\chi}
such that
(\chi\cdot L_{\chi})_{p}\geqslant\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}
; namely the proper transform of the line tangent to
(\sigma_{p})_{*}\chi
. Such
L_{\chi}
will be called a
tangent section
to
\chi
.

3.
Expansions and almost minimal models

In this section, we list some useful properties of expansions. In particular, in Lemma
3.3
we show how to check when the pair
(X,D)
constructed by an expansion is a log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP. In Lemma
3.12
, we relate expansions with an almost minimalization of
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
in the sense of
[
Pal21
]
.

Recall from Definition
1.4
that an
expansion
with centers
(x_{i};U_{i},W_{i})
and weights
v_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
,
i=1,\dots,n
is a birational morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
with
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi^{-1}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, such that
\varphi^{-1}(x_{i})
is a chain determined by
v_{i}
. We will usually keep the choice of
x_{i}\in U_{i}\cap W_{i}
implicit; i.e. we will call
\varphi
an expansion
with centers
(U_{i},W_{i})
and weights
v_{i}
, or simply
at

(U_{i},W_{i};v_{i})
. Note, however, that the intersection
U_{i}\cap W_{i}
can contain more than one center of expansion. In this case, the above list will include
(U_{i},W_{i})
more than once.

We begin with a simple remark, which follows directly from Definition
1.4
.

Remark 3.1
.

Let
\varphi
be an expansion with one center
(x;U,W)
and weight
v=\frac{u}{w}
for some coprime
u,w\in\mathbb{N}
.

(a)

\operatorname{Exc}\varphi=[W^{\prime},1,U^{\prime}]
. Its first tip meets
\varphi^{-1}_{*}U
, last tip meets
\varphi^{-1}_{*}W
, and
d(U^{\prime})=u
,
d(W^{\prime})=w
.

(b)

If
v=1
then
\varphi
is a single blowup. If
v\in\mathbb{N}
then
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi=[1,(2)_{v-1}]
and its first tip meets
\varphi^{-1}_{*}U
.

(c)

The pair
((x;U,W),v)
determines the morphism
\varphi
uniquely. On the other hand, pairs
((x;U,W),v)
and
((x;W,U),v^{-1})
define the same expansion.

(d)

Let
\chi
be a smooth germ meeting the exceptional
(-1)
-curve of
\varphi
at a general point, transversally. Then
x\in\varphi_{*}\chi
is a cusp described in coordinates
U
,
W
by a single HN-pair
\binom{u}{w}
cf.
[
PP17
, §2D]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\varphi_{i}\colon(X_{i},D_{i})\longrightarrow(X_{i+1},D_{i+1})
be an expansion,
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. If
D_{1}
is connected, then
\varphi_{n}\circ\dots\circ\varphi_{1}\colon(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{n+1},D_{n+1})
is an expansion, too.

Since
D_{1}
is connected, so are its images
D_{i}
. Hence by induction, we can assume
n=2
and that
\varphi_{1}
has only one center, say
p
. Put
E_{i}=\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
p\not\in E_{2}
then
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}=E_{1}+(\varphi_{1}^{-1})_{*}E_{2}
, so
\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
is an expansion. Suppose
p\in E_{2}
. Since
p
is a center of
\varphi_{1}
,
p\in\operatorname{Sing}D_{2}
. Since
E
is contained in the sum of twigs of
D_{2}
, we have
\{p\}=T\cap(D_{2}-T)
for some twig
T
of
D_{2}
. Then
(\varphi_{1}^{-1})_{*}T
and
(\varphi_{1}^{-1})_{*}(D_{2}-T)
lie in different connected components of
D_{1}
, a contradiction.

∎

Recall that expansions with fixed centers and varying weights produce a
tower
of pairs
(X,D)
, hence of surfaces
S\coloneqq X\setminus D
.
The following Lemma
3.3
, which is well known, gives a practical way to decide if a surface
S
in the tower is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane, and shows that in this case
“most”
of them are. For an explicit application of this criterion see Example
4.4
.

For the readers’ convenience, we give a full proof of Lemma
3.3
, mostly following
[
tDP93
, Theorems B, 3.13]
. Similar results appear e.g. in
[
MS91
]
,
[
tDP89
]
,
[
GK+14
, 1.10]
or
[
Pal13
, 3.1(vii)]
, see also
[
Mum61
]
.

To formulate this result, we introduce the following notation. For a divisor
W
on a surface
Y
, we denote by
\mathbb{Z}[W]
the free abelian group generated by the components of
W_{\mathrm{red}}
. It admits a natural map
r_{W}\colon\mathbb{Z}[W]\longrightarrow\operatorname{NS}(Y)
, and, for any subdivisor
V
of
W
, a projection
\mathrm{pr}_{V}\colon\mathbb{Z}[W]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[V]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a log smooth pair. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be an expansion with
n
centers, and let
A=(\varphi^{*}D^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}-D\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. Define a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map

(2)

m=\mathrm{pr}_{A}\circ\varphi^{*}\colon\ker r_{D^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[A].

The surface
S\coloneqq X\setminus D
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane if and only if the following conditions hold:

(a)

X^{\prime}
is rational and the components of
D^{\prime}
generate
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X^{\prime})
,

(b)

D
is a rational tree, or equivalently: all components of
D^{\prime}
are rational, and the dual graph of
D^{\prime}
becomes a tree after removing the edges corresponding to the centers of
\varphi
.

(c)

m\otimes_{Z}\mathbb{Q}
is an isomorphism, or equivalently:

n=\#D^{\prime}-\rho(X^{\prime})
and

\det m\neq 0
,
where we view
m
as an element of
\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
.

Furthermore, if
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane then
H_{i}(S;\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i\geqslant 2
, and

(3)

\#H_{1}(S;\,\mathbb{Z})=|d(D)|^{1/2}=|\!\det m|\cdot\#\operatorname{coker}r_{D^{\prime}}.

Put
S^{\prime}=X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
,
E=\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. By the universal coefficient theorem,
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP if and only if
\widetilde{H}^{*}(S;\mathbb{Q})=0
, in which case
H_{1}(S)\cong H^{2}(S)
; where we skip the coefficient ring if it is
\mathbb{Z}
.

Smooth
\mathbb{Q}
HPs are rational by
[
GP99
]
, so we can assume that
X^{\prime}
and
X
are rational. Hence
H_{1}(X)=H_{3}(X)=0
, and by the exponential sequence, we get an isomorphism
\operatorname{NS}(X)=H_{2}(X)
, mapping a curve to its fundamental class. By Mayer-Vietoris,
H_{2}(D)=\mathbb{Z}[D]
.
By Lefschetz duality
H_{i}(X,D)=H^{4-i}(S)
. The homology exact sequence of the pair
(X,D)
gives
H^{3}(S)=\widetilde{H}_{0}(D)
,
H^{i}(S)=0
for
i\geqslant 4
, and
an exact sequence

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[D]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{NS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{D}}

Assume that
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP. Then the sequence (
4
) shows that
\mathbb{Z}[D]
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{NS}(X)
, of finite index
\#H^{2}(S)
. The intersection product, which is a perfect pairing on
X
, has discriminant
|d(D)|
when restricted to
\mathbb{Z}[D]
, so
\#H^{2}(S)=|d(D)|^{1/2}
, which proves the first equality in the formula (
3
). Moreover, the sequence (
4
) gives
H_{1}(D;\mathbb{Q})=\widetilde{H}_{0}(D;\mathbb{Q})=0
, i.e.
D
is a rational tree. So if
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP then condition
(b)
holds.

Assume now that
(b)
holds, i.e.
D
is a rational tree. Then
H_{1}(D)=0
. By induction on the Picard rank
\rho(\varphi)\coloneqq\rho(X)-\rho(X^{\prime})
, it is easy to see that the map
(\varphi_{*},\mathrm{pr})
gives isomorphisms
\operatorname{NS}(X)=\operatorname{NS}(X^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{Z}[E]
and
\mathbb{Z}[D]=\mathbb{Z}[D^{\prime}]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[E-A]
. Thus we get a commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[E-A]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[D]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[D^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[E]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{NS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{NS}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{D^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}

The exact sequence (
4
) shows that
\ker r_{D}=H^{1}(S)
. Since
H_{1}(D)=0
, we get
\operatorname{coker}r_{D}=H^{2}(S)
. Snake lemma yields an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker r_{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[A]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coker}r_{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

A diagram chase shows that the connecting map is indeed
m
. We conclude that, if
(b)
holds,
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP if and only if
\operatorname{coker}r_{D^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=0
and
m\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
is an isomorphism, i.e.
(a)
and
(c)
hold. Under these conditions, we get
\#H_{1}(S)=\#H^{2}(S)=\#\operatorname{coker}m\cdot\#\operatorname{coker}r_{D^{\prime}}=|\!\det m|\cdot\#\operatorname{coker}r_{D^{\prime}}
, i.e. the second equality in (
3
).

∎

Remark 3.4

(cf.
[
Miy01
, III.4.2.1]
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth pair. Lemma
3.3
applied to
\varphi=\mathrm{id}
shows that
X\setminus D
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP if and only if
X
is rational,
D
is a rational tree, and components of
D
freely generate
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
.

Indeed, in this case condition
(a)
asserts that
X
is rational and the components of
D
generate
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
, and condition
(b)
asserts that
D
is a rational tree. To interpret condition
(c)
, note that since
A=0
, the group
\mathbb{Z}[A]
is trivial, so the map
m\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\colon\ker r_{D}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[A]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\ker r_{D}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is trivial, too. Thus condition
(c)
asserts that the components of
D
are linearly independent in
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
, as claimed.

Moreover, if
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP with a log smooth completion
(X,D)
, then
H_{i}(S;\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i\geqslant 2
, and
\#H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})=|d(D)|^{1/2}
by formula (
3
). Thus if
|d(D)|=1
, i.e. if
S
is a
\mathbb{Z}
HP, then by Whitehead theorem
S
is contractible if and only if
\pi_{1}(S)=\{1\}
. Explicit computations of
\pi_{1}(S)
can be complicated, see e.g.
[
AA21
]
for
\mathbb{Q}
HPs obtained from arrangements of lines.

Remark 3.5
.

Let us show how to compute the map
m
from the formula (
2
), assuming that the condition
3.3
(a)
holds. Choose a basis
(\mathcal{R}_{j})
of
\ker r_{D^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[D^{\prime}]
, and for a component
C
of
D^{\prime}
, let
c_{j}(C)
be the coefficient of
C
in
\mathcal{R}_{j}
. Say that
A_{i}
is the exceptional
(-1)
-curve of an expansion at
(U_{i},W_{i};u_{i}/w_{i})
,
\gcd(u_{i},w_{i})=1
. Then by Definition
1.4
, the matrix of
m
with respect to bases
(\mathcal{R}_{j})
and
(A_{i})
is
[m_{ij}]
, where

m_{ij}=u_{i}\cdot c_{j}(U_{i})+w_{i}\cdot c_{j}(W_{i}).

In particular,
m(C)=0
for every component
C
of
D^{\prime}
which does not pass through a center.

Remark 3.6
.

In Section
6
, the pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
will be a log resolution of
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
, where
X_{\min}
is a del Pezzo surface, hence it is rational;
\rho(X_{\min})=1
and
D_{\min}\neq 0
; see Lemma
3.13
(a)
. Thus in this case, the map
r_{D_{\min}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
is surjective, which implies
3.3
(a)
.

In fact, we will see that if
n=\#D_{\min}-1\geqslant 1
, the components of
D_{\min}
generate
\operatorname{NS}(X_{\min})
, hence
r_{D^{\prime}}
is surjective over
\mathbb{Z}
, too. Formula (
3
) in this case gives
\#H_{1}(X;S)=|\!\det m|
. In the
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered case, treated in Section
7
, the map
r_{D^{\prime}}
will turn out to be surjective, too, except for the tower
4
, where
\#\operatorname{coker}r_{D^{\prime}}=3
.

Formula (
3
) shows that
\#H_{1}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbb{Z})
varies a lot within a tower. More precisely, we have the following.

Lemma 3.7

(cf.
[
tDP93
, p. 859]
)
.

Fix a log smooth pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and
n\geqslant 1
centers on
D^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be the corresponding tower. Assume that for some
(X,D)\in\mathcal{T}
, the surface
S\coloneqq X\setminus D
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-homology plane. Then for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
,
\mathcal{T}
contains infinitely many pairs
(X,D)
such that
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP with
\#H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})=k
.

Fixing all but one centers and weights, we can assume
n=1
. Remark
3.5
shows that there are
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that a surface
S
obtained by expansion with weight
u/w
,
\gcd(u,w)=1
, satisfies
\#H_{1}(S)=au+bw
. Since the tower
\mathcal{T}
contains a
\mathbb{Z}
-homology plane, there are positive integers
u_{0},w_{0}
such that
au_{0}+bw_{0}=1
. It follows that, say,
a>0
and
b\leqslant 0
. Fix positive, coprime integers
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
and put
u=ku_{0}-lb
,
w=kw_{0}+la
. Then
u,w>0
;
au+bw=k
and
\gcd(u,w)=1
. To see the last statement, note that
\gcd(u,w)|k
since
au+bw=k
, so
\gcd(u,w)|l
by definition of
u,w
, and therefore
\gcd(u,w)=1
since
l
was chosen coprime to
k
, as claimed. We conclude that an expansion with weight
u/w
gives a
\mathbb{Q}
HP
S
with
\#H_{1}(S)=k
. Varying
l
we get infinitely many such weights. By Remark
3.1
(c)
, they give non-isomorphic pairs
(X,D)
.

∎

Remark 3.8
.

If the surfaces
S=X\setminus D
in Lemma
3.7
are of log general type, then by Lemma
2.1
each of them uniquely determines, up to an isomorphism, its minimal log smooth completion
(X,D)
. Hence for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
, we get infinitely many pairwise non-isomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
HPs
S
with
\#H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})=k
.

In order to view a pair
(X,D)
as an expansion
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, we need find a
(-1)
-curve
A\subseteq X
which can serve as the exceptional curve of the first blowdown. Such
A
are distinguished as follows.

Definition 3.9

(Bubble)
.

Let
D
be a connected reduced divisor on a smooth projective surface
X
. We say that a
(-1)
-curve
A\subseteq X
is a
bubble
on
(X,D)
if

A\not\subseteq D,\quad A\cdot D=2\quad\mbox{and }A\mbox{ meets }D\mbox{ in two points, lying on different components of }D.

In particular, if
A
is a bubble then
A
is a superfluous
(-1)
-curve in
A+D
.

U
-3
-2
A
-1
-3
V
U
V

Figure 3
.
A bubble
A
and an expansion with weight
\frac{3}{5}
and center
(U,V)
.

Assume that some log smooth completion
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
of
S
is obtained by an expansion
\widetilde{\psi}\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. The following observation shows that, in case which is of most interest for us,
\widetilde{\psi}
factors through the
minimal
log smooth completion of
S
. Therefore, even if we allowed arbitrary log smooth completions in Theorem
1.6
, we would not get a shorter list.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
S
be a smooth surface of log general type which admits no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration. Let
\tau\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(X,D)
be a birational morphism between log smooth completions of
S
; and let
\widetilde{A}
be a bubble on
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
. Then
\tau(\widetilde{A})
is a bubble on
(X,D)
.

Suppose the contrary. Then
\tau
is not an isomorphism in any neighborhood of
\widetilde{A}
. By induction on
\rho(\tau)
, we can assume that
\widetilde{A}
meets a
(-1)
-curve
U\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\tau
, so
\widetilde{A}\cdot U=1
by Definition
3.9
. Since
D=\tau_{*}\widetilde{D}
is snc,
U
is superfluous in
D
. The linear system of
U+\widetilde{A}=[1,1]
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\pi
of
\widetilde{X}
whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot\widetilde{D}=(U+\widetilde{A})\cdot\widetilde{D}=\beta_{\widetilde{D}}(U)+U^{2}+\widetilde{A}\cdot\widetilde{D}\leqslant 2-1+2=3
. Since
\kappa(S)=2
, the Iitaka easy addition theorem implies that
F\cdot\widetilde{D}=3
, so
\pi|_{S}
is a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration, a contradiction.

∎

We explain the effect of expansions on Kodaira dimensions. Lemma
3.11
(a)
shows that
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)
is preserved. In fact, by
[
Pal21
, Lemma 5.9(2),(4)]
a bubble
A
on
(X,D)
is, usually, almost log exceptional for
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
, i.e. some MMP run for
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
contracts
A
, see
[
Pal21
, Definitions 3.6, 3.17]
. The very design of MMP requires
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)
to be preserved, see
[
Pal21
, Corollary 3.2, Remark 3.16]
.

On the other hand, MMP for
(X,D)
does not see
A
: in fact, by Lemma
2.3
and
[
Pal21
, Lemmas 4.3, 4.5]
it contracts only twigs of
D
. And indeed, expansions may change
\kappa(K_{X}+D)
. However, Lemma
3.11
(b)
shows that if some expansion produces a surface of log general type, then all further ones do so, too. In particular, in order to verify that
\mathbb{Q}
HPs in Theorem
1.6
are of log general type, which we do in Proposition
11.1
, we only need to consider expansions of low Picard rank; usually one.

Lemma 3.11

(
\kappa
does not drop within a tower)
.

Let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be an expansion. Then

(a)

\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=\kappa(K_{X^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1}{2}D^{\prime})
,

(b)

Assume that
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is an expansion with the same centers as
\varphi
, such that
\widetilde{\varphi}=\varphi\circ\eta
for some birational morphism
\eta
. Then
\kappa(\widetilde{X}\setminus\widetilde{D})\geqslant\kappa(X\setminus D)
.

By induction, we can assume that
\varphi
has only one center. Let
A\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
be the bubble.

(a)
For any integers
a,b\geqslant 1
, we have
A\cdot(a(2K_{X}+D)+bA)=-b<0
, so
A
is in the fixed part of
|a(2K_{X}+D)+bA|
. By induction on
b
, we conclude that
|a(2K_{X}+D)|
and
|a(2K_{X}+D+A)|
define the same map, which contracts
A
. Hence
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}(D+A))=\kappa(K_{X^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1}{2}D^{\prime})
by induction on
\rho(\varphi)
.

(b)
Put
E=\operatorname{Exc}\eta
. By induction, we can assume that
E=[1]
. The point
\eta(E)
lies in
A\cap D\subseteq D^{\mathrm{reg}}
, so
K_{\widetilde{X}}+\widetilde{D}=(\eta^{*}K_{X}+E)+\eta^{-1}_{*}(D+A)=\eta^{*}(K_{X}+D)+\eta^{-1}_{*}A
, which gives an injective map
|a(K_{X}+D)|\hookrightarrow|a(K_{\widetilde{X}}+\widetilde{D})|
for all
a\geqslant 1
.

∎

We will now show how the theory of almost minimal models
[
Pal21
]
recovers all
\mathbb{Q}
HPs by expansions from particularly simple pairs. The remaining task will be to relate those pairs with planar configurations.

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP of log general type. Let
A
be a bubble on
(X,D)
. Contract
A
and all superfluous
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent images of
D
, passing through the image of
A
; and repeat this until the image of
(X,D)
has no bubbles. The process must end since
\rho(X)
drops at each step. Since
D
is connected, Lemma
3.2
implies that the resulting morphism

\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})

is an expansion. Let
\alpha^{\prime}\colon(X^{\prime},\tfrac{1}{2}D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
be a log MMP run. Let
\upsilon\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X_{\mathrm{am}}
be its
almost minimalization

[
Pal21
, Definition 3.1]
, that is, a
K_{X^{\prime}}
-MMP run over
X_{\min}
. Then
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\circ\upsilon
for some morphism
\alpha\colon X_{\mathrm{am}}\longrightarrow X_{\min}
. The characterization of (almost) log exceptional curves on
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
, given in
[
Pal21
, §5]
implies the following.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP
S
of log general type. Let

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\min},D_{\min})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

be as above. Let
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
be the sum of all maximal
(-2)
-twigs of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
, and
R_{\mathrm{am}}=D_{\mathrm{am}}-\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
. Then

(a)

S^{\prime}\coloneqq X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
is an affine open subset of
S
,

(b)

\kappa(S^{\prime})=2
and
S^{\prime}
contains no topologically contractible curves,

(c)

\operatorname{Exc}\alpha=\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
and
\alpha^{*}(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})=2K_{X_{\mathrm{am}}}+D_{\mathrm{am}}-\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
,

(d)

X_{\mathrm{am}}
is smooth, and each singularity of
X_{\min}
is canonical cyclic (type
\mathrm{A}_{k}
),

(e)

\operatorname{Exc}\upsilon\subseteq D^{\prime}
, and every
(-1)
-curve
U
in
D_{\mathrm{am}}
satisfies
\beta_{D_{\mathrm{am}}}(U)\geqslant 4
or
\beta_{R_{\mathrm{am}}}(U)\geqslant 3
,

(f)

There are no bubbles on
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
or
(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
,

(g)

All singularities of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
and
D_{\min}
are of multiplicity
2
; and
\operatorname{Sing}D_{\min}\subseteq X_{\min}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

(a)
By
[
Fuj82
, 2.5]
,
S
is affine. Since
S^{\prime}
equals
S
minus a sum of bubbles,
S^{\prime}
is affine, too.

(b)
This follows from
(a)
and Lemma
2.3
.

(c)
By
[
Pal21
, Definition 3.10]
,
\alpha
is a pure peeling, so
(c)
follows from
[
Pal21
, Corrolary 5.5]
. Indeed, by
(a)

D_{\mathrm{am}}
has no negative definite connected components, so the divisor
\Gamma+\Lambda
defined in loc. cit. is zero in this case.

(d)
By construction, the surface
X^{\prime}
is smooth, so the surface
X_{\mathrm{am}}
, which is an outcome of an
K_{X^{\prime}}
-MMP run is smooth, too. By
(c)
, all singularities of
X_{\min}
are images of
(-2)
-chains in
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
, so they are of type
\mathrm{A}_{k}
.

(e)
,
(f)
Let
\overline{\upsilon}\colon(X^{\prime},\tfrac{1}{2}D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\overline{X}_{\mathrm{am}},\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}})
be an
\alpha^{\prime}
-squeezing, that, is, a contraction of all
(-1)
-curves in
D^{\prime}
and its images which are contracted by
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\circ\upsilon
. By construction,
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
has no bubbles, so
(\overline{X}_{\mathrm{am}},\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}})
has no bubbles either. Indeed, suppose
\overline{A}
is a bubble on
(\overline{X}_{\mathrm{am}},\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}})
. Since
D^{\prime}
is snc-minimal and has no negative definite connected components, we have
\operatorname{Bs}\overline{\upsilon}^{-1}\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}}
. In particular,
\overline{\upsilon}
is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\overline{\upsilon}^{-1}_{*}A
. Thus
A^{\prime}
is a bubble on
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
; a contradiction.

Suppose
\overline{\upsilon}\neq\upsilon
. Then
(\overline{X}_{\mathrm{am}},\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}})
contains an almost log exceptional curve
A
. By
[
Pal21
, Lemma 5.9]
,
A
is a
(-1)
-curve such that either
A\cdot\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}}\leqslant 1
, which is impossible by
(b)
, or
A
is a bubble on
(\overline{X}_{\mathrm{am}},\overline{D}_{\mathrm{am}})
, which is impossible by the above claim. Thus
\overline{\upsilon}=\upsilon
, so
(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
has no bubbles and
\operatorname{Exc}\upsilon\subseteq D^{\prime}
. The second part of
(e)
follows from the characterization of squeezing in
[
Pal21
, Lemma 5.2]
.

(g)
This follows from
[
Pal21
, Remark 5.7]
.

∎

In our case, minimality of
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
implies the following.

Lemma 3.13
.

We keep the notation and assumptions from Lemma
3.12
. Assume further that
S
satisfies the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
and admits no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration. Then

(a)

\rho(X_{\min})=1
and
-(K_{X_{\min}}+\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
is ample.

(b)

X_{\min}
is a del Pezzo surface of rank one, with only
\mathrm{A}_{k}
-type singularities.

(c)

\#R_{\mathrm{am}}=n+1
, and
R_{\mathrm{am}}
has exactly
2n
nodes.

(a)
By assumption,
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty
, so
\kappa(K_{X^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1}{2}D^{\prime})=-\infty
by Lemma
3.11
(a)
. Log abundance in dimension two, cf.
[
Mat02
, p. 127]
, shows that
K_{X_{\min}}+\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min}
is not nef. So there is a curve
F\subseteq X_{\min}
such that
F\cdot(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})<0
. By the contraction theorem
[
Mat02
, 1-3-5]
,
F
is contracted by some morphism
\varphi\colon X_{\min}\longrightarrow Z
with
\rho(\varphi)=1
. Because
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
is minimal,
F^{2}\geqslant 0
, so
\dim Z\leqslant 1
. Suppose
\dim Z=1
. Then
F^{2}=0
, so
F\cdot(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})<0
implies that
F\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
F\cdot D_{\min}\leqslant 3
. Since
\kappa(X_{\min}\setminus D_{\min})=2
, we have
F\cdot D_{\min}=3
by the Iitaka easy addition theorem, so
\varphi
pulls back to a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of
S
; a contradiction. Therefore,
Z
is a point, which implies
(a)
.

(b)
This follows from
(a)
and Lemma
3.12
(d)
.

(c)
By Lemma
3.12
(c)
,
R_{\mathrm{am}}=\alpha^{-1}_{*}D_{\min}
, so by
(a)
,
\#R_{\mathrm{am}}-1=\#D_{\min}-\rho(X_{\min})=\#D+n-\rho(X)=n
since
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)=\mathbb{Z}[D]\otimes\mathbb{Q}
by Remark
3.4
. Thus
\#R_{\mathrm{am}}=n+1
. To prove the second equality, we introduce the following notation. For a reduced divisor
B\neq 0
, let
\nu_{B}
be the number of nodes of
B
, and let
e_{B}=\nu_{B}-\#B
. Clearly,
e_{B}
does not change under snc-minimization or a contraction of a tip of
B
. It is easy to see that resolving a double point does not change
e_{B}
, either. In turn, adding a bubble to
B
increases
e_{B}
by one. We get
e_{R_{\mathrm{am}}}=e_{D_{\mathrm{am}}}=e_{D^{\prime}}=e_{(\psi^{*}D^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}}=e_{D}+n=-1+n
since
D
is a tree. Now
\nu_{R_{\mathrm{am}}}=\#R_{\mathrm{am}}+e_{R_{\mathrm{am}}}=2n
.

∎

We conclude this preliminary section by a simple application of Lemma
3.12
, which shows that the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
is closely related to another variant of tom Dieck–Petrie Conjecture
1.1
(a)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a minimal log smooth completion of a
\mathbb{Q}
HP
S
. We say that a divisor
B
on a surface
Y
is
optimal
for
S
if there is an expansion
(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, where
(Y^{\prime},B^{\prime})\longrightarrow(Y,B)
is a minimal log resolution, see
[
tD92
]
. In other words,
(X,D)
is obtained by the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm with
P=\emptyset
, but with
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
replaced by
(Y,B)
.
Thus
D_{\min}\subseteq X_{\min}
in Lemma
3.12
is an optimal divisor for
S
.

Tom Dieck proposed the following conjecture, see
[
tD92
, Conjecture 1.2]
.

Conjecture 3.14
.

A smooth
\mathbb{Z}
-homology plane of log general type has an optimal divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or
\mathbb{F}_{m}
.

We remark that the definition of an optimal divisor in
[
tD92
]
does not require the log smooth completion
(X,D)
of
S
to be minimal. But Lemma
3.10
shows that we can add this assumption if
S
is not
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered. In the other case, it is easy to construct an optimal divisor for
S
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or on
\mathbb{F}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
: we will do so in Section
7
, see Figure
22
and
[
MS91
]
. Therefore, Conjecture
3.14
holds for
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered
\mathbb{Z}
HPs, and thus is equivalent to
[
tD92
, Conjecture 1.2]
.

Assume that
S
has no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration, and satisfies the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
. Then the optimal divisor
D_{\min}\subseteq X_{\min}
is as in Lemma
3.13
(b)
: hence
S
admits an optimal divisor on a del Pezzo surface of rank one, with at most
\mathrm{A}_{k}
singularities
.

In fact, in Proposition
6.1
we will see that
X_{\min}
can be chosen to be
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
, or
\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
. We conclude that Negativity Conjecture implies the following slight modification of Conjecture
3.14
.

Corollary 3.15
.

Every smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type, satisfying the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, admits an optimal divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
\mathbb{F}_{0}
,
\mathbb{F}_{2}
or
\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
.

The singular surface
\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
cannot be replaced by
\mathbb{F}_{m}
. Indeed, if
S
is as in
–
or
, then
X_{\min}=\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
, and our construction shows that the only bubbles on
(X,D)
are those contracted by
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\min},D_{\min})
. Thus Conjecture
3.14
fails for
\mathbb{Z}
HPs in these towers.

4.
General strategy and notation

In order to settle the notation for Section
6
, we now review our strategy of the proof of Theorem
1.6
, and explain it on a typical example.

Recall that the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, introduced in Definition
1.5
, takes as an input: a configuration of lines and conics
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
, its minimal resolution
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
and a subdivisor
D^{\prime}\subseteq(\pi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}
; and constructs from it a pair
(X,D)
by an expansion
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.

We need to show that, conversely, for any
\mathbb{Q}
HP satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
1.6
, its minimal log smooth completion
(X,D)
is constructed this way. To do this, we take for
\psi
the expansion from Lemma
3.12
. By
[
MS91
]
or by Proposition
7.3
, we can assume that
S
has no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibrations, so
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
is as in Lemma
3.13
. In particular,
\rho(X_{\min})=1
, and
n\coloneqq\#D_{\min}-1
is the number of centers of
\psi
.

To recover
\pi\circ\psi
, we find
\#{p}-n-1
curves on
X^{\prime}
such that adding them to
D^{\prime}
gives a divisor with the same weighted graph as
\pi^{*}{p}
, hence contractible to a configuration with the same graph as
{p}
, on a smooth, projective rational surface of Picard rank
\rho(X_{\min})+(\#D^{\prime}-\#D_{\min})-(\#(\pi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}-\#{p})=1
, that is, on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The graph of
{p}
does not always determine
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
uniquely, nonetheless, if one
{p}
realizing such graph is allowed in Theorem
1.6
, then so are all of them, see Section
8
. Thus we get
\pi\circ\psi
as required.

A major part of the proof consists of transforming given planar configurations (e.g.
D_{\min}
if
X_{\min}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
) to configurations of lines and conics using Cremona maps. For this, we introduce the following notation.

Notation 4.1
.

Curves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
will be denoted by lowercase script letters
{c}_{\star},{l}_{\star},\dots
with some subscripts
\star
. Their proper transforms on
X^{\prime}
will be denoted by corresponding uppercase letters
C_{\star},L_{\star},\dots
. For two points
p,q\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the line joining them will be denoted by
{l}_{pq}
. For a smooth curve germ
(p,\chi)
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
the line tangent to
\chi
at
p
will be denoted by
{l}_{\chi,p}
or simply
{l}_{p}
if
\chi
is clear from the context.

Notation 4.2
.

For a point
r
on a smooth surface we denote by
\pi_{r}
a blow up at
r
. Fix a birational morphism
\sigma
such that
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma^{-1}=\{r\}
, and
\sigma^{-1}(r)
contains a unique
(-1)
-curve. Then the components of
\sigma^{-1}(r)
are naturally ordered as exceptional curves of subsequent blowups in the decomposition of
\sigma
. We denote them by
E_{r},E_{r}^{\prime},\dots
, so
E_{r}
is the
(-1)
-curve. We write
U_{r},U_{r}^{\prime},\dots
for the same curves in the reverse order.

Remark 4.3
.

Notation
4.1
and
4.2
is also used in Figures
5
–
22
, which show graphs of
\pi^{*}{p}
encountered in the course of the proof. There, Notation
4.2
refers always to the morphism
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
, not to
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X_{\min}
.

Dashed lines in these figures correspond to components of
E^{\prime}\coloneqq(\pi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}-D^{\prime}
. By definition, they are
(-1)
-curves not contained in
D^{\prime}
, and meet
D^{\prime}
normally. According to Notation
4.2
, we denote them by
E_{p}
, where the sub-index refers to a point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
which is the image of
E_{p}
.

Sometimes, introducing too many dashed lines could obscure the figure. In such cases, instead of drawing a curve
E_{p}
, we just mark the points where it meets
D^{\prime}
with some fixed symbol, say
“
\bullet
”
, and add a comment
“
\bullet\mapsto p
”
. For example, in the top-right picture of Figure
4
below, the symbol
“
\bullet
”
refers to a curve
E_{p}
meeting
D^{\prime}
in
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
and
C_{2}
. Similarly, curves
E_{p_{1}}
and
E_{r}
meet
D^{\prime}
in points marked by
“
\boldsymbol{\diamond}
”
and
“
\boldsymbol{\circ}
”
, respectively.

Example 4.4
.

In order to illustrate the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, we follow it closely in the case of tower
. For another example of this kind see
[
tD92
, p. 132]
, where tower
is constructed (from a planar divisor slightly different than ours).

We take a planar divisor
{p}={c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{p^{\prime}}
from Configuration
12
, that is,
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{p^{\prime}}
are lines tangent to the conic
{c}_{1}
; and
{c}_{2}
is a conic tangent to
{c}_{1}
with multiplicity
2
, passing through
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}=\{p\}
,
{l}_{1}\cap{c}_{1}=\{p_{1}\}
and tangent to
{l}_{p^{\prime}}
at
{l}_{2}\cap{l}_{p^{\prime}}=\{r\}
, see Figure
4
. We take
P=\{p,p_{1},r\}
.

Let
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the minimal resolution of
{p}
, and let
E^{\prime}\coloneqq E_{p}+E_{p_{1}}+E_{r}
be the sum of
(-1)
-curves over
P
. The divisor
D^{\prime}\coloneqq(\pi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}-E^{\prime}
is shown in the top-right of Figure
4
. It meets
E^{\prime}
in points denoted by
\bullet
,
\boldsymbol{\diamond}
and
\boldsymbol{\circ}
(this notation is explained in Remark
4.3
). We use Notation
4.1
for the components of
D^{\prime}
.

Now,
C_{1}+C_{2}+E_{q}
is a unique circular subdivisor of
D^{\prime}
: thus an expansion
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
such that
D
is a tree must have a unique center, which is a node of
C_{1}+C_{2}+E_{q}
. Each of these choices leads to a pair
(X,D)
with a different graph of
D
.

We now use Lemma
3.3
to check when
S=X\setminus D
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane. We compute that the map
r_{D^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{Z}[D^{\prime}]\longrightarrow\operatorname{NS}(X^{\prime})
is surjective, and
\ker r_{D^{\prime}}
is generated by one relation, say
\mathcal{R}
. By Remark
3.5
,
m(\mathcal{R})=m(\mathcal{R}_{0})
, where
\mathcal{R}_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{C_{1}+C_{2}+E_{q}}(\mathcal{R})
. We compute that, up to a sign:

\mathcal{R}_{0}=C_{1}-6C_{2}-10E_{q}.

Write the weight of our expansion as
\frac{u}{w}
,
\gcd(u,w)=1
. Consider the center
(C_{1},C_{2})
. Then
m(\mathcal{R}_{0})=u-6w
, see Remark
3.5
. Hence
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP if and only if
\frac{u}{w}\neq 6
. In this case formula (
3
) gives
\#H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})=|u-6w|
, so we get infinitely many
\mathbb{Z}
HPs, for weights
6\pm\tfrac{1}{w}
,
w\in\mathbb{N}
. For weight
1
we get
\#H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})=5
: as we will see in Example
4.5
below, this
S
is in fact a complement of a planar quintic.

Similarly, for
(C_{1},E_{q})
we have
m(\mathcal{R}_{0})=u-10w
, so
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP if and only if
u/w\neq 10
, and we get infinitely many
\mathbb{Z}
HPs. But for
(C_{2},E_{q})
,
m(\mathcal{R}_{0})=6u+10w\geqslant 16
, so all
S
’s are
\mathbb{Q}
HPs and none is a
\mathbb{Z}
HP.

Surface obtained in
by an expansion with center
