Example 4.5

(Different minimal models in a tower)
.

In Lemma
3.12
(f)
, we have chosen
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
with no bubbles. This choice is not necessary for the application of MMP as in Lemma
3.12
. However, it will be very useful to restrict the possible types of
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
. To see this, we consider
S
be as in Example
4.4
above.

The expansion
\psi
maps
(X,D)
to
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
shown in the top-right of Figure
4
. Then the almost minimalization
\upsilon\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
contracts
D^{\prime}-(C_{1}+C_{2}+U_{p_{1}})
, so
X_{\mathrm{am}}=\mathbb{F}_{2}
, with negative section
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=\upsilon(U_{p_{1}})
, fiber
\upsilon(C_{2})
and a
3
-section
\upsilon(C_{1})
. Thus
X_{\min}=\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
.

Consider a particular case when
\psi
is centered at
(C_{1},C_{2};1)
, so
\operatorname{Exc}\psi
is the bubble
A
in Figure
4
.
Let
\tau
be the contraction of
D-\psi^{-1}_{*}C_{1}
. Then
\tau_{*}D
is the tricuspidal quintic
\mathcal{Q}_{3}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
from
[
Nam84
, 2.3.10.8]
, see Table
12
. The pair
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}_{3})
satisfies Lemma
3.13
(a)
, so we could have taken
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})=(\mathbb{P}^{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}_{3})
for our minimal model of
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
. In fact,
\tau
is the unique MMP run for
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
as in
[
Pal21
, Lemma 3.11]
. Nonetheless, in Lemma
3.12
we have decided to contract
A
anyway.

Note that the image of
A
is visible on the minimal model
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}_{3})
: it is the line tangent to
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
at one of its cusps. This suggests that the lack of bubbles restricts the geometry of
D_{\min}
. It is indeed the case, and we will use it frequently, see claims (
13
) or (
14
). For instance, claim (
14
) – roughly speaking – shows that whenever
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
has no bubbles, lines like
\tau(A)
should lie in
D_{\min}
. Moreover, a lot of potential bubbles is produced by Lemma
2.4
, see Figure
2
: we will exploit them in Proposition
6.1
to restrict the singularity types of
X_{\min}
.

Example 4.6

(Corollaries
1.7
(c)
and
1.8
for
)
.

We will now outline the proof of Corollary
1.7
(c)
for surfaces considered in Example
4.4
; that is, we explain why the weighted graph of
D
uniquely determines the isomorphism class of
(X,D)
, hence of
S
. We will make this explanation more rigorous in Example
9.2
, after introducing suitable notation. For another illustration of this argument see Example
9.3
.

First, note that the computation of Configuration
12
in Section
8
shows that, up to a projective equivalence, there are exactly two planar divisors
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
as in Example
4.4
, see Proposition
8.3
(a)
.

Since the centers of the blowups in the decomposition of
\pi
are determined uniquely by
{p}
, we get a one-to-one correspondence between isomorphism classes of pairs
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
and
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+E^{\prime})
,
each with fixed order of the boundary components
. For
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
, this order is uniquely determined by the combinatorics of
{p}
. But the weighted graph
D^{\prime}+E^{\prime}
has an involution
\iota

interchanging the subchains
[2,3,1,2]
of
D^{\prime}
, see Figure
4
.
The two possible orders of the components of
D^{\prime}+E^{\prime}
correspond to two classes of projective equivalence of
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
. We conclude that
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+E^{\prime})
is actually unique up to an isomorphism. Now since the centers of the blowups in the decomposition of
\psi^{\prime}
are determined uniquely by
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+E^{\prime})
, we infer that
(X,D)
is unique, as claimed.

The graph of
D
has an involution coming from
\iota
, but we have seen that it is not induced by automorphism of
S
. However, for an expansion at
(C_{1},C_{2};1)
, shown in Figure
4
, the graph of
D
has another automorphism, of order three, which does correspond to an automorphism of
S
. Indeed, we have seen in Example
4.5
that in this case
S
is the complement of the tricuspidal quintic
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, so
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
is generated by the rotation of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, see
[
tD92
, Proposition 2.9]
,
[
PP19
, Remark 4.8]
or Example
9.2
.

5.
Comparison with some results in the literature

We now comment how some well-known examples fit into our classification. As in Section
3
, we denote by
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
the tower obtained in Theorem
1.6
from
*
-th row of Tables
–
.

The first example of a
topologically contractible
surface which is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
was given by Ramanujam
[
Ram71
, §3]
. We recover it in
by expansions with centers
(L_{pp^{\prime}},C_{1})
,
(L_{pp^{\prime}},C_{1})
,
(E_{p},L_{2})
and weights
(\frac{1}{2},1,1)
. Originally, this surface was distinguished from
\mathbb{C}^{2}
using the fundamental group at infinity. In fact, by loc. cit.
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the only contractible surface for which this group is trivial. Another way to distinguish it is to show that its Kodaira dimension is two. It was done by Gurjar and Miyanishi in
[
GM88
, §4]
. In loc. cit, another, similar example of a contractible surface of log general type was constructed, we recover it in
by expansions with centers
(E_{p^{\prime}},C_{1})
,
(E_{p_{2}},C_{1})
and weights
(1,1)
, cf.
[
tDP93
, 3.15]
.

Some generalizations of Ramanujam’s construction are given in
[
Sug90
]
. In the Appendix to loc. cit, Miyanishi and Sugie constructed a series of
\mathbb{Z}
-homology planes of log general type. We recover them in
by expansions with centers
(L_{1},L_{2})
,
(E_{q_{1}},L_{q_{1}q_{2}})
,
(L_{1}^{\prime},L_{1})
,
(L_{2}^{\prime},L_{2})
and weights
(m^{-1},n-2,r+1,(n-m)^{-1})
, where
r=m(n-1)
or
m(n-1)-2
. By Theorem 2 loc. cit, the surfaces with
r=m(n-1)
are contractible.

As an illustration of their algorithm, in
[
tD92
]
tom Dieck and Petrie constructed
14
towers
A,B,\dots,N
. In our classification, they are called
,
3
,
,
,
,
,
,
,
2
,
,
,
,
,
4
, respectively. Proposition 2.2 loc. cit. shows that for towers
A
,
B
,
C
, the initial planar divisor
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
can be an arrangement of lines. It is not so e.g. for the tower
I
, see
[
tD90
]
. All line arrangements leading to
\mathbb{Q}
HPs of log general type are classified in
[
tDP91
]
; those consisting of lines and one conic were studied in
[
Neu92
]
.
Unfortunately, exhaustive lists in each case are rather long, which makes it difficult to study the resulting
\mathbb{Q}
HPs directly. Therefore, we will not attempt to compare them with Theorem
1.6
(which shows that, assuming Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, a much shorter list of planar arrangements is sufficient).

A plenty of examples of
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes is given by surfaces
S=\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\overline{E}
, where
\overline{E}
is a
rational cuspidal curve
, i.e. a curve homeomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
in the Euclidean topology. Classification of such curves, up to a projective equivalence, is still an open problem, with many interesting connections to low-dimensional topology and singularity theory, see e.g.
[
FdBL+07
,
KP22
]
and references there.

Those rational cuspidal curves whose complements are of log general type and satisfy the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
were classified by Palka and the author in
[
PP17
,
PP19
]
. Table
12
shows how to recover
2
2

2

To be precise, it shows how to construct the pair
(X,D)
, where
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{E})
is the minimal log resolution. This pair is the minimal log smooth completion of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\overline{E}
in all cases except
\mathcal{A}
-
\mathcal{D}
with
\gamma=1
, where one needs to contract a superfluous
(-1)
-curve
\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{E}\subseteq D
. And indeed, the respective weights from Table
12
are not admissible (see Table
). In these cases, the minimal log smooth completion is obtained in
by expansion with centers
(L_{1},L_{2})
,
(E_{p_{1}^{\prime}},L_{p_{1}^{\prime}p_{2}^{\prime}})
,
(L_{1},E_{p_{1}^{\prime}})
,
(L_{2},E_{p_{2}^{\prime}})
and weights as follows. In case
\mathcal{A}
:
(1,\tfrac{2}{2s-1},p-1,p-1+\tfrac{1}{s})
,
\mathcal{B}
:
(1,\tfrac{2}{2s-1},\tfrac{s}{ps+p-1},p-1)
,
\mathcal{C}
:
(1,\tfrac{1}{s},p-1,p-1+\tfrac{1}{2s+1})
and
\mathcal{D}
:
(1,\tfrac{1}{s},p-1+\tfrac{4}{2s+1},p-1)
. A similar adjustment is made in the proof of Proposition
7.3
.

these complements from Theorem
1.6
. Note that the towers listed there have
{N}=1
, that is, by Corollary
1.7
(c)
, they are uniquely determined by the weighted graph of
D
. On the other hand, for the complements of rational cuspidal curves, that graph depends only on the topology of the cusps. It therefore follows from Theorem
1.6
that any rational cuspidal curve whose complement is of log general type and satisfies the Negativity Conjecture, is uniquely determined, up to a projective equivalence, by the topological type of its singularities. In
[
PP19
, §4]
, this result is deduced by combining known results about rational cuspidal curves. The proof of Corollary
1.7
(c)
, given in Section
9
, provides an independent, uniform argument.

Remark 5.1

(
\mathbb{Z}
HPs)
.

Those towers from Theorem
1.6
which contain
\mathbb{Z}
-homology planes are marked by
“
\checkmark
”
in the column
“
\mathbb{Z}
”
of the respective table. By Lemma
3.7
, every such tower contains infinitely many
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes
S
with
\#H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})=k
for any positive integer
k
. Therefore, the tom Dieck–Petrie approach is well suited for the study of
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes, but not of
\mathbb{Z}
-homology planes in particular.

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

Let
S
be a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane, and let
(X,D)
be the minimal log smooth completion of
S
. To prove Theorem
1.6
we can, and will, assume that

(6)

\kappa(S)=2,\quad\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty\mbox{ and }S\mbox{ admits no $\mathbb{C}^{**}$-fibration.}

Indeed, if
S
is
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered then Theorem
1.6
follows from
[
MS91
]
, see Section
7
. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\min},D_{\min})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

be as in Lemma
3.12
. By definition,
\psi
is an expansion with, say,
n
centers. By Lemma
3.12
(f)

(7)

there are no bubbles on
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and on
(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
.

By assumption (
6
) and Lemma
3.13
(a)
,
-(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})
is ample and
\rho(X_{\min})=1
. As in Lemma
3.12
, write

(8)

D_{\mathrm{am}}=R_{\mathrm{am}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{am}},\quad\mbox{where $\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}$ is the sum of all $(-2)$-twigs of $D_{\mathrm{am}}$,}

Then by Lemma
3.12
(c)
, for any curve
U\subseteq X_{\mathrm{am}}
not contained in
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
we have

(9)

0>\alpha(U)\cdot(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})=U\cdot(2K_{X_{\mathrm{am}}}+D_{\mathrm{am}}-\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}).

Furthermore, by Lemma
3.13
(c)

(10)

\#R_{\mathrm{am}}=n+1,\quad\mbox{and}\quad R_{\mathrm{am}}\mbox{ has exactly }2n\mbox{ nodes}.

In this section, we show that all
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes satisfying assumption (
6
) are arranged in 39 towers obtained by the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm from arrangements
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
of lines and conics, listed in Section
8
.

Conversely, by Lemma
3.11
(a)
, any
\mathbb{Q}
HP whose log smooth completion
(X,D)
is obtained by an expansion
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
from a minimal log resolution of
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
as in Lemma
3.13
(a)
, automatically satisfies
\kappa(K_{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}D)=-\infty
. Thus our
\mathbb{Q}
HPs satisfy the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
. In Proposition
11.1
, we will show they are of log general type, and thus complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Note that the assumption (
6
) and the Iitaka easy addition theorem imply that

(11)

X_{\min}\setminus D_{\min}=X_{\mathrm{am}}\setminus D_{\mathrm{am}}\mbox{ admits no $\mathbb{C}^{1}$, $\mathbb{C}^{*}$- or $\mathbb{C}^{**}$-fibration.}

6A.
Possible types of
X_{\min}

By assumption (
6
), the pair
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
is as in Lemma
3.13
(a)
. In particular,
-K_{X_{\min}}
is ample,
\rho(X_{\min})=1
and
X_{\min}
has at most
\mathrm{A}_{k}
-singularities, so
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
, or
X_{\min}
is as in Lemma
2.4
. In the latter case, Figure
2
shows some
(-1)
-curves on
X_{\mathrm{am}}
. In Proposition
6.1
below, we will see that unless
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
, at least one of these curves is a bubble on
(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
, contrary to condition (
7
). Later, we will see that lack of bubbles restricts the geometry of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
, too, see Example
4.5
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
(X_{\min},\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\min})
be as above.
Write
D_{\mathrm{am}}=R_{\mathrm{am}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
as in formula (
8
). Then

(a)

The surface
X_{\min}
is either:
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, or a quadric cone
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
, or
\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
.

(b)

Assume that
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
and let
U\subseteq X_{\mathrm{am}}
be the proper transform of the line joining its singular points, see Figure
2(a)
. Then
U\not\subseteq D_{\mathrm{am}}
,
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=1
and
U
meets
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
in tips of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
.

Assume
X_{\min}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
. By Lemma
3.13
(a)
,
X_{\min}
is one of the surfaces in Lemma
2.4
, and
-(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})
is ample. Let
U\subseteq X_{\mathrm{am}}
be a
(-1)
-curve. The inequality (
9
) and the adjunction formula give

0>U\cdot(2K_{X_{\mathrm{am}}}+R_{\mathrm{am}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}-\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}})=-2+U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}+U\cdot(\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}-\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}),

so

(12)

U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}<2-U\cdot(\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}-\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}})\leqslant 2,\quad\mbox{in particular}\quad U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}\leqslant 1.

Claim
.

Assume that
U
meets exactly one connected component
T
of
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
. The following
cannot
hold:

(a)

U\cdot T=1
and
U
does not meet a tip of
T
.

(b)

U\cdot T=2
,
\#T\geqslant 2
and
U
meets both tips of
T
.

Suppose
(a)
or
(b)
holds. Then
U\not\subseteq R_{\mathrm{am}}
, since
R_{\mathrm{am}}
meets
T
once, in a tip. Consider case
(a)
. Then
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}\geqslant 1
since by Lemma
3.12
(b)

X_{\mathrm{am}}\setminus D_{\mathrm{am}}
contains no contractible curves. By the formula (
12
),
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=1
, hence
U
is a bubble on
(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
, contrary to the condition (
7
). In case
(b)
, formula (
1
) gives
U\cdot\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=1
, so
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=0
by formula (
12
), and again
U
is a bubble; a contradiction.

∎Conditions
(a)
and
(b)
above exclude
X_{\min}
shown in Figures
2(f)
–
2(j)
and
2(k)
–
2(n)
, respectively.

Suppose
X_{\min}
is of type
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Figure
2(e)
. Then
\vartheta_{*}D_{\mathrm{am}}={q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
, where:
{q}
is a cubic with a node, say
q\in{q}
,
{l}_{1}
is a line tangent to
{q}
at its inflection point, say
p_{1}
, and
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{3}
are lines such that for some
p_{2},p_{3}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
we have
p_{1}\in{l}_{3}
,
p_{3}\in{l}_{2}
and
({q}\cdot{l}_{2})_{p_{2}}=({q}\cdot{l}_{3})_{p_{3}}=2
, see
[
Fur86
, p. 15]
. Now the proper transform of the line joining
q
with
p_{3}
satisfies condition
(b)
above; a contradiction.

Suppose that
X_{\min}
is of one of the types

3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,

2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
or
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Figures
2(b)
–
2(d)
. Then
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
has a connected component
T=[2,2,2]
or
[2,2]
such that each of its tips, say
T^{\pm}
meet a
(-1)
-curve, say
U^{\pm}\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta
. Since
R_{\mathrm{am}}
meets only one tip of
T
, say
T^{-}
, we have
U^{+}\not\subseteq R_{\mathrm{am}}
. Formula (
1
) gives
U^{+}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}T=1-\tfrac{1}{\#T+1}
. Looking at Figures
2(b)
–
2(d)
again, we see that
U^{+}\cdot\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=2
, and
U^{+}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}(\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}-T)\geqslant\tfrac{1}{\#T+1}
, so
U^{+}\cdot(\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}-\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}})\geqslant 1
. By formula (
12
),
U^{+}\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=0
, so
U^{+}
is a bubble; a contradiction with condition (
7
).

Thus
X_{\min}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{2}
, i.e.
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
. Let
U
be the
(-1)
-curve in Figure
2(a)
. Suppose some
(-2)
-tip of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
does not meet
U
. Then
U\cdot\operatorname{Bk}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=\tfrac{5}{6}
, so
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}\leqslant 0
by formula (
12
). If
U\subseteq R_{\mathrm{am}}
then
\beta_{R_{\mathrm{am}}}(U)=U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}-U^{2}\leqslant 1
and
\beta_{D_{\mathrm{am}}}(U)=\beta_{R_{\mathrm{am}}}(U)+U\cdot\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}\leqslant 3
, contrary to Lemma
3.12
(e)
. Hence
U\not\subseteq R_{\mathrm{am}}
, so
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=0
and thus
U
is a bubble on
(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
; a contradiction with condition (
7
).

We have shown that
U
meets both
(-2)
-tips of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
, in particular,
U\not\subseteq D_{\mathrm{am}}
. Now
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}\leqslant 1
by formula (
12
), so in fact
U\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=1
by condition (
7
), as claimed.

∎

Proposition
6.1
shows that, under our assumptions,
X_{\min}
is
\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
,
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
, or
\mathbb{P}^{2}

6C.
Case
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)

In this case,
\alpha\colon X_{\mathrm{am}}=\mathbb{F}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)=X_{\min}
is a blowup at the vertex, so
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
is the negative section, see Section
2F
. Let
F
be the fiber of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, so
F\cdot\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=1
. Condition (
11
) gives
F\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=F\cdot D_{\mathrm{am}}-1\geqslant 3
. By formula (
9
),

0>F\cdot(2K_{X_{\mathrm{am}}}+R_{\mathrm{am}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}})=F\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}-3-\tfrac{1}{2}\geqslant-\tfrac{1}{2}
,
hence
F\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=3
.
Recall that by formula (
10
)
R_{\mathrm{am}}
has
2n
nodes and
n+1
components. By Lemma
3.3
(b)
, all components of
R_{\mathrm{am}}
are rational, and by Lemma
3.12
(g)
and all singularities of
R_{\mathrm{am}}
have multiplicity
2
.

Because
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
is a tip of
D_{\mathrm{am}}=R_{\mathrm{am}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
, the divisor
R_{\mathrm{am}}
is of type
(1,3)
, see Section
2F
. We claim that

(13)

\mbox{if a singular point }p\mbox{ of }(R_{\mathrm{am}})_{\mathrm{hor}}\mbox{ has }\delta_{p}\geqslant 3\mbox{ then }p\mbox{ lies on a positive section in }(R_{\mathrm{am}})_{\mathrm{hor}}.

Suppose the contrary. Then
p\not\in\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
since
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
is a tip of
D_{\mathrm{am}}
. Let
L_{p}
be the tangent section to
R_{\mathrm{am}}
at
p
, see Section
2F
. Then
L_{p}\cdot\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=0
and
L_{p}\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}}=7
because
L_{p}
and
R_{\mathrm{am}}
are of type
(1,3)
and
(0,1)
, respectively. Moreover, the condition
\delta_{p}\geqslant 3
implies that
(L_{p}\cdot R_{\mathrm{am}})_{p}\geqslant 6
. By assumption,
L_{p}\not\subseteq D_{\mathrm{am}}
. By Lemma
3.12
(b)
, the surface
X_{\mathrm{am}}\setminus D_{\mathrm{am}}
contains no contractible curves, so
L_{p}
meets
D_{\mathrm{am}}\setminus\{p\}
. Hence
L_{p}
meets
D_{\mathrm{am}}
at
p
with multiplicity
6
and at some smooth point of
R_{\mathrm{am}}
with multiplicity
1
. It follows that the proper transform
L_{p}^{\prime}
of
L_{p}
on
X^{\prime}
satisfies
(L_{p}^{\prime})^{2}=L_{p}^{2}-3=-1
,
L_{p}^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=2
and
L_{p}^{\prime}
meets the proper transform of
R_{\mathrm{am}}
and an exceptional curve over
p
. Hence
L_{p}^{\prime}
is a bubble on
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
; a contradiction with (
7
). This proves (
13
).

Assume first that
D_{\mathrm{am}}
contains a fiber, say
F_{0}
. By formula (
10
)
n=\#(R_{\mathrm{am}}-F_{0})
, so
n\leqslant(R_{\mathrm{am}}-F_{0})\cdot F=3
. Suppose
n=3
. Then
R_{\mathrm{am}}-F_{0}
consists of three positive sections, say
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
. In particular,
C_{i}\cdot C_{j}=2
. Because the components of
R_{\mathrm{am}}
meet in
2n=6
double points, three of them being
F_{0}\cap C_{i}
, the three remaining ones are the points where
C_{i}
is tangent to
C_{j}
,
i\neq j
, with multiplicity
2
. Write
F_{0}\cap C_{3}=\{p\}
and let
\sigma_{p}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be as in Section
2F
. Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
((\sigma_{p})_{*}C_{i})^{2}=C_{i}^{2}+2=4
, so
(\sigma_{p})_{*}C_{i}
are conics tangent to each other at two points with multiplicity
2
, and
((\sigma_{p})_{*}C_{3})^{2}=C_{3}^{2}-1=1
, so
(\sigma_{p})_{*}C_{3}
is a line tangent to those conics outside their common points. This is a contradiction with Lemma
2.6
(a)
.

Consider the case
n=2
. Then
R_{\mathrm{am}}-F_{0}
consists of a positive section
H_{1}
and a curve
H_{2}
of type
(0,2)
. We have
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=4
. By Lemma
2.7
,
H_{2}
has a unique singular point
p
, which is ordinary. Because
R_{\mathrm{am}}
has
4
self-intersection points, if
H_{2}
is nodal then meets
H_{1}
and
F_{0}
in one point each; and if
H_{2}
is cuspidal it meets
F_{0}+H_{1}
in three points, either one, or two of them contained in
F_{0}
. Let
\sigma_{p}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be as in Section
2F
. In case
\#(H_{1}\cap H_{2})=2
put
{l}_{1}=\widehat{{l}}_{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
{l}_{q}=(\sigma_{p})_{*}F_{0}
. Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
{c}_{i}\coloneqq(\sigma_{p})_{*}H_{3-i}
are conics meeting with multiplicities
3
,
1
off
{l}_{1}+{l}_{q}
, and
{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{q}
, and hence
S
, is as in
. Note that if the expansions is at
(C_{2},C_{1};1)
and
(L_{q},C_{2};1)
, then the proper transform of
|C_{2}+3E_{p_{2}}+2E_{p_{2}}^{\prime}+E_{p_{2}}^{\prime\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which restricts to a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of
S
, contrary to assumption (
6
).

Consider the case
\#(H_{1}\cap H_{2})=1
. Put
p_{2}=\widehat{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
{l}_{2}=\widehat{{l}}_{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
{l}_{2}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{p})_{*}F_{0}
. Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
{c}_{i}\coloneqq(\sigma_{p})_{*}H_{i}
are conics meeting with multiplicity
4
off
{l}_{2}+{l}_{2}^{\prime}
, and
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{2}^{\prime}
are lines such that
({l}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{p_{2}}=2
,
({l}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot{c}_{1})_{p_{2}}=1
. By Lemma
2.6
(b)

{l}_{2}
is not tangent to
{c}_{2}
. This means that
H_{2}
is nodal, so it is tangent to
F_{0}
, hence
({l}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot{c}_{2})_{r}=2
for some
r\neq p_{2}
. Thus
{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{2}^{\prime}
, and hence
S
, is as in
.

Consider now the case
n=1
. Then
H\coloneqq R_{\mathrm{am}}-F_{0}
is a curve of type
(0,3)
. Because
R_{\mathrm{am}}
has
2n=2
nodes
H
meets
F_{0}
in
2-\nu
points, where
\nu\leqslant 1
is the number of nodes of
H
. The morphism
\pi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
has degree
3
and is ramified at least at each singularity of
H
, with index at least
2
, and at the point of tangency of
F_{0}
and
H
, with index
2+\nu
. By the Hurwitz formula,
2-\nu\leqslant 3\cdot 2-\#\operatorname{Sing}H-(1+\nu)
, so
\#\operatorname{Sing}H\leqslant 3
.

Lemma
2.7
gives
\delta_{H}=4
, so by condition (
13
)
H
has either two singular points with
\delta=2
or one with
\delta=2
and two ordinary. Choose a point
p\in\operatorname{Sing}H
with
\delta_{p}=2
, a node if possible. Let
\sigma_{p}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be as in Section
2F
,
{l}_{0}\coloneqq(\sigma_{p})_{*}F_{0}
,
{h}\coloneqq(\sigma_{p})_{*}H
and let
p^{\prime}\in{h}
be the point infinitely near to
p
. Then
{l}_{0}
is a line meeting
\widehat{{l}}_{p}
at
\widehat{p}
, and
{h}
is a quartic such that
(\widehat{{l}}_{p}\cdot{h})_{\widehat{p}}=(\widehat{{l}}_{p}\cdot{h})_{p^{\prime}}=2
,
\widehat{p}\in{h}
is smooth and
\delta_{p^{\prime}}=1
.

Assume that
\#\operatorname{Sing}{h}=2
, so
\operatorname{Sing}{h}=\{p^{\prime},q\}
, where
\delta_{q}=2
. The line
{l}_{qp^{\prime}}
joining
q
and
p^{\prime}
meets
{h}
only at
q
,
p^{\prime}
, and the line
{l}_{q}
tangent to
{h}
at
q
meets
{h}
only at
q
, with multiplicity
4
, see Lemma
2.5
(a)
. Consider the quadratic transformation centered at
p^{\prime}
,
q
and its infinitely near point on the proper transform of
{l}_{q}
, that is, blow up these points and contract the proper transforms of
{l}_{qp^{\prime}}
,
{l}_{q}
and the first exceptional curve over
q
. Let
{c}_{1}
,
{c}_{2}
,
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,
{l}
be the images of
{h}
,
\widehat{{l}}_{p}
,
{l}_{0}
and the exceptional curves over
q
and
p^{\prime}
, respectively. Now
{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}
, and hence
S
, is as in
if
p^{\prime}
is a node and as in
if
p^{\prime}
is a cusp; where the line
{l}
correspond to
{l}_{r}
in
and to
{l}_{p^{\prime}}
in
.

Assume now that
{h}
has three (ordinary) singular points
p^{\prime}
,
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
. At most one of them is a node. We order them so that
q_{2}
is a cusp. Consider the standard quadratic transformation centered at
p^{\prime}
,
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
. Let
{c}_{1}
,
{c}_{2}
,
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,

,

be the images of
{h}
,
{l}_{0}
,

, and the exceptional curves over

,
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
, respectively. The lines
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{r}^{\prime}
and
{l}_{pq}
pass through one point (the image of
{l}_{q_{1}q_{2}}
) and
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{r}^{\prime}
are tangent to
{c}_{1}
, so
{l}_{pq}
is not. This means that
q_{1}\in{h}
is a node, so
p^{\prime}\in{h}
is a cusp. It follows that
{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{pq}+{l}_{r}^{\prime}
, and hence
S
, is as in
.

Now consider the case when

for a fiber

, so

. Consider the case

. Then

-sections, say,

of type
(1,1)
and

,

. We have

and

. The divisor

contains

self-intersection points, so by renaming

with

if necessary, we can assume that

meets

with multiplicity

, at, say,

, and

meets

with multiplicities

,

if

is tangent to
H_{1}
and with multiplicity

otherwise. Let

. Note that

,

are conics meeting at

2
;

is tangent to

at

, and

is a line tangent to
(\sigma_{p})_{*}H
at

. If

is tangent to

put

,

,

. Then

, and hence

, is as in
. In the other case, put

,

,

,

. Then
{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{1}^{\prime}
, hence
S
, is as in
. In this case, we can assume that the weight at the preimage of

is not
1
: indeed, otherwise the proper transform of
{l}_{rp_{2}}
on

is a bubble, and contracting it instead of the one over

, we see that

is as in
. Moreover, if both points of
{l}_{1}\cap{c}_{2}
are centers of expansions with weights

, we can, by symmetry of the graph, take

, cf. Example
9.3
.

{l}_{rp^{\prime}}\coloneqq(\sigma^{-1})^{*}(\widehat{p})
,
{l}_{qs}\coloneqq\sigma_{*}\widehat{{l}}_{p}
we see that
S
is as in
. If
p\in H_{2}
was a cusp then
\sigma_{*}\widehat{{l}}_{p}
is tangent to
{c}_{1}
, and putting
{l}_{pp^{\prime}}\coloneqq(\sigma^{-1})^{*}(r)
,
{l}\coloneqq(\sigma^{-1})^{*}(\widehat{p})
and
{l}_{1}\coloneqq\sigma_{*}\widehat{{l}}_{p}
we see that
S
is as in
(in particular,
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{\widehat{r}}=3
).

Eventually, consider the case
n=0
. Then
R_{\mathrm{am}}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a rational cuspidal curve, whose cusps have multiplicity two. By condition (
13
),
\delta
-invariants of those cusps are at most two. Hence
R_{\mathrm{am}}
is as in Proposition
6.2
(a)
below, so
S
is as in
.

6D.
Case
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}

Recall that by formula (
10
),
D_{\min}
has
2n
self intersection points and
n+1
components, all of which are rational by Lemma
3.3
(b)
; and all singularities of
D_{\min}
are of multiplicity
2
by Lemma
3.12
(g)
. By Lemma
3.13
(a)
,
-(2K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})
is ample, so
\deg D_{\min}\leqslant 5
. Suppose
\deg D_{\min}\leqslant 4
. If
\deg D_{\min}=4
then since all components of
D_{\min}
are rational,
D_{\min}
has a singular point
q
; if
\deg D_{\min}\leqslant 3
fix any
q\in D_{\min}
. The projection from
q
restricts to a
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-,
\mathbb{C}^{*}
- or a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus D_{\min}
, contrary to condition (
11
).

Thus
\deg D_{\min}=5
. We note that

(14)

\mbox{if }p\in\operatorname{Sing}D_{\min}\mbox{ is not ordinary (i.e.\ $\delta_{p}>1$) then }p\mbox{ lies on a line in }D_{\min}.

Indeed, otherwise by Lemma
2.3
the line
{l}_{p}
tangent to
D_{\min}
at
p
satisfies
({l}_{p}\cdot D_{\min})_{p}=4
, so its proper transform is a bubble on
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, contrary to condition (
7
). It follows that

(15)

\mbox{exactly one component of }D_{\min}\mbox{ is not a line.}

Indeed, if
D_{\min}
has two components
{c}
,
{c}^{\prime}
which are not lines then by condition (
14
)
{c}
meets
{c}^{\prime}
in
{c}\cdot{c}^{\prime}\geqslant 4
points off
D_{\min}-{c}-{c}^{\prime}
. Since
\#D_{\min}=n+1
and
D_{\min}
has
2n
nodes, we get
2n\geqslant\#D_{\min}-2+{c}\cdot{c}^{\prime}\geqslant n+3
, hence
4\leqslant\#D_{\min}\leqslant\deg(D_{\min}-{c}-{c}^{\prime})+2\leqslant 3
, which is impossible. On the other hand, if
D_{\min}
is a sum of lines then
n+1=\#D_{\min}=\deg D_{\min}=5
, and
D_{\min}
has
10=2n
nodes, which is false.

In particular, condition (
15
) implies
n\leqslant 3
. Suppose
n=0
. Then
D_{\min}
is a rational cuspidal quintic, so
\#\operatorname{Sing}D_{\min}=6
by condition (
14
) and Lemma
2.5
(b)
. The projection from a cusp
p\in D_{\min}
induces a map
(\pi_{p}^{-1})_{*}D_{\min}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
3
, ramified at the preimages of the five remaining cusps; a contradiction with the Hurwitz formula. Thus
n\in\{1,2,3\}
.
We use Notation
4.1
for curves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and their proper transforms on
X^{\prime}
.

Consider the case

. Then
D_{\min}={c}_{1}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}

{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
as in

. Write
\{p_{i}\}={c}_{1}\cap{l}_{i}
for

. If the expansion
\psi
has a center at a preimage of
{l}_{i}\cap{l}_{j}
for some
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
, then the corresponding weight cannot be equal to one. Indeed, otherwise the pencil of lines through
{l}_{i}\cap{l}_{j}
restricts to a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of

, which is impossible by assumption (
6
).

We will now use the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},D_{\min})\cong S_{3}
to prove that the centers of
\psi
, say
p,q,r
, are as in Table
8
. For
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
define
\tau_{ij}\in\operatorname{Aut}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
by
\tau_{ij}(L_{i})=L_{j}
,
\tau_{ij}(L_{k})=L_{k}
.

Put
T=L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3}
. Since
T
is circular, one of the centers of
\psi
is a node of
T
, say
\{p\}=L_{1}\cap L_{2}
. More precisely, the first center is
(L_{1},L_{2};u)
for some
u\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\setminus\{1\}
. Assume that
q,r\not\in\operatorname{Sing}T
, so
q\in E_{p_{i}}
,
r\in E_{p_{j}}
for some
\{i,j\}\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
, see Figure
17
. Since
D
is connected, we have
i\neq j
, so, say,
i<j
. Using the action of
\tau_{12}
, we can assume that
i=1
. Furthermore, if
j=2
then using
\tau_{12}
again we can assume that
u>1
if
q,r\in C_{1}
or
q,r\not\in C_{1}
; and
q\in C_{1}
,
r\not\in C_{1}
otherwise.

Assume now that
q
is a node of
T
. Using
\tau_{12}
we can assume that
\{q\}=L_{2}\cap L_{3}
. If
r\not\in\operatorname{Sing}T
then since
D
is connected, we have
r\in E_{p_{1}}\cup E_{p_{3}}
, and using
\tau_{13}
we can assume
r\in E_{p_{1}}
. Assume
r\in\operatorname{Sing}T
, so
\{r\}=L_{1}\cap L_{3}
. Then the expansion
\psi
is centered at
(L_{1},L_{2};u)
,
(L_{2},L_{3};v)
,
(L_{3},L_{1};w)
for some
u,v,w\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\setminus\{1\}
. The action of
\tau_{12}
replaces the triple
(u,v,w)
by
(\frac{1}{u},\frac{1}{w},\frac{1}{v})
, so we can assume that, say,
u,v>1
. Using the
3
-cycle in
\operatorname{Aut}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\cong S_{3}
, we can assume that
u\geqslant v,w
, as needed.

.
Cases when

.

Consider the case
n=1
. Then by condition (
15
),

consists of a line
{l}
and a rational quartic
{q}
with

2.5
(b)
and condition (
14
) imply that
{q}
has three singular points. Let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a standard quadratic transformation centered at
\operatorname{Sing}{q}
. The total transform of
\operatorname{Sing}{q}
is a triangle inscribed in a conic
\sigma_{*}{l}
and the conic
\sigma_{*}{q}
is tangent to two of its sides if
{q}
is nodal and three if
{q}

, so

is as in
. In the second case, write
{l}\cap{q}=\{p,q\}
for some

. If

,

then
|(\pi_{p})^{-1}_{*}{l}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which restricts to a morphism
(\pi_{p})^{-1}_{*}{q}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
4
, ramified at thee cusps of
(\pi_{p})^{-1}_{*}{q}
and, with index
3
, at
\pi_{p}^{-1}(q)
, which is impossible by the Hurwitz formula. Hence
({l}\cdot{q})_{p}=({l}\cdot{q})_{q}=2
and
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is as in
2
. Using
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
as above we can assume that the center of
\psi^{\prime}
lies on
E_{q_{1}}
.

6E.
Sporadic smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
in case
n=0
, omitted in Sections
6B
and
6C
. This amounts to describe a very specific class of rational curves on the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Recall from Section
2F
that we denote by
\Xi_{2}
the negative section on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, and we say that a divisor
C\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}
is
of type

(a,b)
if
C\cdot\Xi_{2}=a
and
C\cdot F=b
for a fiber
F
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
R\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}
be a rational curve of type
(1,3)
, such that all cusps of
R
have multiplicity
2
, and denote by
\boldsymbol{\delta}
the sequence of their
\delta
-invariants, written in a non-increasing order.

(a)

Assume that all singular points of
R
are cusps with
\delta
-invariants at most
2
. Then such a curve
R
exists, is unique up to an action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, and has
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(2,2,1,1)
. The surface
S\coloneqq\mathbb{F}_{2}\setminus(R+\Xi_{2})
is as in
.

(b)

Assume that
R
has an ordinary node
q
, with one branch tangent to a fiber
F_{q}
; and all the singular points of
R\setminus\{q\}
are cusps. Let
\varphi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
be blowup at
q
and at its infinitely near point on the proper transform of
F_{q}
; let
E\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
be the
(-1)
-curve, and let
S=Y\setminus(\pi^{*}(R+\Xi_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}-E)
. Then
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(5),(4,1),(3,2)
or
(3,1,1)
. The surface
S
is as in
,
1
,
or
, respectively. Up to the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, there are exactly
3
such curves
R
if
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(5),(4,1)
or
(3,2)
; and
1
if
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(3,1,1)
.

In both cases, for each cusp
r\in R
the fiber passing through
r
satisfies
(F_{r}\cdot R)_{r}=2
and
F_{r}\cap R\cap\Xi_{2}=\emptyset
.

By Lemma
2.7
, we have
\delta_{R}=6
. In case
(a)
this implies that
R
has at least three cusps.

For
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R
denote by
F_{r}
the fiber passing through
r
. Since
F_{r}\cdot R=3
, we have
F_{r}\neq F_{r^{\prime}}
for
r\neq r^{\prime}
. Put
\eta_{r}=1
if
F_{r}
passes through
R\cap\Xi_{2}
and
\eta_{r}=0
otherwise. If
r\in R
is a cusp and
F_{r}
is tangent to
R
at
r
, put
\varepsilon_{r}=1
, otherwise put
\varepsilon_{r}=0
. Note that since
R\cdot F_{r}=3
, if
\varepsilon_{r}=1
then
r\in R
is ordinary. Put
\varepsilon=\sum_{r}\varepsilon_{r}
. Applying the Hurwitz formula to
\pi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|_{R}\colon R\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
we infer that in case
(a)

\varepsilon=0
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(2,2,1,1)
or
(2,2,2)
; and in case
(b)

R
has at most
3-\varepsilon
cusps, so either
\varepsilon=0
or
\varepsilon=1
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(4,1)
.

Fix a cusp
p\in R
with maximal
\delta
-invariant. Then
\delta_{p}\geqslant 2
, so
\varepsilon_{p}=0
. Blow up over each singular point of
R
until the proper transform of
R
is smooth, and contract the exceptional
(-1)
-curve over
p
. This map is a composition of
\delta_{R}-1=5
blowups over points of multiplicity
2
on
R\setminus\Xi_{2}
. Next, contract the proper transforms of the fibers passing through
\operatorname{Sing}R
and the
(-1)
-curves appearing in subsequent total transforms of
R
. Each contracted curve meets the proper transforms of
R
and
\Xi_{2}
once each. Denote the resulting map by
\vartheta
. We have
\rho(\vartheta(\mathbb{F}_{2}))=2
, so
\vartheta(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
for some
m\geqslant 0
. We have
(\vartheta_{*}R)^{2}=R^{2}-20+5=9
and
(\vartheta_{*}\Xi_{2})^{2}=\Xi_{2}^{2}+5=3
. Like on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the curves
\vartheta_{*}R
,
\vartheta_{*}\Xi_{2}
are a
3
-section and a
1
-section, respectively. Numerical properties of
\mathbb{F}_{m}
imply that
m=1
and
\vartheta_{*}R
,
\vartheta_{*}\Xi_{2}
are of types
(0,3)
and
(1,1)
, respectively.

Contract the negative section of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
and denote its image by
u\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Let
{r},{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
the images of
\vartheta_{*}R
,
\vartheta_{*}\Xi_{2}
. For each
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R
let
{l}_{r}
be the image of the exceptional
(-1)
-curve over
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R
, and let
\widehat{r},r^{\prime}\in{l}_{r}
be the image of
F_{r}
and the point infinitely near to
r
, respectively. Note that
\widehat{r}=r^{\prime}
if
\varepsilon_{r}=1
and
\widehat{r}\neq r^{\prime}
otherwise. For a cusp
r\neq p
, we have
({r}\cdot{l}_{r})_{r^{\prime}}=2+\varepsilon_{r}
,
({r}\cdot{l}_{r})_{\widehat{r}}=1
and
({r}\cdot{c})_{\widehat{r}}=\delta_{r}+\eta_{r}
. For the chosen cusp
p
, the point
p^{\prime}\in{r}
is an ordinary cusp, and since
\varepsilon_{p}=0
, we have
({r}\cdot{l}_{p})_{p^{\prime}}=2
,
({r}\cdot{l}_{r})_{\widehat{p}}=1
and
({r}\cdot{c})_{\widehat{p}}=\delta_{p}+\eta_{p}-1
. Eventually, for the node
q\in R
in case
(b)
, we have
({r}\cdot{l}_{q})_{q^{\prime}}=1
,
({r}\cdot{l}_{q})_{\widehat{q}}=2
and
({r}\cdot{c})_{\widehat{q}}=1
.

The curve
{r}
is a cuspidal cubic not passing through
u
. The curve
{c}
is a conic such that for every
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R
, we have
{c}\cap{l}_{r}=\{\widehat{r},u\}
and
\widehat{r}\neq u
. Moreover,
{r}\cdot({c}\setminus\bigcup_{r}{l}_{r})\leqslant 1
, with equality if and only if
\eta_{r}=0
for every
r
.

.

Consider the case

and

,

In the remaining cases, let

∎

Remark 6.3
.

The curve
R
from Proposition
6.2
(a)
is a new example of a rational cuspidal curve on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
with four cusps. Some other examples were found in
[
Moe14
]
. Conjecture 1.2 loc. cit. asserts that a rational cuspidal curve on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
cannot have more than four cusps; the same holds for curves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
by
[
KP22
]
.

The above construction allows one to compute
the explicit equation of
R\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}
, or equivalently, of its image
\overline{R}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
. In some coordinates
[x:y:z]
of weights
1,1,2
, this curve is given by

\begin{split}\{0=&\ 2304\ x^{5}y^{2}+324\ x^{4}y^{3}-7680\ x^{4}yz+1980\ x^{3}y^{2}z+360\ x^{2}y^{3}z+\\
+&\ 6400\ x^{3}z^{2}-6900\ x^{2}yz^{2}+300\ xy^{2}z^{2}+100\ y^{3}z^{2}+4500\ xz^{3}-1125\ yz^{3}\},\end{split}

has cusps with
\delta=2
at
[0:1:0]
and
[1:0:0]
; and ordinary ones at

\left[-\tfrac{61}{256}+\tfrac{5}{256}\sqrt{-15}:1:-\tfrac{79}{1280}+\tfrac{53}{3840}\sqrt{-15}\right]\mbox{ and }\left[-\tfrac{61}{256}-\tfrac{5}{256}\sqrt{-15}:1:-\tfrac{79}{1280}-\tfrac{53}{3840}\sqrt{-15}\right].

This computation was kindly provided to us by Torguun K. Moe.

We remark that the above equation of
\overline{R}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
has rational coefficients, so the complement
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)\setminus\overline{R}
, i.e. the surface
, is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. This fact can also be seen from an alternative construction of the curve
R
, given in Example
9.6
, see Remark
9.7
. There, we construct an involution
\widehat{\iota}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},R)
such that
\widehat{\iota}(p_{1})=p_{2}
,
\widehat{\iota}(p_{1}^{\prime})=p_{2}^{\prime}
, where
p_{i},p_{i}^{\prime}\in R
are cusps with
\delta_{p_{i}}=2
,
\delta_{p_{i}^{\prime}}=1
,
i\in\{1,2\}
.

7.
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes with
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibrations

In Section
6
we have classified
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes of log general type which satisfy the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
and admit no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration, see assumption (
6
). Here we go back to
\mathbb{Q}
HPs with
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibrations. They are much easier to understand. In fact, as we have remarked in the introduction, they are classified in
[
MS91
, Theorem 1]
(to be precise, loc. cit. is formulated for
\mathbb{Z}
HPs, but the proof works for
\mathbb{Q}
HPs as well, the only difference is that one has to include types
(UC_{2-1})^{\prime}
and
(T3C_{2})
in the statement). It was observed in
[
Sug99
]
that the proof can be much simplified by means of Lemma
2.3
, which was not available at that time. In order to deduce Theorem
1.6
from loc. cit, we need to slightly adjust this result: for example, we are interested in the minimal log smooth completions, while loc. cit. considers those completions for which some
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration extends to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration with disjoint sections in the boundary. Hence for the convenience of the reader, we give a self-contained argument below; of course relying on Lemma
2.3
.

The proof is organized as follows. In Lemma
7.1
we complete some
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of
S
to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
and describe degenerate fibers of the latter. While doing it, we introduce some notation which will be used throughout in the proof. In Lemma
7.2
we gather some useful formulas relating e.g. the number of fibers of each type with the shape of the horizontal part
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
of
D
. Eventually, in Proposition
7.3
we study each possible
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
and classify the corresponding
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
S
be a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type, and let
(X,D)
be its minimal log smooth completion. Assume that
S
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration. Then the following holds.

(a)

There is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\overline{p}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
which restricts to a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of
S
.

(b)

Contract a vertical bubble in
(X,D)
, and all vertical
(-1)
-curves which are superfluous in the subsequent images of
D
. Repeat this process until the resulting pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
has no vertical bubbles; and
D^{\prime}
has no vertical superfluous
(-1)
-curves. Then the resulting morphism
\psi^{\prime}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is an expansion.

(c)

There is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\overline{p}^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that
\overline{p}=\overline{p}^{\prime}\circ\psi^{\prime}
. We have
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=\psi^{\prime}(D_{\mathrm{hor}})
and
\#D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}
.

(d)

Let
F^{\circ}
be a degenerate fiber of
\overline{p}^{\prime}|_{X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}}
. Then
F^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
. The closure
\overline{F}^{\circ}
is a nondegenerate fiber of
\overline{p}^{\prime}
.

(e)

Let
F
be a degenerate fiber of
\overline{p}^{\prime}
. Then
F\subseteq D^{\prime}
and one of the following holds:

(
1)

F_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
and
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets the first and the second component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
,

(
2)

F_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]
and
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
3
-section meeting
F_{\mathrm{red}}
only in the
(-1)
-curve.

Part
(a)
is proved in
[
PP17
, Proposition 3.3]
. Part
(b)
follows from Lemma
3.2
. Part
(c)
is a direct consequence of the definition of
\psi^{\prime}
. Part
(d)
follows from
(e)
: indeed, since
\overline{F}^{\circ}\not\subseteq D^{\prime}
, part
(e)
implies that
\overline{F}^{\circ}
is nondegenerate. In particular
F^{\circ}
is irreducible, so
F^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
by
[
PP17
, Corollary 2.8(a)]
.

It remains to prove part
(e)
. Let
L\subseteq F
be a
(-1)
-curve. We claim that
\beta_{(D^{\prime}+L)_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)\geqslant 3
. Suppose the contrary. If
L\subseteq D^{\prime}
then
L
is a superfluous, vertical
(-1)
-curve in
D
, which is impossible. Hence
L\not\subseteq D^{\prime}
and
L\cdot D^{\prime}\leqslant 2
. Lemma
2.3
gives
L\cdot D^{\prime}=2
. Because
L
is not a bubble, it meets exactly one component of
D^{\prime}
, say
H
. Since the fiber
F
is a rational tree, we have
H\not\subseteq F
, so
H\subseteq D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. It follows that
L
meets a component
L^{\prime}
of
F
such that
L^{\prime}\not\subseteq D^{\prime}
. Since
D^{\prime}
is connected, each point of
L^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime}
lies in
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
or in a connected component of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}}
meeting
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Hence
\#(L^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime})\leqslant(F-L)\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
; a contradiction with Lemma
2.3
.

Thus
\beta_{(D^{\prime}+L)_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)\geqslant 3
. Since
\beta_{F_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)\leqslant 2
, it follows that
L
meets
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Let
\mu
be the multiplicity of
L
in
F
. If
\mu=1
then
L
is a tip of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, so
L\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2
. In any case, we have
2\leqslant\mu L\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant F\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=3
. It follows that
L
is a unique
(-1)
-curve in
F
. In particular,
\mu>1
. Thus the above inequality gives
\mu\in\{2,3\}
and
L\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
, so
\beta_{F_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)\geqslant\beta_{(D^{\prime}+L)_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)-L\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2
. It follows that
\beta_{F_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)=2
and both components of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
meeting
L
are contained in
D^{\prime}
. Because
(F-\mu L)\cdot D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant 1
, at most one of the connected components of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L
meets
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Since
D^{\prime}
is connected, it follows that
L\subseteq D^{\prime}
. Now if
\mu=2
then
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
, so
(e)(e.1)
holds. If
\mu=3
then
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]
,
F
meets
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
only in
L
, and all components of
F
except possibly the
(-2)
-tip are contained in
D^{\prime}
. Lemma
2.3
implies that
F\subseteq D^{\prime}
, so
(e)(e.2)
holds, as claimed.

∎

Lemma 7.2
.

We keep notation from Lemma
7.1
. Furthermore, let
n
be the number of centers of the expansion
\psi^{\prime}
. Let
n_{h}
be the number of nodes of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Let
\nu^{\prime}
be the number of degenerate fibers of
p^{\prime}\coloneqq\overline{p}^{\prime}|_{X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}}
, see Lemma
7.1
(d)
. Let
\nu_{2}
,
\nu_{3}
be the number of fibers as in Lemma
7.1
(e)(e.1)
,
(e)(e.2)
, respectively; and let
\nu_{1}
be the number of nondegenerate fibers contained in
D^{\prime}
. Then the following holds.

(a)

Exactly
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}
fibers of
p^{\prime}
are tangent to some multi-section
H\subseteq D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Every such fiber is tangent to
H
in a unique point, with multiplicity
2
.

(b)

0\leqslant\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}=6-2\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-2\nu_{3}-\nu_{2}
.

(c)

n=3\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}+2\nu_{3}+\nu_{2}-n_{h}-5
.

(d)

\nu_{1}-\nu_{3}+n_{h}-2\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}+3=0
.

(e)

\nu_{3}\leqslant 1
.

(a)
Let
F
be a closure of a degenerate fiber of
p^{\prime}
. By Lemma
7.1
(d)
,
F
is a
0
-curve not contained in
D^{\prime}
. We have
\#(F\cap D^{\prime})\leqslant F\cdot D^{\prime}-1=2
, so
\#(F\cap D^{\prime})=2
by Lemma
2.3
. Thus
F
meets
D^{\prime}
in two points, with multiplicity
1
and
2
. In particular,
F
passes through at most one node of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Since
D^{\prime}
is snc, every node lies on a fiber of this type, so there are
n_{h}
such fibers. The remaining
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}
degenerate fibers of
p^{\prime}
are therefore tangent to some multi-section in
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
; as needed.

(b)
The inequality
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}\geqslant 0
follows from
(a)
. If
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
1
-sections then
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=\#D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=3
,
\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}=0
, and
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}=0
by
(a)
, so
(b)
holds. Assume that
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
contains a
2
-section
H
. Then
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=2
and
\nu_{3}=0
. The restriction
\overline{p}|_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a morphism of degree
2
, ramified with index
2
on each fiber of
p^{\prime}
tangent to
H
; and on each fiber of type
[2,1,2]
. Thus by
(a)
,
\overline{p}|_{H}
has
(\nu^{\prime}-n_{h})+\nu_{2}
ramification points. By Hurwitz formula,
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}+\nu_{2}=2
, which proves
(b)
in this case. Eventually, assume that
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
3
-section. Then
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
and
\nu_{2}=2
. The restriction
\overline{p}|_{D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}}\colon D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a morphism of degree
3
. It is ramified with index
2
at
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}
points on fibers as in
(a)
; and with index
3
at
\nu_{3}
points on fibers of type
[3,1,2,2]
. By Hurwitz formula, we get
\nu^{\prime}-n_{h}+2\nu_{3}=4
, as needed.

(c)
By
[
PP17
, Corollary 2.8]
, the sum of all degenerate fibers of
p
is a sum of
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}+1
curves isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{*}
. If such a curve is contracted by
\psi^{\prime}
then it is a vertical bubble; otherwise it is a proper transform of a degenerate fiber of
p^{\prime}
. Thus
n+\nu^{\prime}=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}+1
. Substituting the formula for
\nu^{\prime}
from
(b)
, we get
(c)
.

(d)
Choose a contraction
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{m},D^{\prime\prime})
of degenerate fibers which does not introduce new nodes in
D^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Then
D^{\prime\prime}
has
3\nu_{1}+2\nu_{2}+\nu_{3}+n_{h}
nodes. On the other hand, since
D
is a tree, the properties of expansions imply that the number of nodes in
D^{\prime\prime}
equals
\#D^{\prime\prime}-1+n
. We have
\#D^{\prime\prime}=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+\nu_{3}+\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Hence
2\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+n_{h}=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-1+n
. Substituting the formula for
n
from
(c)
, we get the required equality.

(e)
A fiber
F\subseteq D^{\prime}
as in Lemma
7.1
(e)(e.2)
meets
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
in one point. Connectedness of
D
implies that
F
does not contain a center of
\psi^{\prime}
. Therefore, the preimage of
F
is a fiber contained in
D
. Because components of
D
are numerically independent,
D
contains at most one fiber, so
\nu_{3}\leqslant 1
, as claimed.

∎

Proposition 7.3

(cf.
[
MS91
]
)
.

Let
S
be a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type. If
S
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
fibration then
S
is obtained by tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm from data listed in Table
, see Figure
22
.

We keep the notation introduced in Lemmas
7.1
,
7.2
.

Assume first that
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of three
1
-sections. Then
X^{\prime}
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
for some
m\geqslant 0
; and
\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}=0
. By Lemma
7.2
(d)
, we have
n_{h}=3-\nu_{1}
. In particular,
n_{h}\leqslant 3
. If
n_{h}=3
then
\nu_{1}=0
, but since the centers of expansion lie on the fibers in
D^{\prime}
, we get
n=0
, contrary to Lemma
7.2
(c)
.

Consider the case
n_{h}=2
. Numerical properties of Hirzebruch surfaces, together with the fact that
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
is snc, imply that
m\in\{0,2\}
. Suppose
m=0
, i.e.
X^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a diagonal and two horizontal lines, i.e. one curve of type
(1,1)
and two of type
(0,1)
. Since
\nu_{1}=3-n_{h}=1
, each horizontal line meets
D^{\prime}
twice. Hence the second projection
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
restricts to a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibration of
X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
; a contradiction with the Iitaka easy addition theorem.

Thus
m=2
. Now,
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of the negative section and two positive ones. As before,
\nu_{1}=1
. By Lemma
7.2
(c)
, we have
n=2
. The centers of the expansion
\psi^{\prime}
are either two nodes of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
, or one node of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
and one common point of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
and the fiber in
D^{\prime}
. In each case, the pencil of positive sections passing through those centers restricts to a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration of
X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
, and pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\widetilde{p}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By the Iitaka easy addition theorem, the restriction
\widetilde{p}|_{S}
is a
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration, too. The horizontal part of
D
with respect to
\widetilde{p}
consists of three
1
-sections, namely: the first exceptional curves over each center of
\psi^{\prime}
; and the proper transform of the fiber
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}}
or of one of the positive sections in
D^{\prime}
. We now replace
\overline{p}
with
\widetilde{p}
, and choose
\psi^{\prime}
in such a way that the number of nodes in
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
and
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
is the same. This way, we reduce the proof to the case
n_{h}\in\{0,1\}
.

Consider the case
n_{h}=0
. Then
m=0
and
D^{\prime}
is a union of three vertical and three horizontal lines. This case covers types
[
MS91
,
(UP_{3-1})
,
(UC_{2-1})

(UC_{2-1})^{\prime}
]
, see pp. 13-18 loc. cit. We claim that
S
is as in
.

Let
p
,

be two centers of
\psi^{\prime}
which do not lie on the same component of
D^{\prime}
. Then the weight corresponding to, say,
p
, is different than
1
. Indeed, otherwise a proper transform on
X
of the pencil of curves of type
(1,1)
passing through

and

would induce a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibration of an open subset of
S
, which is impossible since
\kappa(S)=2
. Let
\pi_{p}\colon\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a blowup at

and let
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\pi_{p}^{*}D^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then

is a minimal log resolution of
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
as in
, and
\psi^{\prime}=\pi_{p}\circ\psi
for some expansion
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\widetilde{X}^{\prime},\widetilde{D}^{\prime})
. Using the action of

we can arrange the centers of

to be as in
. Note that if one of the centers is

for some

-curve in

The weights

and

are explicitly excluded in Table

because they correspond to the Fujita surfaces

and

[

.

Consider now the case
n_{h}=1
. Then

and

consists of the negative section

and two sections of type

. Let

-fibration of some open subset of

, which is impossible. Similarly, if two weights are
(2,\tfrac{1}{2})
then so does a pencil of conics tangent to the corresponding lines.

By symmetry, we can assume that the centers of
\psi
are
({l}_{1},{l}_{2})

Assume now that

∎

8.
Construction of planar divisors

The results obtained so far allow us to conclude that all
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes of log general type satisfying the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
are obtained by the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm from certain arrangements of lines and conics. Combinatorial types of the latter are listed in Tables
–
. In this section, we construct arrangements realizing each combinatorial type, and count their number. The latter computation, summarized in Proposition
8.3
below, will be important in the proof of Corollary
1.7
(c)
. Indeed, as we will see in Section
9
, different (marked) arrangements of the same type will yield different
\mathbb{Q}
HPs with the same weighted boundary graph (with the exception of tower
, discussed in Examples
4.4
and
4.6
).

To state Proposition
8.3
, we recall technical definitions of a combinatorial type and a marking. Let
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be an arrangement (also called: a configuration) of lines and conics. The
combinatorial type
of
{p}
, see
[
ABCT08
, Remark 3]
, is a pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S})
, where
\mathcal{C}
is the set of components of
{p}
, each decorated by its degree (either
1
or
2
); and
\mathcal{S}
is the set of singular points of
{p}
, each decorated with the following data: a list of components meeting at that point, and for each pair of such components, their local intersection number. Note that since all components of
{p}
are smooth, a decoration of a point
q\in\mathcal{S}
encodes the topological type of
{p}
at
q
. We identify two combinatorial types
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S})
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
if there are bijections
\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}\cong\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
which preserve decorations. This way, projectively equivalent configurations have the same combinatorial type.

A
marking
on a configuration
{p}
is the choice of an order on each maximal subset of
\mathcal{S}
whose elements have the same decorations. We say that
marked
configurations
{p},{p}^{\prime}
are projectively equivalent if there is an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
which maps
{p}
to
{p}^{\prime}
and preserves the marking. The reader might have in mind the following simple example, which will often appear as a sub-configuration of the ones constructed below.

Example 8.1
.

Let
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{3}
be non-concurrent lines, let
{c}
be a conic tangent to each of them, and let
{p}_{0}={c}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
. The configuration
{p}_{0}
admits a unique (trivial) marking; and is uniquely determined, up to a projective equivalence, by its combinatorial type. Let now
{l}
be a line meeting
{p}_{0}
normally. The configuration
{p}\coloneqq{p}_{0}+{l}
admits two markings, corresponding to the two possible orders on the set
{c}\cap{l}
. The two marked configurations are projectively equivalent if and only if those two points lie in the same orbit of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
: this happens if and only if the pair
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}_{0})
admits an involution fixing
{l}
.

We say that a configuration
{p}
is
realized
over a number field
{k}
if in some coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the equation defining
{p}
has coefficients in
{k}
; and all singular points of
{p}
have coordinates in
{k}
. In Example
8.1
, the configuration
{p}_{0}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}
: to see this, one can use e.g. coordinates shown in Figure
23
below. If in these coordinates the equation of
{l}
has rational coefficients, too, then the configuration
{p}_{0}+{l}
is realized over a minimal extension
{k}
of
\mathbb{Q}
containing coordinates of the points in
{c}\cap{l}
. In case
{k}\neq\mathbb{Q}
, the field
{k}
is a quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
, and the points in
{c}\cap{l}
are conjugate by
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{Q}}({k})
.

Each Configuration
1
–
19
below specifies a combinatorial type of an arrangement of lines and conics. These types are then referred to in Tables
–
(note that some of them, e.g. Configuration
5
, are quoted multiple times, but each time we refer to a type of a different sub-arrangement). We introduce the following notation.

Notation 8.2
.

For a row
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
of Tables
–
we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
the set of projective equivalence classes of marked configurations whose combinatorial type is specified in
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
. Moreover, we denote by
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
the number in the column
“
{N}
”
, and by
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
the number field in the column
“
{k}
”
.

Recall that Corollaries
1.7
(c)
and
1.9
assert that
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is the number of isomorphism classes of
\mathbb{Q}
HPs in tower
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
sharing the same boundary graph; and
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is a field over which all
\mathbb{Q}
HPs in
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
are defined. To prove this assertion, we first relate
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
and
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
with the corresponding planar configurations, as follows.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
be a row of Tables
–
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
,
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
,
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
be as in Notation
8.2
.

(a)

We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}={N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
if
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}\neq

; and
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}}=2
.

(b)

Every configuration in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is realized over
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
if
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}\neq
. The configuration
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2n0}}}}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-15})
.

(c)

Every two configurations in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
are conjugate by some element of the Galois group of
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
.

(d)

Assume that
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}\geqslant 2
and
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}\neq

,
,
,
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
contains a pair of complex-conjugate configurations. If
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}=4
then
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
consists of two pairs of complex-conjugate configurations.

By Definition
1.5
of the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, each surface
S
in Theorem
1.6
is obtained by taking a minimal log resolution of some
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
, followed by an expansion. Thus if a configuration
{p}
is realized over
{k}
, then the surface
S
is defined over
{k}
. Hence for every
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}\neq

, Corollary
1.9
follows from Proposition
8.3
(b)
. It remains to see that the surface
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
: this is proved in Remark
6.3
. An alternative argument (relying on fewer computations) is given in Remark
9.8
.

∎

Remark 8.4

(candidate Zariski pairs, cf. Remark
9.14
)
.

Consider tower
, constructed from Configuration
5
, see Figure
28
. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_C2C3-node}}}}
consists of four classes of marked configurations. Viewing them as unmarked ones, we get two non-projectively equivalent configurations of the same combinatorial type. As we will see below, the field automorphism from Proposition
8.3
which conjugates them is the generator of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5}))
, so it does not extend to a complex conjugation.

It remains an open question whether there exists a homeomorphism
\mathbb{P}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
which maps one of those configurations to the other. This makes the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_C2C3-node}}}}
a candidate for an
algebraic Zariski pair
, see
[
ABCC03
, Definition 8.2]
for a definition and
[
ABCT08
]
for a survey. Note that the complements of both configurations in
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_q-cn_31}}}}
have the same algebraic fundamental groups, see
[
AM69
, Corollary 12.11]
.

A similar phenomenon occurs in triples
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=2_32}}}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=2_41}}}}
, see Figures
26
and
28
. In each of those cases, the three configurations are not projectively equivalent even viewed as unmarked ones (in fact, their markings are trivial). Each of those triples consists of one configuration realized over
\mathbb{R}
, and a pair of complex-conjugate ones. Modding out by complex conjugation, we get candidates for algebraic Zariski pairs.

Some examples of Zariski pairs of degree
7
conic-line arrangements were recently given in
[
Tok14
,
AS+23
]
.
Like in our setting, one can use them to construct towers of affine surfaces. The main difference is that those affine surfaces will have topological Euler characteristic at least
3
: this can be seen from the combinatorics of those arrangements by the same argument as in Lemma
3.3
(c)
.

We will now construct all our configurations, proving Proposition
8.3
along the way.

From now on, by a
conic

{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
we mean a
smooth
curve of degree two. By a
pencil of conics
we mean a line in the linear system
|{c}|
, so its degenerate members are not conics in our sense. In all configurations, we keep Notation
8.5
. As in Notation
4.1
, for two points
a,b\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
we denote by
{l}_{ab}
the line joining them. Moreover, for each configuration we will introduce additional notation, which will
not
be consistent throughout the section.

Notation 8.5

(Figure
23
)
.

{k}
. This means that all configurations in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
are realized over
{k}
, and any two of them are conjugate by some element of the Galois group of
{k}
, see Proposition
8.3
(b)
,
(c)
.

We denote by
\omega
a primitive third root of unity.

Configuration 1

(Figure
26
)
.

Put
p_{3}=p^{\prime}
,
{l}_{3}={l}_{p^{\prime}}
.
Write
{l}_{3}\cap{l}_{1}=\{q_{2}\}
,
{l}_{3}\cap{l}_{2}=\{q_{1}\}
,
q_{3}=p
. Fix a point
r\in{c}_{1}\setminus\bigcup_{i}{l}_{p_{i}q_{i}}
and write
{c}_{1}\cap{l}_{rq_{i}}=\{r,q_{i}^{\prime}\}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. For a fixed
r
, define conics
{c}_{2}
,
{c}_{3}
as follows.

•

{c}_{2}
is the unique conic passing through
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}
.

•

{c}_{3}
is the unique conic passing through
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},r
and tangent to
{c}_{1}
at some point
s_{2}
.

We have
q_{3}^{\prime}\not\in{c}_{2}
and
({c}_{3}\cdot{c}_{2})_{s_{2}}=2
. Write
{c}_{3}\cap{c}_{1}=\{r,s_{2},s_{1}\}
. Up to the action of
\tau
, there is a unique point
r\in{c}_{1}
such that
s_{1}\in{c}_{2}
. For such
r
, we have
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{1}}=1
.

As a consequence,
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2n0}}}}=1
. The configuration
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2n0}}}}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-15})
.

Parametrize
{c}_{1}
by
\nu\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\ni[u:v]\mapsto[u^{2}:uv:v^{2}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
. We have
{l}_{p_{1}q_{1}}=\{2y=z\}
, so
{l}_{p_{1}q_{1}}
meets
{c}_{1}
at
p_{1}=\nu[1:0]
and at
\nu[1:2]
. Similarly,
{l}_{p_{2}q_{2}}=\{2y=x\}
meets
{c}_{1}
at
p_{2}=\nu[0:1]
and at
\nu[2:1]
. Eventually,
{l}_{p_{3}q_{3}}=\{x=z\}
meets
{c}_{1}
at
p_{3}=\nu[1:1]
and
\nu[1:-1]
. Thus
r=\nu[t:1]=[t:1:t^{-1}]
for some
t\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1,-1,2,\frac{1}{2}\}
. We compute

\displaystyle{l}_{rq_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{2y=z+(2-t^{-1})t^{-1}x\},\qquad

\displaystyle{l}_{rq_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{2y=x+(2-t)tz\},

\displaystyle{l}_{rq_{3}}

\displaystyle=\{t^{-1}x=tz\},

\displaystyle q_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=[(2-t^{-1})^{-1}:1:2-t^{-1}],

\displaystyle q_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=[2-t:1:(2-t)^{-1}],

\displaystyle q_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=[t:-1:t^{-1}].

Therefore,
{l}_{q_{1}^{\prime}q_{2}^{\prime}}=\{2(t^{2}-3t+1)y=(1-2t)x+(t-2)tz\}
. The pencil of conics passing through
q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}
is generated by
{l}_{rq_{1}}+{l}_{rq_{2}}
and
{l}_{q_{1}q_{2}}+{l}_{q_{1}^{\prime}q_{2}^{\prime}}
. It has a unique member passing through
q_{3}=[0:1:0]
, namely:

\begin{split}{c}_{2}=\{&(t^{2}-3t+1)(z+(2-t^{-1})t^{-1}x-2y)(x+(2-t)tz-2y)=\\
&=(x+z-2y)((1-2t)x+(t-2)tz-2(t^{2}-3t+1)y)\}.\end{split}

Now if
q_{3}^{\prime}=[t:-1:t^{-1}]
lies on
{c}_{2}
then
t=1
, which is impossible. Hence
q_{3}^{\prime}\not\in{c}_{2}
.

The pencil of conics passing through
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},r
is generated by
{l}_{2}+{l}_{rq_{2}}
and
{l}_{1}+{l}_{rq_{1}}
. It induces a morphism
{c}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
3
, ramified at points which corresponds to degenerate members:
{l}_{i}+{l}_{rq_{i}}
,
i=1,2,3
. By Hurwitz formula, this morphism is ramified at exactly one other point, which corresponds to a conic
{c}_{3}
as in the statement. Thus
{c}_{3}
is unique. We compute that

(17)

{c}_{3}=\{(1-2t)^{3}\cdot x(x-2y+t(2-t)z)=t(t-2)^{3}\cdot z(t^{2}(z-2y)+(2t-1)x)\}.

Thus we have
{c}_{1}\cap{c}_{3}=\{s_{1},s_{2},r\}
and
({c}_{3}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{1}}=1
,
({c}_{3}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{2}}=2
, where:

(18)

s_{1}=[(t-2)^{2}:(t-2)(2t-1):(2t-1)^{2}],\ s_{2}=[t^{2}(t-2)^{2}:t(t-2)(1-2t):(1-2t)^{2}].

Substituting
s_{1}
to the equation of
{c}_{1}
, we see that
s_{1}\in{c}_{1}
if and only if
t^{\pm 1}=\frac{1}{4}(1-\sqrt{-15})
. The two solutions are mapped to each other by the action of
\tau
; as claimed.

∎

Configuration 2

(Figure
26
)
.

Let
r,{l}_{i},q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
{c}_{3}
be as in Configuration
1
. Define
{c}_{2}
as the unique conic passing through
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}
. Then
q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}\not\in{c}_{2}
. Moreover, up to the action of
\tau
there is a unique point

such that

is tangent to

at

. In this case,

As a consequence,
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=3}}}}=1
. The configuration
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=3}}}}
is realized over

.

We keep the notation from the previous proof; except for
{c}_{2}
which will now be a different conic, constructed below. In particular, we have formulas (
17
) and (
18
) for
{c}_{3}
and

, respectively, depending on the chosen point

, where

.

The pencil of conics passing through

is generated by

and

. By definition,

is the unique member of this pencil which passes through

. Recall that
{l}_{q_{1}q_{2}}={l}_{3}=\{2y=x+z\}
,

,
{l}_{q_{1}q_{3}}={l}_{2}=\{x=0\}
. Moreover,
q_{2}=[2:1:0]
,

, so

. Substituting these equations of lines to the above pencil and evaluating at the point

given by the formula (
18
), we conclude that

{c}_{2}=\{(t-2)^{2}t^{2}(3t-2)(x+z-2y)(t^{-1}x-tz)=3(t-1)^{3}(t+1)x(x-t(t+2)z-2y)\}.

Recall that
q_{1}^{\prime}=[(2-t^{-1})^{-1}:1:2-t^{-1}]
,
q_{2}^{\prime}=[2-t:1:(2-t)^{-1}]
. Substituting each of these points to the above formula we get
t\in\{0,1\}
, which is impossible. Hence
q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}\not\in{c}_{2}
, as claimed.

The line tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
q_{3}^{\prime}=[t:-1:t^{-1}]
is given by
\{x+2ty+t^{2}z=0\}
. This line is tangent to
{c}_{2}
at

if and only if

. As before, these two solutions are equivalent under the action of
\tau
. Thus we get a unique
{c}_{2}
, as claimed. A direct computation shows that this
{c}_{2}
satisfies
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{q_{3}^{\prime}}=2
.

Configuration 3

(Figure
26
)
.

Fix
r\in{l}_{p_{1}p_{2}}\setminus\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
. Write
{l}_{2}\cap{l}_{rp^{\prime}}=\{q\}
and let
{c}_{2}
be the unique conic passing through
q
,
r
and tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
p_{1}
with multiplicity at least
3
. There exist exactly three points
r
such that
({c}_{2}\cdot{l}_{2}^{\prime})_{s}=2
for some
s\neq p_{2},p^{\prime}
. For these points,
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{p_{1}}=3
.

We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}=3
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}
is realized over the splitting field of the polynomial
g_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}(t)\coloneqq 4t^{3}-8t^{2}+4t-1\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
. Moreover, for every root
t_{0}
of
g_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}
, some configuration in
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(t_{0})
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=1}}}}
contains a configuration realized over
\mathbb{R}
, and a pair of complex-conjugate ones.

We have
{l}_{p_{1}p_{2}}=\{y=0\}
, so
r=[1:0:t]
for some
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Then
{l}_{rp^{\prime}}=\{z=tx+(1-t)y\}
, so
q=[0:1:1-t]
. The pencil of conics passing through
q,r
and tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
p_{1}
is generated by
{l}_{1}+{l}_{qr}
and
{l}_{rp_{1}}+{l}_{qp_{1}}
. Recall that
{l}_{1}=\{z=0\}
,
{l}_{qr}={l}_{rp^{\prime}}=\{z=tx+(1-t)y\}
,
{l}_{rp_{1}}={l}_{p_{1}p_{2}}=\{y=0\}
and
{l}_{qp_{1}}=\{z=(1-t)y\}
. Hence the unique member
{c}_{2}
of the above pencil satisfying
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{p_{1}}\geqslant 3
is

{c}_{2}=\{(1-t)\cdot z\cdot(tx+(1-t)y-z)=t\cdot y\cdot((1-t)y-z)\}.

For any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we have
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{p_{1}}=3
. Now
{c}_{2}
is tangent to
{l}_{2}^{\prime}=\{y=x\}
if and only if
4t(t-1)^{2}=1
, i.e.
4t^{3}-8t^{2}+4t-1=0
. This equation has three different solutions, which correspond to three different conics
{c}_{2}
. We have
s=[1:1:-\frac{1}{2}(t-1)]
, so
s\neq p_{2},p^{\prime}
, as claimed.

∎

Configuration 4

(Figure
28
)
.

Write
{l}_{pp^{\prime}}\cap{c}_{1}=\{p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}\}
. Fix a line
{l}\neq{l}_{pp^{\prime}},{l}_{p_{1}p^{\prime\prime}},{l}_{p_{2}p^{\prime\prime}}
passing through
p^{\prime\prime}
, not tangent to
{c}_{1}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
write
{l}\cap{l}_{i}=\{r_{i}\}
and
{l}_{r_{i}p^{\prime}}\cap{c}_{1}=\{p^{\prime},s_{i}\}
.
Assume
s_{1}\neq p_{2}
.
Let
{c}_{2}
be the unique conic passing through
p
,
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
and tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
s_{1}
. Then

(a)

There are exactly three lines
{l}
such that
s_{2}\in{c}_{2}
. In this case,
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{s_{1}}=2
,
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{s_{2}}=1
.

(b)

There are exactly three lines
{l}
such that
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{1}}\geqslant 3
. In this case,
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{1}}=3
,
s_{2}\not\in{c}_{2}
.

For
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}=
,
1
we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}=3
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is realized over the splitting field of
g_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
where
g_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=2_32}}}}(t)=t^{3}+7t^{2}+15t+5
and
g_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_c=2_41}}}}(t)=t^{3}+3t^{2}+3t+5
. Each configuration in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is realized over an extension of
\mathbb{Q}
by a different root of
g_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
contains a configuration realized over
\mathbb{R}
, and a pair of complex-conjugate ones.

We have
p^{\prime\prime}=[1:-1:1]
,
{l}_{pp^{\prime}}=\{x=z\}
,
{l}_{p_{1}p^{\prime\prime}}=\{y=-z\}
,
{l}_{p_{2}p^{\prime\prime}}=\{y=-x\}
and the line tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
p^{\prime\prime}
is given by
\{-2y=x+z\}
. Thus
{l}=\{x+(1+t)y+tz=0\}
for some
t\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1,-1\}
. We compute

\displaystyle r_{1}=[t+1:-1:0],

\displaystyle{l}_{r_{1}p^{\prime}}=\{(t+1)y+x=(t+2)z\},

\displaystyle s_{1}=[(t+2)^{2}:-(t+2):1],

\displaystyle r_{2}=[0:-1:t^{-1}+1],

\displaystyle{l}_{r_{2}p^{\prime}}=\{(t^{-1}+1)y+z=(t^{-1}+2)x\},

\displaystyle s_{2}=[1:-(t^{-1}+2):(t^{-1}+2)^{2}].

Note that
t\neq-2
because
s_{1}\neq p_{2}
. We have
{l}_{r_{1}s_{1}}={l}_{r_{1}p^{\prime}}=\{(t+1)y+x=(t+2)z\}
and

{l}_{r_{2}s_{1}}=\{(t+2)^{2}(tz+(1+t)y)+(t^{2}+2t+2)x=0\}.

The pencil of conics passing through
p,r_{1},r_{2},s_{1}
is generated by
{l}_{2}+{l}_{r_{1}s_{1}}
and
{l}_{1}+{l}_{r_{2}s_{1}}
. Substituting a parametrization
[u:v]\mapsto[u^{2}:uv:v^{2}]
of
{c}_{1}
to the above equations, we compute that the unique member of this pencil which is tangent to
{c}_{1}
at
s_{1}
is given by

{c}_{2}=\{(t^{2}+t+2)x(x+(1+t)y-(2+t)z)=(t+2)(t+3)z((t+2)^{2}(tz+(1+t)y)+(t^{2}+2t+2)x)\}.

Once again, we substitute the parametrization
[u:v]\mapsto[u^{2}:uv:v^{2}]
of
{c}_{1}
to the equation of
{c}_{2}
, and denote the resulting polynomial by
f_{t}\in\mathbb{C}[u,v]
. Since
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{1}}\geqslant 2
, we have
(u+(t+2)v)^{2}|f_{t}
. Put
g_{t}(u)=f_{t}(u,1)\cdot(u+t+2)^{-2}\in\mathbb{C}[u]
. We have

\begin{split}s_{2}\in{c}_{2}&\iff g_{t}(-t^{-1}-2)=0\iff t^{3}+7t^{2}+15t+5=0,\\
({c}_{2}\cdot{c}_{1})_{s_{1}}\geqslant 3&\iff g_{t}(t+2)=0\iff t^{3}+3t^{2}+3t+5=0.\end{split}

Each of these polynomials has three different roots, which proves the first statement of
(a)
and
(b)
. Moreover, the above polynomials have no roots in common, so
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{s_{1}}=2
in case
(a)
and
s_{2}\not\in{c}_{2}
in case
(b)
. Eventually, neither of those polynomials has a common root with the discriminant of
g_{t}
, which implies that
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{s_{2}}=2
in case
(a)
and
({c}_{1}\cdot{c}_{2})_{s_{3}}=3
in case
(b)
, as claimed.

∎

Configuration 5

(Figure
28
)
.

Write
{l}_{2}\cap{l}_{1}^{\prime}=\{q\}
. Let
{c}_{2}
be the unique conic tangent to
{c}_{1}
with multiplicity
4
at
p_{1}
and passing through
q
. Then the following holds.

(a)

The line
{l}_{2}
meets
{c}_{2}
in some point
r\neq q
.

(b)

The line
{l}_{2}^{\prime}
is not tangent to
{c}_{1}
,
{c}_{2}
. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}}
has two elements, and is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
.

(c)

The line
{l}_{rp}^{\prime}
is not tangent to
{c}_{1}
,
{c}_{2}
. Write
{l}_{rp^{\prime}}\cap{c}_{2}=\{r,s\}
. The line
{l}_{qs}
meets
{c}_{1}
in two point. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5_cn}}}}
has two elements, and is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-15})
.

(d)

Choose
t\in{c}_{2}\cap{l}_{2}^{\prime}
. The line
{l}_{qt}
meets
{c}_{1}
in two complex-conjugate points. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_C2C3-node}}}}
consists of four configurations, two of them are realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5},\alpha_{+})
, and two over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5},\alpha_{-})
, where
\alpha_{\pm}=(-5\pm 2\sqrt{5})^{1/2}
. All four configurations are conjugate by
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5},\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}))
.

(a)
We have
q=[0:1:1]
, see Figure
23
. The pencil of conics tangent to
{c}_{1}
in
p_{1}
with multiplicity
4
is generated by
2{l}_{1}=\{z^{2}=0\}
and
{c}_{1}=\{y^{2}=xz\}
. Its unique member passing through
q=[0:1:1]
is
{c}_{2}=\{y^{2}-xz=z^{2}\}
. Substituting the equation
{l}_{2}=\{x=0\}
to the equation of
{c}_{2}
, we get that
{l}_{2}\cap{c}_{2}=\{q,r\}
, where
r=[0:1:-1]\neq q
.

(b)
Recall that
{l}_{2}^{\prime}\cap{c}_{1}=\{p_{2},p^{\prime}\}
, see Figure
23
, so
{l}_{2}^{\prime}
is not tangent to
{c}_{1}
. Substituting the equation
{l}_{2}^{\prime}=\{x=y\}
to the equation of
{c}_{2}
, we get
{l}_{2}^{\prime}\cap{c}_{2}=\{[2:2:\sqrt{5}-1],[2:2:-\sqrt{5}-1]\}
, so
{l}_{2}^{\prime}
is not tangent to
{c}_{2}
. The resulting configuration
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}}\coloneqq{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}^{\prime}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
. Without a marking,
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}}
is unique up to a projective equivalence, and
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}})
is trivial: indeed, any automorphism of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}})
sends a quadruple
(p,p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime})
of points in general position to itself, hence is the identity. Therefore, the two possible orders of the set
{l}_{2}^{\prime}\cap{c}_{2}
give two non-equivalent markings of
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}}
. Thus
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5}}}}=2
, as claimed.

(c)
Since
r=[0:1:-1]
,
p^{\prime}=[1:1:1]
, we have
{l}_{rp^{\prime}}=\{2x=y+z\}
, so
{l}_{rp^{\prime}}\cap{c}_{1}=\{p^{\prime},[1:-2:4]\}
and
\ell_{rp^{\prime}}\cap{c}_{2}=\{r,s\}
, where
s=[5:6:4]
. Now
{l}_{qs}=\{2x=5(y-z)\}
, so
{l}_{qs}\cap{c}_{1}=\{[\alpha^{2}:\alpha:1],[\overline{\alpha}^{2}:\alpha:1]\}
, where
\alpha=\frac{1}{4}(5-\sqrt{-15})
. Like before, we see that the configuration
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2-5_cn}}}}\coloneqq{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{1}^{\prime}+{l}_{rp^{\prime}}+{l}_{qs}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-15})
, and admits two non-equivalent markings corresponding to the order of

.

Put
h[x:y:z]=[x:\beta_{+}\cdot y:\beta_{+}^{2}\cdot z]
. Then
h
maps
(p,p_{1},p_{2})
to itself,
h(p^{\prime})=p^{\prime\prime}_{+}
and
h(r_{-})=[0:\beta_{+}:\beta_{+}^{2}\alpha_{-}]=[0:1:\beta_{+}\alpha_{-}]=[0:1:\alpha_{+}]=r_{+}
. Thus
h({p}_{-})={p}_{+}
.

We have shown that the unmarked configuration
{p}_{-}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
is unique up to a projective equivalence, and is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
. We claim that the two possible markings on
{p}_{-}
, corresponding to the orders of
\{p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}_{-}\}
, are not projectively equivalent. Suppose there is
g\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}_{-})
such that
g(p^{\prime})=p^{\prime\prime}_{-}
. Then
g
maps
(p,p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime})
to
(p,p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime\prime}_{-})
, so
g[x:y:z]=[x:\beta_{-}y:\beta_{-}^{2}z]
. But then
g(r_{-})=[0:1:\alpha_{-}\beta_{-}]\neq r_{-}
, a contradiction.

Hence
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_q-cn_22}}}}=2
. It remains to prove that the two marked configurations in
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:A1A2_q-cn_22}}}}
, are conjugate over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
. Consider a diagonal coordinate change
\varphi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
given by
\varphi[x:y:z]=[-\alpha_{-}x:y:\alpha_{+}z]
. Then
\varphi
maps
(p,p_{1},p_{2})
to itself and
\varphi(r_{-})=[0:1:1]
. Moreover,
\varphi(p^{\prime})=[-\alpha_{-}:1:\alpha_{+}]
,
\varphi(p^{\prime\prime}_{-})=[-\alpha_{-}:\beta_{-}:\beta_{-}^{2}\alpha_{+}]=[-\alpha_{-}\beta_{-}^{-1}:1:\beta_{-}\alpha_{+}]
. We have
\beta_{-}^{-1}=\beta_{+}
, so
\alpha_{-}\beta_{-}^{-1}=\alpha_{-}\beta_{+}=\alpha_{+}
, and similarly
\alpha_{+}\beta_{-}=\alpha_{-}
. Thus
\varphi(p^{\prime\prime}_{-})=[-\alpha_{+}:1:\alpha_{-}]
is the conjugate of
\varphi(p^{\prime})
, and the claim follows.

∎

Write

Write
