and let
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the automorphism mapping
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},q)
to
(r_{3},r_{1},r_{2},q)
. Then the group
\langle\tau,\sigma\rangle\cong S_{3}
permutes
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})
. We have
\sigma({c}_{1})={c}_{1}
, so since
\sigma^{3}=\mathrm{id}
,
\sigma
has two fixed points on
{c}_{1}
, say
q_{1}
,
q_{2}

\tau
acts non trivially on
{c}_{1}

\tau(q_{1})=q_{2}
. Let

,

,

, so
{c}_{1}\cap{c}_{2}=\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
, hence
{c}_{2}
is as in the statement.

To see the uniqueness, suppose that
{c}_{2}^{\prime}
is another conic as in the statement. The pencil of conics generated by
{c}_{2}
and
{c}_{2}^{\prime}
induces a morphism
{c}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
4
, ramified with index
2
at
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},q_{1},q_{2}
, and with indices
e_{1}^{\prime}
,
e_{2}^{\prime}
satisfying
e_{1}^{\prime}+e_{2}^{\prime}=4
at
{c}_{1}\cap{c}_{2}^{\prime}
. This is a contradiction with the Hurwitz formula.

The configuration
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:P2n1-cuspidal}}}}={c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}+{l}_{q_{1}q_{2}}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\omega)
because the eigenvalues of
\sigma
are
1,\omega,\omega^{2}
. All possible markings on
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:P2n1-cuspidal}}}}
are equivalent by the action of
\langle\tau,\sigma\rangle
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:P2n1-cuspidal}}}}=1
. The configuration
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:P2n3}}}}={c}_{1}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
is a conic inscribed in a triangle; it is unique and realized over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Configuration 18

(Figure

)
.

Write

.

We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:C**_1}}}},\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:C**_1a}}}}

The configuration in

consists of four lines in a general position, so it is clearly unique and realized over
\mathbb{Q}
. The configuration in
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:C**_1}}}}
is obtained from it by adding a line through two singular points, so it is again unique and realized over
\mathbb{Q}
. It remains to prove the assertions about
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:nodal-cubic-P2_un}}}}

\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:nodal-cubic_P2}}}}

. Thus the marked configuration

is unique up to a projective equivalence, and realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\omega)
. We have
{l}=\{z=4(y-x)\}
and

, so
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:nodal-cubic-P2_un}}}}\coloneqq{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:nodal-cubic_P2}}}}+{l}
is realized over
\mathbb{Q}(\omega)
, too, but since
\tau({l})\neq{l}
, the two markings on
{p}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:nodal-cubic-P2_un}}}}
are not projectively equivalent. Hence
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:nodal-cubic-P2_un}}}}=2
.

Configuration 19

(Figure

)
.

Write
{l}_{pp^{\prime}}\cap{c}_{1}=\{p^{\prime},q\}
and
{l}_{qp_{2}}\cap{l}_{1}=\{r\}
. We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:P2n2_cuspidal}}}}=\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:C**_2}}}}=1
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:P2n2_cuspidal}}}},\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:C**_2}}}}
are realized over
\mathbb{Q}
. ∎

9.
Uniqueness and automorphisms

In this section we prove Corollaries
1.7
(c)
and
1.8
. The first result counts the numbers of non-isomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes in Theorem
1.6
whose minimal log smooth completions
(X,D)
share the same weighted graph of
D
.
Its proof relies on a simple observation that each surface in a tom Dieck–Petrie tower is obtained by a blowup whose center is uniquely determined by the geometry of the preceding surface.

Nonetheless, the correspondence between isomorphism classes of
\mathbb{Q}
HPs and the combinatorial data
they are constructed from, is not exactly one to one. Roughly speaking, at each step of the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, see Definition
1.5
, we get the following ambiguities:

•

We choose
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
P\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}{p}
, which may not be unique up to a projective equivalence.

•

We choose centers of expansions: they may depend not only on the geometry of
{p}
, but also on its marking, e.g. when two components of
{p}
meet transversally in two points, and we choose one but not the other.

•

Nonetheless, some of the above choices might be covered by automorphisms of
S
, see Example
4.6
.

In order to make the above discussion precise, we need to introduce some notation. We will illustrate it by explicit computations in Examples
9.2
,
9.3
below.

We keep Notation
8.2
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
denotes the set of projective equivalence classes of marked configurations of lines and conics, whose combinatorial type is specified by the row
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
of Tables
–
.

Fix a representative
{p}
of a class in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
. As in Section
4
, let
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a minimal resolution of
{p}
; let
E^{\prime}
be the sum of
(-1)
-curves in
\pi^{-1}(P)
; and
D^{\prime}=(\pi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}-E^{\prime}
. The combinatorial data in row in the last columns of
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
specify centers and weights of an expansion
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. By Remark
3.1
(c)
, they uniquely determine the isomorphism class of
(X,D)
, hence of
S\coloneqq X\setminus D
. Thus we get a well defined map

(19)

\Phi\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}\ni{p}\mapsto[S]\in\{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$-homology planes}\}/[\mbox{isomorphism}].

To prove Corollary
1.7
(c)
, we will show that
\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})={N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
.

Fix
[S]\in\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})
, so
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane whose (unique) log smooth completion is constructed by
\vartheta=\pi\circ\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
as above. By our construction,
E\coloneqq(\vartheta^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}-D
is a sum of
(-1)
-curves.

Let
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
be the automorphism group of the weighted graph of
D
. Since the components of
D
form a basis of
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
, every
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
induces an automorphism of
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
preserving the intersection form. We denote this automorphism by
\sigma
, too. We define a subset
\mathcal{G}(S)
of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
as

\mathcal{G}(S)=\{\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D):\mbox{for every component $L$ of $E$, the class $\sigma[L]$ is represented by a $(-1)$-curve}\}

By Lemma
2.1
and Proposition
11.1
, the log smooth completion
(X,D)
of
S
is unique, hence
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
. This group acts on
\mathcal{G}(S)
by
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\times\mathcal{G}(S)\ni(\tau,\sigma)\mapsto\tau\circ\sigma\in\mathcal{G}(S)
.

Lemma 9.1
.

We have

\#\Phi^{-1}[S]=\#\mathcal{G}(S)/\operatorname{Aut}(S)
. Moreover, the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
on
\mathcal{G}(S)
is free.

Assume that
[S]=\Phi({p}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Write
\vartheta_{i}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}_{i})
, and
E_{i}=(\vartheta_{i}^{*}{p}_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}-D
: it is a sum of
(-1)
-curves, one in each connected component
G
of
\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta_{i}
. The marking on
{p}_{i}
gives an order on the set of components of
E_{i}
. Moreover, writing
\vartheta_{i}
as a sequence of blowups naturally orders the components of each
G
. Thus we get a unique
\sigma\in\mathcal{G}(S)
mapping
\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta_{1}
to
\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta_{2}
which preserves the above order.
Applying inductively the universal property of blowing up, we see that the marked configurations
{p}_{1}
and
{p}_{2}
are projectively equivalent if and only if
\sigma
comes from an automorphism of
(X,D)
, hence of
S
.

Thus every marked configuration
{p}\in\Phi^{-1}[S]
can be obtained from a fixed element of
\Phi^{-1}[S]
by an action of some
\sigma_{{p}}\in\mathcal{G}(S)
, and
{p}
is equivalent to
{p}^{\prime}
if and only if
\sigma_{{p}^{\prime}}=\tau\circ\sigma_{{p}}
for some
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(S)
, as needed.

This proves the first statement. To prove the second one, assume that
\tau\circ\sigma=\sigma
for some
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(S)
,
\sigma\in\mathcal{G}(S)
. Then
\tau
induces the trivial automorphism of the weighted graph of
D
, hence the identity on
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
. In particular,
\tau
maps
D+E
to itself componentwise. It follows that
\tau
descends to an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
preserving the marked configuration
{p}
. Thus it fixes a quadruple of points in general position, namely
(p,p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime})
as in Notation
8.5
. Therefore,
\tau=\mathrm{id}
.

∎

Example 9.2
.

Let
S
be as in
. By Proposition
8.3
(a)
, we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}}=2
. We will compute
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
and show that
\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}})=1
, i.e. for each center,
S
is unique up to an isomorphism.

Assume first that our center is
(C_{1},C_{2};1)
, so
S
is as in Example
4.4
, see Figure
4
. In Example
4.5
, we have seen that there is a morphism
\tau\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{Q}_{3})
, where
\operatorname{Sing}\mathcal{Q}_{3}=\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}
are cusps of multiplicity sequences
(2,2)
. The group
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)\cong S_{3}
permutes
\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}
.

We claim that
\mathcal{G}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
. Figure
4
shows that
E=\operatorname{Exc}\psi+E_{p}+E_{p_{1}}+E_{r}
, and
\tau
maps these curves to
{l}^{\prime}_{3}
,
{c}^{\prime}_{1}
,
{l}^{\prime}_{13}
and
{c}^{\prime}_{2}
; respectively. Here
{l}_{j}^{\prime}
is the line tangent to
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
at
q_{j}
,
{c}_{j}^{\prime}
is the conic passing through
q_{j}
and tangent to
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
at the remaining cusps, and
{l}_{ij}^{\prime}
is the line joining
q_{i}
with
q_{j}
. Hence for any component
E_{0}
of
E
and any
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
, the class
\sigma[E_{0}]\in\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
is represented by some
{l}_{j}^{\prime}
,
{c}_{j}^{\prime}
or
{l}_{ij}^{\prime}
, which is a
(-1)
-curve. Thus
\mathcal{G}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=S_{3}
, as claimed.

We claim that
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
. The inclusion
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\supseteq\mathbb{Z}_{3}
was shown in
[
tD92
, Proposition 2.9]
. We give an independent argument, similar to
[
PP19
, Remark 4.8]
, but not relying on an explicit parametrization of
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
.

By Lemma
9.1
,
\#\operatorname{Aut}(S)\geqslant\#\mathcal{G}(S)/\#\Phi^{-1}[S]\geqslant\#\mathcal{G}(S)/\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}}=6/2=3
, so
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
or
S_{3}
. Suppose
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=S_{3}
. Then some
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(S)
descends to
\overline{\sigma}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{Q}_{3})
such that
\overline{\sigma}\colon(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\mapsto(q_{2},q_{1},q_{3})
. Then
\overline{\sigma}
fixes three points of
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, namely
q_{3}
,
{l}_{3}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and
{l}_{12}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. These points are pairwise distinct: indeed, if
{l}_{12}^{\prime}\cap{l}_{3}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{\mathrm{reg}}=\{p\}
then we infer from Figure
4
that
(\pi\circ\psi)_{*}(\tau^{-1}_{*}{l}_{12}^{\prime})
is a line passing through
r
and tangent to
{c}_{1}
(at
{c}_{1}\cap{c}_{2}\setminus\{q,p_{1}\}
), other than
{l}_{2}
and
{l}_{p^{\prime}}
, which is impossible. Let
\nu\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{3}
be the normalization, and let
\sigma^{\nu}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
be the lift of
\overline{\sigma}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{3}}
. Since
\nu
is a bijection,
\sigma^{\nu}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
fixes three points, hence
\sigma^{\nu}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
. Thus
\overline{\sigma}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{3}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}_{3}}
, but
\overline{\sigma}(q_{1})=q_{2}
; a contradiction.

We conclude that, for
S
obtained by an expansion at
(C_{1},C_{2};1)
, we have

\mathcal{G}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=S_{3},\quad\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\mathbb{Z}_{3},\quad\mbox{so by Lemma \ref{lem:fiber_formula}}\quad\#\Phi^{-1}[S]=\#\mathcal{G}(S)/\operatorname{Aut}(S)=2.

Assume now that our center is not
(C_{1},C_{2};1)
. Then
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is generated by the involution interchanging the total transforms
[2,3,1,2]
of
\tau^{-1}(q_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2\}
. Hence, as in the previous case, we get
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=\mathcal{G}(S)
. Moreover, every automorphism of
S
descends to
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\{\mathrm{id}\}
, so

\mathcal{G}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\{\mathrm{id}\},\quad\mbox{so by Lemma \ref{lem:fiber_formula}}\quad\#\Phi^{-1}[S]=\#\mathcal{G}(S)/\operatorname{Aut}(S)=2.

Thus we have shown that
\#\Phi^{-1}[S]=2
for every
[S]\in\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}})
. It follows that

\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}}=1,

that is, each combinatorial datum in
leads to a unique isomorphism class of a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane.

Example 9.3
.

Let
S
be as in
, see Figure
14(b)
. Then
{p}={c}_{1}+{c}_{1}^{\prime}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
is as in Configuration
9
. It admits two possible markings, corresponding to the orders of the pair
\{q,q^{\prime}\}={l}_{1}\cap{c}_{2}
. Those two marked configurations are not projectively equivalent, in fact,
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n2-transversal}}}}=2
by Proposition
8.3
(a)
.

We have
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if
S
is obtained by expansions at
(C_{2},L_{1},v)
,
(C_{2},L_{1},v^{-1})
for some
v>1
; and
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=\{\mathrm{id}\}
otherwise. Example
9.4
below shows that in either case,
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
is generated by
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
. Conversely, if
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(S)
fixes
D
componentwise, then
\sigma
descends to
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\{\mathrm{id}\}
.

Hence
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
. In particular,
\mathcal{G}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}(S)
, so by Lemma
9.1
,
\#\Phi^{-1}[S]=1
for every
[S]\in\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n2-transversal}}}})
. It follows that

\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n2-transversal}}}})=\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n2-transversal}}}}=2,

that is, each combinatorial datum in
leads to exactly two isomorphism classes of
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes.

Example 9.4
.

Let again
S
be as in
, with expansions at
(C_{2},L_{1},v)
,
(C_{2},L_{1},v^{-1})
for some

. A particular case
v=2
is shown in Figure
43
. We will now construct an involution
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(S)
which realizes the generator of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Recall that the pair
(X,D)
is constructed by expansion from a pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. The graph of
D^{\prime}
is shown in Figure
14(b)
. The
“symmetric”

\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D^{\prime})
. Thus to construct
\iota
, it is enough to construct an involution
\iota^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Aut}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
realizing the generator of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D^{\prime})
.

To this end, we will construct a birational map
\overline{\pi}\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{{p}})

, such that
\overline{\pi}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\overline{\pi}
extends to the required involution
\iota^{\prime}
.

The map
\overline{\pi}
will be a composition
\sigma\circ\pi

, where
\widetilde{\pi}
is a minimal log resolution of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{{p}})
, and

is a blowup at the common point of the branching
(-2)
-curve and the twig
[2]
of
D^{\prime}
, see Figure
43
. The maps
\widetilde{\pi}
and
\beta
will be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant.

We remark that by composing
\overline{\pi}
with expansions as in
, we get an alternative construction of the tower
. This construction is not exactly as in tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, because the map
\overline{\pi}
is not regular.

Remark 9.5
.

Let
S
be as in Example
9.4
. It might be tempting to believe that, by symmetry, the choice of the marking on
{p}
, i.e. the order of
{l}_{1}\cap{c}_{2}=\{q,q^{\prime}\}
, does not matter, hence
S
is unique up to an isomorphism. This is not true:
to get an isomorphism between surfaces obtained from two different markings, we need to find an element of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
whose extension to
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
not only maps
(E_{q},E_{q^{\prime}})
to
(E_{q^{\prime}},E_{q})
, but also fixes
E_{p^{\prime}}
, and there is no such.

Example 9.6
.

Let
S
be the surface
, and let
(X,D)
be its minimal log smooth completion. The graph of
D
is shown in Figure
44
below, cf. Figure
20
. We will now construct an involution
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
such that
\iota(T_{1})=T_{2}
,
\iota(T_{2}^{\prime})=T_{2}^{\prime}
, where
T_{i}
and
T_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
are the subchains of
D
of type
[2,3,1,2]
and
[3,1,2]
, respectively, meeting the remaining part of
D
in the
(-1)
-curve. In Proposition
9.11
we will see that the involution
\iota|_{S}
generates the group
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Like in Example
9.4
, we will obtain
\iota
by constructing a birational map
\overline{\pi}\colon(X,D)\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{{p}})
, where
\overline{{p}}
is a configuration of lines and conics admitting an involution
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{{p}})
; such that
\iota\coloneqq\overline{\pi}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\overline{\pi}
is regular, see Figure
44
. This gives an alternative construction of the surface
. However, since the map
\overline{\pi}
will not be a morphism, this construction cannot be used to infer tom Dieck–Petrie Conjecture
1.1
(a)
in this case.

First, we construct the configuration
\overline{{p}}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
, shown in the right of Figure
44
above. We use Notation
8.5
and coordinates (
16
), but we put a bar over each symbol, so that the notation does not conflict with the one used in Configuration
1
and Figure
20
. Let us recall this notation here. We fix coordinates
[x:y:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and put
\overline{p}^{\prime}=[1:1:1]
,
\overline{{l}}_{1}=\{z=0\}
,
\overline{{l}}_{2}=\{x=0\}
and
\overline{{c}}_{1}=\{y^{2}=xz\}
. The lines
\overline{{l}}_{1}
,
\overline{{l}}_{2}
meet at
\overline{p}\coloneqq[0:1:0]
. The conic
\overline{{c}}_{1}
passes through
\overline{p}^{\prime}
and is tangent to
\overline{{l}}_{1}
,
\overline{{l}}_{2}
at
[1:0:0]
and
[0:0:1]
, respectively. We define the involution
\tau\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
by
[x:y:z]\mapsto[z:y:x]
. Then
\tau(\overline{p})=\overline{p}
,
\tau(\overline{p}^{\prime})=\overline{p}^{\prime}
,
\tau(\overline{{c}}_{1})=\overline{{c}}_{1}
and
\tau(\overline{{l}}_{1})=\overline{{l}}_{2}
.

Let
\overline{q}_{1}=[3:1:0]\in\overline{{l}}_{1}
,
\overline{q}_{2}=\tau(\overline{q}_{1})=[0:1:3]\in\overline{{l}}_{2}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
let
\overline{{l}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
be the line joining
\overline{q}_{i}
with
\overline{p}^{\prime}
. We have
\overline{{l}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\{3y=x+2z\}
,
\overline{{l}}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\{3y=z+2x\}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
write
\overline{{l}}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\cap\overline{{c}}_{1}=\{\overline{p}^{\prime},\overline{q}_{i}\}
, so
\overline{q}_{1}^{\prime}=[4:2:1]
,
\overline{q}_{2}^{\prime}=[1:2:4]
. Let
\overline{{c}}_{2}
be the conic passing through
\overline{p}
,
\overline{q}_{1}
,
\overline{q}_{2}
,
\overline{q}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{q}_{2}^{\prime}
, that is

\overline{{c}}_{2}=\{144\cdot(3y-x-2z)(3y-z-2x)=9\cdot(12y-4x-5z)(12y-4z-5y)\}.

We have
\overline{{c}}_{1}\cap\overline{{c}}_{2}=\{\overline{q}_{1}^{\prime},\overline{q}_{2}^{\prime},\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2}\}
, where
\overline{s}_{1}=[\alpha^{2}:\alpha:1]
,
\overline{s}_{2}=[1:\alpha:\alpha^{2}]
for
\alpha=\frac{1}{4}(1+\sqrt{-15})
. Now for
i\in\{1,2\}
let
\overline{{c}}_{i}^{\prime}
be the conic passing through
\overline{p},\overline{p}^{\prime},\overline{q}_{1},\overline{q}_{2}
and
\overline{s}_{i}
. Hence
\overline{{c}}_{2}^{\prime}=\tau(\overline{{c}}_{1}^{\prime})
and

\overline{{c}}_{1}^{\prime}=\{\alpha^{2}(3\alpha-\alpha^{2}-2)\cdot z\cdot(3y-z-2x)=(3\alpha-1-2\alpha^{2})\cdot x\cdot(3y-x-2z)\}.

Substituting a parametrization
\mathbb{P}^{1}\ni[s:t]\mapsto[s^{2}:st:t^{2}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
of
{c}_{1}
to the above equation of
\overline{{c}}_{1}^{\prime}
, we compute that
\overline{{c}}_{1}^{\prime}
is tangent to
\overline{{c}}_{1}
at
[1:-\alpha^{2}:\alpha^{4}]\neq\overline{p}^{\prime},\overline{s}_{1}
. Applying
\tau
, we get that
\overline{{c}}_{2}^{\prime}
is tangent to
\overline{{c}}_{1}
at
[\alpha^{4}:-\alpha^{2}:1]
.

Let
\overline{{l}}
be the line joining
\overline{p}
with
\overline{p}^{\prime}
. We put
\overline{{p}}=\overline{{l}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overline{{c}}_{i}+\overline{{l}}_{i}+\overline{{l}}_{i}^{\prime\prime}+\overline{{c}}_{i}^{\prime}
. Then
\tau(\overline{{p}})=\overline{{p}}
and
\tau(\overline{{l}})=\overline{{l}}
.

Let
\widetilde{\pi}\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{{p}})
be the minimal log resolution. Define
\widetilde{D}
as
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
minus the
(-1)
-curves over
\overline{p}
,
\overline{p}^{\prime}
,
\overline{q}_{i}
,
\overline{q}_{i}^{\prime}
,
\overline{s}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Now
\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\widetilde{\pi}
extends to a regular involution
\widetilde{\iota}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})

The proper transform of
\overline{{l}}
, say
\widetilde{L}
, is a superfluous
(-1)
-curve in
\widetilde{D}
. Since
\tau(\overline{{l}})=\overline{{l}}
, we have
\widetilde{\iota}(\widetilde{L})=\widetilde{L}
. Let
\beta\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the contraction of
\widetilde{L}
, and let
\overline{\pi}=\widetilde{\pi}\circ\beta^{-1}\colon\overline{X}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Now, the birational map
\beta\circ\widetilde{\iota}\circ\beta^{-1}=\overline{\pi}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\overline{\pi}\colon\overline{X}\dashrightarrow\overline{X}
extends to a regular involution
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X},\overline{D})
.

Note that
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X},\overline{D})
interchanges the subchains
\overline{T}_{i}=[2,3,1,2]
of
\overline{D}
. Indeed, after renaming
\overline{T}_{i}
’s if necessary, for
i\in\{1,2\}
the subchain
\overline{T}_{i}
contains the proper transform of
\overline{{l}}_{i}+\overline{{l}}_{3-i}^{\prime\prime}
, see Figure
44
. Hence
\iota(\overline{T}_{1})=\overline{T}_{2}
. Similarly,
\iota
interchanges the subchains
[3,1,2]
containing the proper transforms of
\overline{{c}}_{1}^{\prime},\overline{{c}}_{2}^{\prime}
.

It remains to show that the pair
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is isomorphic to the minimal log smooth completion
(X,D)
of the surface
. Note that the weighted graphs of
D
and
\overline{D}
agree, see Figures
20
and
44
. Thus the claim can be inferred from the classification Theorem
1.6
. We will now give a direct argument.

Let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a standard quadratic transformation centered at
\overline{p}
,
\overline{p}^{\prime}
,
\overline{s}_{2}
.
Let

{c}_{1}
,
{c}_{2}
,
{c}_{3}
,
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{3}
,
{l}_{rq_{1}}
,
{l}_{rq_{2}}
,

r
,
q_{3}

and

q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{1}^{\prime}
,
q_{2}^{\prime}
,
s_{1}
be the images of

\overline{{c}}_{1}
,
\overline{{c}}_{2}
,
\overline{{c}}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{{l}}_{2}
,
\overline{{l}}_{1}
,
\overline{{c}}_{2}^{\prime}
,
\overline{{l}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
\overline{{l}}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
,

{l}_{\overline{p}\overline{s}_{2}}
,
{l}_{\overline{p}^{\prime}\overline{s}_{2}}
and

\overline{q}_{1}
,
\overline{q}_{2}
,
\overline{q}_{1}^{\prime}
,

.
Then
{p}\coloneqq{c}_{1}+{c}_{2}+{c}_{3}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}+{l}_{rq_{1}}+{l}_{rq_{2}}
is as in Configuration
1
. Since the latter is unique up to a projective equivalence, we can identify it with
{p}
. Recall that the pair
(X,D)
is constructed in
as follows: take the minimal log resolution
\pi\colon(X,D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})

as

extends to the required isomorphism

.

Remark 9.7
.

Let again
(X,D)
be a minimal log smooth completion of the surface
. Let
\upsilon\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
be the contraction of
D-\overline{C}_{1}-\overline{C}_{2}
, where
\overline{C}_{1}
,
\overline{C}_{2}
are as in Figure
44
. Then
\upsilon(\overline{C}_{2})
is the negative section, and
R\coloneqq\upsilon(\overline{C}_{1})
is as in Proposition
6.2
(a)
, that is,
R
is a rational curve of type
(1,3)
, with four cusps of multiplicity two, say
p_{1},p_{2},p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime}
, such that
\delta_{p_{i}}=2
,
\delta_{p_{i}^{\prime}}=1
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. The involution
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
constructed in Example
9.6
induces an involution
\widehat{\iota}\coloneqq\upsilon\circ\iota\circ\upsilon^{-1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},R)
such that
\widehat{\iota}(p_{1})=p_{2}
,
\widehat{\iota}(p_{1}^{\prime})=p_{2}^{\prime}
.

Remark 9.8
.

The construction of the surface
given in Example
9.6
is invariant under the action of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-15}))
. Therefore, the surface
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. This fact also follows from Remark
6.3
, which views
S
as a complement of a curve
\overline{R}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
given by an equation with rational coefficients.

Example 9.9
.

Let
S
be as in
3
, see Figure
17
. The group
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
is nontrivial if and only if
\psi
is centered at
(L_{1},L_{2};v)
,
(L_{2},L_{3};v)
,
(L_{3},L_{1};v)
for some
v>1
. We claim that in this case,
\mathcal{G}(S)=\operatorname{Aut}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{3}
.

We have
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)=S_{3}
. The configuration
{p}
consists of a conic
{c}_{1}
inscribed a triangle
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
, so
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})\cong S_{3}
. Clearly, the
3
-cycle in
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
lifts to an automorphism of
(X,D)
, hence of
S
. Suppose that a transposition
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
lies in
\mathcal{G}(S)
. Say that
\tau(L_{1})=L_{2}
,
\tau(L_{3})=L_{3}
. Then
\tau
maps the bubble
A
over
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{3}
to a bubble
\tau(A)
meeting
D
in the preimages of
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{3}
and
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{3}
. The image of
\tau(A)
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
meets
{p}
only in the vertices of the triangle
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
, so it is disjoint from
{c}_{1}
; a contradiction.

We now proceed with the proof of Corollaries
1.7
(c)
and
1.8
.
Lemma
9.1
shows that to prove Corollary
1.7
(c)
, we need to describe
\mathcal{G}(S)
and
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
for
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes
S
as in Theorem
1.6
. This is done in Proposition
9.11
below. The following observation will be useful.

Lemma 9.10
.

(a)

Fix
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
. Let
C
be a component of
D
. If
\sigma
fixes three points of
C
then
\sigma|_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{C}
.

(b)

Fix
\sigma\in\mathcal{G}(S)
. Let
T
be a
(-2)
-twig of
D
and let
A\subseteq E
be a
(-1)
-curve meeting
T
only once, in a tip of
D
. Assume that
\sigma(B)=B
for every component
B
of
D-T
meeting
A+T
. Then
\sigma(T)=T
.

Part
(a)
follows from the fact that
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Suppose
(b)
fails, so
\sigma(T)\neq T
. Then
\sigma(T)
is another
(-2)
-twig of
D
, disjoint from
T
. By definition of
\mathcal{G}(S)
, the class
\sigma(A)
is represented by a
(-1)
-curve. Since
\sigma(T)\neq T
, we have
\sigma(A)\neq A
, so
\sigma(A)\cdot A\geqslant 0
. Let
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow Z
be the contraction of
T+\sigma(T)
and let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the induced automorphism of
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Z)
. By assumption, we have
\sigma^{\prime}(\varphi(A))\equiv\varphi(A)
and
A\cdot\sigma(T)=0
. Since
A+T
is negative definite, we get
0>\varphi(A)^{2}=\sigma^{\prime}(\varphi(A))\cdot\varphi(A)\geqslant\sigma(A)\cdot A\geqslant 0
; a contradiction.

∎

We can now give a description of
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
and
\mathcal{G}(S)
, which implies Corollary
1.8
.

Proposition 9.11
.

Let
S
be one of the
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes listed in Theorem
1.6
. Then the group
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is nontrivial if and only if one of the following holds.

(a)

We have
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\cong S_{3}
, and
S
is constructed in
by expansions at
\{(C_{1},L_{pp^{\prime}};2),(E_{p},L_{1},1),(E_{p},L_{2},1)\}
. Here
S
is the complement of the tricuspidal quartic, see e.g.
[
tD92
, Proposition 2.9]
or
[
PP19
, Lemma 4.5]
.

(b)

We have
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{3}
,
S
is constructed in
by an expansion at
(C_{1},C_{2},1)
, and
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is described in Example
9.2
, cf. Example
4.6
.

(c)

We have
\operatorname{Aut}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, the generator of
\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is induced by the rotation of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, defined using Notation
8.5
by
(p,p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime})\mapsto({l}_{2}\cap{l}_{p^{\prime}},p_{2},p^{\prime},p_{1})
; and
S
is one of the following:

2

with any expansion, see
[
tD90
, Theorem 1]
.

3

with expansions at
(L_{1},L_{2};v),(L_{2},L_{3};v),(L_{3},L_{1};v)
for some
v>1
, see Example
9.9
.

with expansions at
({l}_{1},{l}_{2};v),({l}_{2},{l}_{1}^{\prime};v),({l}_{1}^{\prime},{l}_{1};v)
for some
v>1
.

(d)

We have
\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\langle\iota\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
(S,\iota)
is one of the following.

.

Here
\iota
is constructed in Example
9.6
.

with expansions at
(C_{2},L_{1},v)
,
(C_{2},L_{1},v^{-1})
for some
v\neq 1
. Here
\iota
is as in Example
9.4
.

with expansions at
(L_{1},L_{2};t)
,
(E_{q_{1}},L_{q_{1}q_{2}},u)
,
(L_{j}^{\prime},L_{j},(u+1)^{3-2j})
,
(L_{3-j},E_{q_{3-j}},t)
for some
t,u
and
j\in\{1,2\}
. To see
\iota
, let
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the contraction of
L_{q_{1}q_{2}}
. Now
\iota
comes from an involution of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
mapping vertical lines
\varphi(L_{1})
,
\varphi(L_{2}^{\prime})
,
\varphi(E_{q_{2}})
to
\varphi(E_{q_{2}})
,
\varphi(L_{2}^{\prime})
,
\varphi(L_{1})
; and horizontal lines
\varphi(L_{2})
,
\varphi(L_{1}^{\prime})
,
\varphi(E_{q_{2}})
to
\varphi(L_{2})
,
\varphi(E_{q_{1}})
,
\varphi(L_{1}^{\prime})
.

with expansions at
(C_{1},L_{pp^{\prime}},w)
and, for both
j\in\{1,2\}
, at
(L_{j},E_{p_{j}},v)
or
(C_{1},E_{p_{j}},v)
or
(E_{p},L_{j},v)
, for some
v,w
; in the latter case
(v,w)\neq(1,2)
. Here
\iota
comes from a reflection
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
given by
\tau\colon(p,p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime})\mapsto(p,p_{2},p_{1},p^{\prime})
.

4

with expansions at one of:
(L_{1}^{\prime},E_{p^{\prime}};v)
,
(L_{qp^{\prime}}^{\prime},E_{p^{\prime}};v)
or
(E_{p^{\prime}},C_{1};v)
,
(E_{p^{\prime}},C_{2};v)
or
(C_{2},L_{1}^{\prime};v)
,
(C_{1},L_{qp^{\prime}}^{\prime};v)
for some
v
. Here
\iota
comes from a reflection of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
mapping
(p_{1},p_{2},p^{\prime},q)
to
(q,p_{2},p^{\prime},p_{1})
, see Figure
29(a)
.

The quotient
\mathcal{G}(S)/\operatorname{Aut}(S)
is trivial in all cases except
,
where
\mathcal{G}(S)/\operatorname{Aut}(S)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, see Example
9.2
.

We check directly that nontrivial elements of
\operatorname{Aut}_{\textnormal{graph}}(D)
which satisfy Lemma
9.10
(b)
occur only in towers
,
,
,
,
,
2
,
,
and
3
–

. Cases

are settled in Examples

,

,

. Consider all the remaining ones together. In these cases, we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}=1
by Proposition

(a)
, so

by Lemma

. Recall that

by Lemma

. Now, the elements of

which satisfy Lemma

are exactly the ones listed in the statement.

∎

Remark 9.12
.

Exactly two of the symmetric towers from Proposition
9.11
contain
\mathbb{Z}
-homology planes, namely
2
with
\operatorname{Aut}=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
and
with
\operatorname{Aut}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. These examples were first constructed by tom Dieck in
[
tD90
, Theorems 2 and 6]
. The latter was also obtained in
[
MS91
, §4]
by a different method.

The surface
is a new example of a symmetric
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane. All the remaining ones were previously known, see
[
tD90
, Remark 4]
. The towers
,
3
with
\operatorname{Aut}=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
were constructed e.g. in
[
tD92
,
A
,
B
]
. The towers
,
4
with
\operatorname{Aut}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
were constructed in
[
tD92
,
F,N
]
; the latter also in
[
MS91
,
(T3C_{2})
]
. The remaining surfaces are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\overline{E}
, where
\overline{E}
is a rational tricuspidal curve: in case
,
\overline{E}
is the quintic
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, see Example
4.4
; in case
,
\overline{E}
is the quartic as in Proposition
9.11
(a)
, see
[
tD90
, Remark 3]
.

We check directly
that different combinatorial data from Tables
–
lead to different weighted graphs of
D
. Thus to prove Corollary
1.7
(c)
, it remains to show that for every row
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
of Tables
–
, we have
\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})={N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
, see Notation
8.2
and formula (
19
).

By Lemma
9.1
, for
[S]\in\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})
we have
\#\Phi^{-1}[S]=\#\mathcal{G}/\operatorname{Aut}(S)
, which by Proposition
9.11
equals
2
for
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}=

and
1
otherwise. Hence

\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\#\mathcal{P}_{\mbox{\tiny{\ref{def:F2_n1-cusp}}}}
and
\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})=\#\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
for
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}\neq

. By Proposition
8.3
(a)
, we get
\#\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})={N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
, as claimed.

∎

We conclude this section with the following result, which implies Corollary
1.10
.

Proposition 9.13
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
be the set of isomorphism classes of
\mathbb{Q}
HPs obtained by tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm from data given by the row
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
of Tables
–
, including fixed weights of expansions. Then the following holds.

(a)

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log smooth completion of a surface
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
. Then the weighted graph of
D
does not depend on
S
.

(b)

We have
\#\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}={N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
, where
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is the number in the column
“
{N}
”
of
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
.

(c)

All surfaces in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
are conjugate by
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{Q}}({k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}})
, where
{k}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
is a finite field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
, specified in column
“
{k}
”
of
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}
. In particular, their fundamental groups have the same profinite completions.

(d)

If
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}=2
and
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}\neq
,
,
then the two surfaces in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
are complex-conjugate, hence diffeomorphic.

(e)

If
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}=3
then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
consist of a pair of complex-conjugate surfaces, and a surface defined over
\mathbb{R}
.

(f)

If
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}=4
then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
consists of two pairs of complex-conjugate surfaces.

Part
(a)
follows from the construction of
(X,D)
by the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, see Definition
1.5
. Part
(b)
is a restatement of Corollary
1.7
(c)
, proved above. The remaining parts follow from Proposition
8.3
; for the assertion about fundamental groups see
[
AM69
, Corollary 12.11]
.

∎

Remark 9.14

(cf. Remark
8.4
)
.

In view of Proposition
9.13
, it is natural to pose the following question. Are there two non-diffeomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
HPs of log general type, whose minimal log smooth completion share the same weighted boundary graph?

This question is open. Proposition
9.13
implies that if such
\mathbb{Q}
HPs exist and satisfy the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
, then they are Galois conjugate, and in fact belong to the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}
with
{N}_{\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}}>2
or
\mathfrak{\langle*\rangle}=

,
,
. We remark that
examples of non-diffeomorphic, but Galois conjugate varieties do exist, they were constructed by various methods e.g. in
[
Ser64
,
Abe74
,
Shi09
]
.

Remark 9.15
.

Projective surface with the same Betti numbers as
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are classified in
[
CS10
,
PY07
]
. Like
\mathbb{Q}
HPs in Proposition
9.13
(d)
, those
fake projective planes
come in complex-conjugate pairs, there is
50
of them.

10.
Negativity Conjecture implies Rigidity Conjecture

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
1.3
, by proving that
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes in Theorem
1.6
satisfy the Rigidity Conjecture
1.1
(b)
, that is,
H^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
for
i\geqslant 0
. We prove this result in Proposition
10.3
, after some preparations. Our argument is based on
[
FZ00
, Proposition 2.2]
.

Recall that for a log smooth pair
(V,T)
, the logarithmic tangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T)
is a subsheaf of
\mathcal{T}_{V}
consisting of those derivations which preserve the ideal sheaf of
T
. Its cohomology controls the deformation theory of
(V,T)
, see
[
FZ94
,
Kaw78
]
. Below, we list some of its properties.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
V_{0}
be a smooth surface and let
(V,T)
be some log smooth completion of
V_{0}
. Then

(a)

The number
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))
depends only on
V_{0}
.

(b)

If
L\subseteq T
is a
(-1)
-curve then
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log(T-L)))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))
for all
i\geqslant 0
.

(c)

If
V_{0}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane then
\chi(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))=K_{V}\cdot(K_{V}+T)
.

(d)

If
V_{0}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type then
\chi(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))=h^{0}(2K_{V}+T)\geqslant 0
.

Parts
(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
are proved in
[
FZ94
]
, Lemma 1.5(5), Proposition 1.7(3) and Lemma 1.3(5), respectively. Part
(d)
is shown in
[
Pal19
, Lemma 4.3(i)]
for
V_{0}
being a complement of a planar rational cuspidal curve; however, the argument works in our generality, too. Let us recall it here for completeness. By
(c)
and by the Riemann–Roch theorem, we have
\chi(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))=K_{V}\cdot(K_{V}+T)=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V}(2K_{V}+T))
. Let
K_{V}+T=\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{N}
be the Zariski–Fujita decomposition. Lemma
2.3
implies that
\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{Bk}_{T}T^{\prime}
, where
T^{\prime}
is the sum of maximal twigs of
T
, see
[
Pal19
, Lemma 2.1.(iv)]
. We have
\lfloor\mathcal{N}\rfloor=\lfloor\operatorname{Bk}_{T}T^{\prime}\rfloor=0
by
[
Miy01
, II.3.5.2]
, so
\lceil\mathcal{P}\rceil=K_{V}+T
. Because
\mathcal{P}
is big and nef, the Kawamata-Viehweg vanishing theorem
[
Laz04
, 9.1.18]
gives
h^{i}(2K_{V}+T)=h^{i}(K_{V}+\lceil\mathcal{P}\rceil)=0
for
i>0
, which ends the proof.

∎

To prove that the Negativity Conjecture
1.2
implies the Rigidity Conjecture
1.1
(b)
, we need to show that the number
h^{2}\coloneqq H^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
is zero for pairs
(X,D)
in Theorem
1.6
. To do this, we use specific
\mathbb{C}^{(t*)}
-fibrations of
X\setminus D
, where
t\leqslant 3
. If
t\leqslant 2
, then
h^{2}=0
by
[
FZ94
, Proposition 6.2]
. If
t=3
,
[
FZ00
, Proposition 2.2]
gives
h^{2}=0
provided
D
is a particular configuration on
X=\mathbb{F}_{m}
. We will need this result in a more general setting, where the same argument works. For the readers’ convenience, we repeat it here in detail.

Lemma 10.2

(cf.
[
FZ94
, Proposition 6.2]
or
[
FZ00
, Proposition 2.2]
)
.

Let
S
be a smooth surface which admits a
\mathbb{C}^{(t*)}
-fibration for some
t\leqslant 3
. If
t=3
, assume additionally that some fiber has a component
F_{0}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{**}
. Then
H^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))=0
for any log smooth completion
(V,T)
of
S
.

By Lemma
10.1
(a)
, the number
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))
does not depend on the choice of the log smooth completion
(V,T)
. Choose one such that the
\mathbb{C}^{(t*)}
-fibration of
S
extends to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\pi
of
V
; so
F_{\mathrm{gen}}\cdot T=t+1
for a general fiber
F_{\mathrm{gen}}
of
\pi
. Let
F
be the closure of
F_{0}
if
t=3
and
F=F_{\mathrm{gen}}
if
t<3
. In any case,
F\cdot T>\tfrac{1}{2}F_{\mathrm{gen}}\cdot T
, so
F
has multiplicity one in the fiber. Hence there is a morphism
\varphi\colon(V,T)\longrightarrow(V^{\prime},T^{\prime})
which contracts the fibers of
\pi
to
0
-curves, such that
F\not\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. Put
F^{\prime}=\varphi(F)
, and denote by
\pi^{\prime}
the induced
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
V^{\prime}
.

By Serre duality, we have an isomorphism
H^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))\cong H^{0}(\Omega_{V}^{1}(\log T)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}(K_{V}))
. Using the perfect pairing
\Omega^{1}_{V}(\log T)^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{V}(K_{V}+T)
, we get

\Omega^{1}_{V}(\log T)\cong Hom(\Omega^{1}_{V}(\log T),\mathcal{O}_{V}(K_{V}+T))\cong\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}(K_{V}+T),

hence
\Omega^{1}_{V}(\log T)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}(K_{V})\cong\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}(2K_{V}+T)
.
It follows that

H^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T))\cong H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{V}(-\log T)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}(2K_{V}+T))\subseteq H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{V^{\prime}}(-\log T^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}(2K_{V^{\prime}}+T^{\prime})).

Suppose the latter space contains a vector field
\xi\neq 0
. Let
\xi_{0}
be the image of
\xi
under the map
\mathcal{T}_{V^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{F^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{F^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{F^{\prime}}
is the normal bundle to
F^{\prime}
in
V^{\prime}
. Hence
\xi_{0}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{F^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{F^{\prime}}((2K_{V^{\prime}}+T^{\prime})\cdot F^{\prime}))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(t-3))
. If
t<3
then
\xi_{0}\equiv 0
. If
t=3
then by assumption
F^{\prime}
is tangent to
T^{\prime}
at some
r\in F^{\prime}
, hence
\xi_{0}|_{r}=0
, so since
\xi_{0}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
is constant, we get
\xi_{0}\equiv 0
, too.

Therefore,
\xi
is tangent to
F^{\prime}
, so
\xi|_{F^{\prime}}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{F^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{F^{\prime}}((2K_{V^{\prime}}+T^{\prime})\cdot F^{\prime}))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(t-1))
. After shrinking
\pi^{\prime}(V^{\prime})
if needed, we can divide
\xi
by the equation of
F^{\prime}
and assume
\xi|_{F^{\prime}}\not\equiv 0
.

Suppose
t<3
. Then
F^{\prime}
is a general fiber, so it meets
T^{\prime}
transversally, at
t+1
points. Since
\xi
is tangent to
T^{\prime}+F^{\prime}
, each of these points is a zero of
\xi|_{F^{\prime}}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(t-1))
, a contradiction.

Therefore,
t=3
, so
\xi|_{F^{\prime}}
has two zeros. By definition,
F^{\prime}
meets
T^{\prime}
in three points, say
p,q,r
. We have
T^{\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}=4
, so, say,
(T^{\prime}\cdot F^{\prime})_{p}=(T^{\prime}\cdot F)_{q}=1
,
(T^{\prime}\cdot F^{\prime})_{r}=2
. Since
\xi
is tangent to
T^{\prime}+F^{\prime}
, we have
\xi|_{p}=\xi|_{q}=0
. To get a contradiction, we will show that
\xi|_{r}=0
, too. We argue as in
[
FZ94
, Lemma 6.5]
. If
r\in\operatorname{Sing}T^{\prime}
then clearly
\xi|_{r}=0
, so assume
r\not\in\operatorname{Sing}T^{\prime}
. In some local analytic coordinates
(x,y)
at
r
, we have
F^{\prime}=\{x=0\}
,
T^{\prime}=\{x=y^{2}\}
, and
\xi=f\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x}+g\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y}
for some
f,g\in\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]
. Since
\xi
is tangent to
F^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
, we have
\xi(x)=f=x\cdot a
and
\xi(y^{2}-x)=2yg-f=(y^{2}-x)\cdot b
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]
. The equality
f=xa
implies that
f(0,0)=0
. Moreover, substituting
f=xa
to the equality
2yg-f=(y^{2}-x)b
, we infer that
y|(a-b)
and
g=y\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}b+x\cdot\tfrac{a-b}{2y}
, so
g(0,0)=0
, too. Thus
\xi|_{r}=0
, as required.

∎

Proposition 10.3
.

Let
S
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-homology plane of log general type, and let
(X,D)
be its log smooth completion. Assume that
\kappa(K_{X}+\frac{1}{2}D)=-\infty
. Then for every
i\geqslant 0
we have
H^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
.

Put
h^{i}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. Recall that
\kappa(X\setminus D)=2
implies that
h^{0}=0
. Indeed, by
[
Iit82
, Theorem 11.12]
in this case the group
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is finite, so
(X,D)
admits no infinitesimal automorphisms. Hence
h^{2}-h^{1}=\chi(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))\geqslant 0
by Lemma
10.1
(d)
. It remains to show
h^{2}=0
.

By Lemma
10.2
we can assume that
S
has no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibration, so
(X,\tfrac{1}{2}D)
has an almost minimal model
(X_{\mathrm{am}},\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{am}})
as in Section
6
. We use the notation introduced there. In particular,
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X_{\mathrm{am}},D_{\mathrm{am}})
is the minimal log resolution. Lemma
10.1
implies that
h^{2}=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log(\psi^{*}D^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}))=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))
.

Consider the case
X_{\mathrm{am}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, see Section
6D
. Then
\deg D_{\mathrm{am}}=5
. Assume that
D_{\mathrm{am}}
has an ordinary node, say
p
. Let
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be a blowup at the preimage of
p
. Then
h^{2}=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log(\pi^{-1}_{*}D^{\prime})))
by Lemma
10.1
. The pencil of lines through
p
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
with a fiber
F
such that
F\cdot\pi^{-1}_{*}D^{\prime}=\pi(F)\cdot D^{\prime}-2=\deg D_{\mathrm{am}}-2=3
, so
h^{2}=0
by Lemma
10.2
. Thus we can assume that
D_{\mathrm{am}}
does not have ordinary nodes.

In this case,
D_{\mathrm{am}}
has at most one line. Using condition (
14
), we infer that
D_{\mathrm{am}}={l}+{q}
, where
{l}
is a line tangent to a quartic
{q}
at two points; and
{q}
has three ordinary singularities. By Lemma
3.13
(c)
, the singular points of
{q}
are cusps. Let
\mathbb{F}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a blowup at a cusp
p\in{q}
. The fibration
\pi_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}
is given by the pencil of lines through
p
. By Hurwitz formula, neither of these lines is tangent to
{q}\setminus\{p\}
. Hence the line joining
p
with some other cusp of
{q}
meets
D_{\mathrm{am}}
in exactly three points. Thus
h^{2}=0
by Lemma
10.2
.

Consider the remaining cases together.
In case
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,2,3)
, let
\varphi\colon X_{\mathrm{am}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-curve
U\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta
and the short
(-2)
-twig in
\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}
, see Figure
2(a)
; and put
R\coloneqq\varphi_{*}R_{\mathrm{am}}
. We have
\varphi_{*}\Delta_{\mathrm{am}}=\Xi_{2}+F_{0}
for some fiber
F_{0}
, such that
F_{0}\cap R
is an ordinary node of
R
. In case
X_{\min}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
put
U=0
,
F_{0}=0
and
R=R_{\mathrm{am}}
. Now in both cases, the divisor
R\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}
is of type
(1,3)
. The pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+U)
is a log smooth completion of
S^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbb{F}_{2}\setminus(R+\Xi_{2}+F_{0})
, and
h^{2}=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log(D^{\prime}+U)))
by Lemma
10.1
(b)
.

Write
R\cap\Xi_{2}=\{p\}
. For a point
r\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
, we denote by
F_{r}
the fiber passing through
r
.

Let
r
be a singular point of
R_{\mathrm{hor}}\setminus F_{p}
. Since
R
has no triple points, we have
F_{r}\not\subseteq R
. Hence
\#(F_{r}\cap R)\leqslant F_{r}\cdot R-1=2
. If
\#(F_{r}\cap R)=2
then by construction
F_{r}\neq F_{0}
, and since
F_{r}\neq F_{p}
, we have
\#(F_{r}\cap(R\cup\Xi_{2}))=3
. Hence
F_{r}\cap S^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{C}^{**}
, so
h^{2}=0
by Lemma
10.2
. Therefore, we can assume that

(20)

for every
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R_{\mathrm{hor}}\setminus F_{p}
we have
F_{r}\cap R=\{r\}
.

Suppose
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
is reducible. Then
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
contains a
1
-section, say
H_{1}
. Put
H_{2}=R_{\mathrm{hor}}-H_{1}
. Numerical properties of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
imply that
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}\geqslant 4
. Moreover, if
H_{2}
is irreducible then
\delta_{H_{2}}\geqslant 1
; so in any case,
H_{2}
is singular. Since all singularities of
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
are double points, we have
H_{1}\cap\operatorname{Sing}H_{2}=\emptyset
. In particular,
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
has at least two singular points. Every fiber contains at most one singular point of
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so
\operatorname{Sing}R_{\mathrm{hor}}\setminus F_{p}\neq\emptyset
.

Fix
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R_{\mathrm{hor}}\setminus F_{p}
. Condition (
20
) implies that
r
is a common point of all components of
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
. In particular,
H_{2}
is irreducible,
(F_{r}\cdot H_{2})_{r}=2
and
r\in H_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. It follows that
\operatorname{Sing}H_{2}\subseteq F_{p}
. Since
H_{2}
is a
2
-section, we have
H_{2}\cap F_{p}=\operatorname{Sing}H_{2}
, so
H_{2}\cap H_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}\setminus F_{p}
. Thus at every point
r\in H_{2}\cap H_{1}
, the fiber
F_{r}
is tangent to
H_{2}
. Since
H_{1}
is a
1
-section, it is not tangent to
F_{r}
, hence
(H_{1}\cdot H_{2})_{r}=1
Applying Hurwitz formula to
\pi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|_{H_{2}}
we infer that
2\geqslant\#(H_{2}\cap H_{1})=H_{2}\cdot H_{1}\geqslant 4
; a contradiction.

Therefore,
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
is irreducible. Suppose
F_{p}\subseteq R
. Then
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
is of type
(0,3)
, so
\delta_{R_{\mathrm{hor}}}=4
by Lemma
2.7
. Moreover, singular points of
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
do not lie on
F_{p}
. Condition (
20
) implies that for every
r\in\operatorname{Sing}R_{\mathrm{hor}}
, we have
(R_{\mathrm{hor}}\cdot F_{r})_{r}=3
, so
\delta_{r}=1
. By Lemma
3.13
(c)
,
D_{\mathrm{am}}
has only two nodes, which are mapped to
\Xi_{2}\cap F_{p}
and
R_{\mathrm{hor}}\cap F_{p}
. Hence the only possible node of
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
is the one on
F_{0}
. Since
\delta_{R_{\mathrm{hor}}}=4
, it follows that
R_{\mathrm{hor}}
has at least
3
cusps, each tangent to a fiber. This is a contradiction with the Hurwitz formula.

Thus
F_{p}\not\subseteq R_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so
R=R_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Now,
R
is as in Proposition
6.2
. Its last statement implies that condition (
20
) fails in this case; a contradiction.

∎

11.
Surfaces in Theorem
1.6
are of log general type

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
, by showing the following proposition.

Proposition 11.1
.

Let
(X,D)
be a pair obtained by tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm from data in one of the rows of Tables
–
. Then the surface
S\coloneqq X\setminus D
is of log general type.

A direct check shows that
D
is snc-minimal, and all non-branching components of
D
have negative self-intersection numbers. Hence by Lemma
2.1
(b)
,
(X,D)
is the unique minimal log smooth completion of
S
.

Suppose
\kappa(S)=-\infty
. Then by
[
Miy01
, III.1.3.2]
(see Proposition
2.2
)
S
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-fibration over
\mathbb{C}^{1}
. The fiber at infinity of a minimal completion of such a fibration is a non-branching
0
-curve in
D
, but we have seen that
D
contains no such; a contradiction.

Suppose now
\kappa(S)\in\{0,1\}
. We check directly that
S
is not a Fujita surface. Indeed, these surfaces are constructed in
[
Fuj82
, 8.64]
by expansion as in
, with weights
(2,2,2)
, or as in
, with weights
(1,1,2,1)
and
(1,1,2,2)
. These weights are explicitly excluded in Tables
and
9
.

Therefore,
[
Miy01
, III.1.7.1]

implies that
S
has a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibration. By
[
PP17
, Proposition 4.2]
we may choose one which extends to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Suppose
D
contains a fiber of
p
, say
F
. Since all
0
-curves in
D
are branching,
[
Fuj82
, 7.5]
implies that
F=[2,1,2]
and
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets
F
once, in the middle component, say
C
. Put
C^{\prime}=\psi(C)
. If
\psi
is an isomorphism in some neighborhood of
C
then
\beta_{D^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})=3
and
C^{\prime}
meets two twigs of
D^{\prime}
of type
[2]
: we check directly that this is not the case. Hence
(C^{\prime})^{2}\geqslant 0
, so
D
is as in Figure
22
. Suppose
(C^{\prime})^{2}=0
. Then
C^{\prime}
contains only one center of
\psi
. Since
\beta_{D^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})=3
, it follows that both components of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-C
are proper transforms of components of
D^{\prime}
meeting
C^{\prime}
. We check directly (see Figure
22
) that those components are disjoint and branching in
D^{\prime}
, so each of them contains two other centers of
\psi
. This is impossible, since
\#\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}=n\leqslant 4
. Thus
(C^{\prime})^{2}\geqslant 1
. It follows that
S
is as in
with expansions of weights
2
and
\tfrac{1}{2}
. Those weights are excluded in Table
; a contradiction.

Thus
D
contains no fibers.
Since
\kappa(S)\geqslant 0
, the description of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fibrations in
[
Pal12
, Theorem 4.9(C)]
implies that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, say
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
;
\beta\coloneqq\beta_{D}(H_{1})=\beta_{D}(H_{2})\geqslant 3
and each
H_{i}
meets
\beta-1
twigs of
D
, say
T_{i,1},\dots,T_{i,\beta-1}
, such that for
j\in\{1,\dots,\beta-1\}
, the chain
[T_{1,j}^{t},1,T_{2,j}]
is a fiber of
p
. In particular,
T_{2,j}=T_{1,j}^{*}
. Here we write
T^{t}
for the chain
T
with the opposite order, and
T^{*}
for the chain adjoint to
T
, see
[
Fuj82
, Section 4]
for definitions.

By Lemma
3.11
(b)
we can assume that all expansions have weight
1
(if such weight is allowed).

Consider case
. Then
D
contains a
(+1)
-curve
L_{2}^{\prime}
meeting all branching components of
D
, so, say,
L_{2}^{\prime}=H_{1}
. Thus
\beta=3
, and, say,
L_{j}\subseteq T_{1,j}
. In particular,
\beta_{D}(L_{j})=2
, so for some
i\in\{1,2\}
, the chain
[1,T_{2,i}^{*}]
is the preimage of one of the centers of
\psi
. Hence
T_{2,i}^{*}
is a
(-2)
-chain, so
T_{2,i}
is irreducible, i.e.
T_{2,i}=L_{i}
. Thus the corresponding weight equals one, which is excluded in Table
; a contradiction.

Consider case
with center
(L_{1},E_{q_{1}})
. Then
L_{1}^{\prime}
is the only branching component of
D
; a contradiction.

Now, consider all the remaining cases. Then
D
contains a branching
(-1)
-curve
C
meeting at most one other branching component of
D
. Such
C
is horizontal, say,
C=H_{1}
. Indeed, suppose
C
is a component of a fiber
F
. Then
\beta_{D}(C)=3
implies that
C
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, hence
C
has multiplicity
1
in
F
. This is possible only if
C
is a tip of
F
, so
C
meets
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, but the latter are branching in
D
; a contradiction.

Now we check directly that in each case one of the following conditions hold:

(a)

There is another
(-1)
-curve
C^{\prime}\neq C
meeting at most one other branching component of
D
(hence
C^{\prime}=H_{2}
), and
\beta_{D}(C^{\prime})\neq\beta_{D}(C)
or
\beta_{D}(C)=\beta_{D}(C^{\prime})
, but
C^{\prime}
does not meet twigs of type
T_{1,j}^{*}
.

(b)

There is no branching component of
D
meeting twigs of type
T_{1,j}^{*}
.

Either one yields a contradiction. Note that
(a)
holds e.g. when
R_{\mathrm{am}}
has at least two cusps, and we take
C
,
C^{\prime}
in their resolutions; or when the only twigs meeting
C
,
C^{\prime}
are
(-2)
-twigs, at least one reducible. In turn,

(b)
holds e.g. when
R_{\mathrm{am}}
has a non-ordinary cusp, and all twigs of
D
off its resolution are
(-2)
-twigs.

Let us follow this argument more closely in one of the more complicated cases, say
, see Figure
18(b)
. In all other cases we argue in a similar, straightforward manner. We use Notation
4.1
, and the same letters for the curves on
X^{\prime}
and their proper transforms on
X
. Choose
C=E_{p_{1}}
, so
T_{1,1}=[2]
,
T_{1,2}=[3]
. We can assume that one expansion is centered on
E_{p_{2}}
, for otherwise
C^{\prime}=E_{p_{2}}
does not meet a twig of type
[2]^{*}=[2]
, so
(a)
holds. If the other center is
(E_{p^{\prime}},L_{rp^{\prime}})
then
(b)
holds, and if it is
(C_{1},E_{p^{\prime}})
then
C^{\prime}
meets a twig of type
[3]
or
[2,2,2,2]
, and
(a)
holds. Hence the other expansion is centered at
(C_{1},L_{rp^{\prime}})
. We cannot take it with weight
1
, so let us denote this weight by
w
. Now
(a)
holds for
C^{\prime}=E_{p^{\prime}}
: indeed, if
w\not\in\mathbb{Z}
then
C^{\prime}
meets two branching components of
D
, and if
w\in\mathbb{Z}
then
C^{\prime}
meets twigs of type
[2]
and either
[3,w+1]
or
[2,2,2,2,w+1]
, neither of which is of type
[3]^{*}=[2,2]
.

∎

Tables

We now list the combinatorial data
({p},P,\mathcal{C},\boldsymbol{v})
needed to construct all
\mathbb{Q}
-homology planes in Theorem
1.6
. For planar divisors
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
and points in
P\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}{p}
, we use notation introduced in Section
8
. In particular, in each case:
{c}_{1}
is a conic,
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{p^{\prime}}
are lines tangent to
{c}_{1}
at points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p^{\prime}
;
{l}_{i}^{\prime}
are lines joining
p_{i}
with
p^{\prime}
; and for given points
a,b
, we denote by
{l}_{ab}
the line joining them. Notation for the remaining components and points of
{p}
is introduced separately for each particular configuration from Section
8
, specified in the column
“config.”
.

The towers are arranged according to the number
n
of centers of expansions used in the tom Dieck–Petrie algorithm, see Definition
1.5
. Towers with no
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibrations are listed in Tables
–
: in this case, results of Section
6
show that
n\leqslant 3
. The
\mathbb{C}^{**}
-fibered case, studied in Section
7
is summarized in Table
, here
n\leqslant 4
.

In case
n>0
, possible centers and weights
\boldsymbol{v}=(v,w,\dots)
are listed in the second-to-last column: if the indicated components meet at two points, we chose one of them. The restrictions on weights guarantee that the resulting surface is a
\mathbb{Q}
HP, see Lemma
3.3
(c)
.

The column
“
{N}
”
shows the number of non-isomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
HPs obtained from the same combinatorial data (including fixed weights of the expansions), hence sharing the same weighted graph of
D
, see Corollary
1.7
(c)
.

The column
“
{k}
”
shows a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}
over which the corresponding
\mathbb{Q}
HPs are defined. We denote by
\omega\in\mathbb{C}
a primitive third root of unity. Recall that by Proposition
9.13
(c)
, the
{N}
-tuples of
\mathbb{Q}
HPs obtained using the same combinatorial data are conjugate by the Galois group of
{k}
. In particular, if
{k}\not\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we get pairs of complex-conjugate, hence diffeomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
HPs, see Proposition
9.13
(d)
–
(f)
.

In the column
“
\mathbb{Z}
”
we put a
“
\checkmark
”
when the corresponding tower contains infinitely many
\mathbb{Z}
HPs, and a
“
\times
”
if it contains none, see Remark
5.1
. For towers
,
,
,
3
,
,
,
4
the corresponding symbols are listed in Tables
5
–
11
. If a cell is entirely crossed out (with a large
“
”
) then the corresponding set of centers does not occur.

Table 12.
Complements of planar rational cuspidal curves in Theorem
1.6
.
We use the notation from
[
PP17
,
PP19
]
. In particular,
\gamma,p,s,k
are some integer parameters and
(F_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
is the Fibonacci sequence, that is
F_{0}=0
,
F_{1}=1
,
F_{j+2}=F_{j+1}+F_{j}
for
j\geqslant 0
.
